Real-Variable Theory of Matrix-Weighted Multi-Parameter Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-Type Spaces Thanks: *) Corresponding author Thanks: This project is partially supported by the National Natural Science Foundation of China (Grant Nos. 12431006 and 12371093), the Beijing Natural Science Foundation (Grant No. 1262011), the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities (Grant No. 2253200028), the Research Council of Finland (Grant Nos. 346314, 364208, and 371637), and the China Postdoctoral Science Foundation (No. 2025M773047).
Fan Bu, Yiqun Chen, Tuomas P. Hytönen (*, Dachun Yang, Wen Yuan

Abstract
. We develop a comprehensive theory for a general class of multi-parameter function spaces of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin type, equipped with a matrix-valued weight function. We prove the equivalence of different quasi-norms, the identification of function and sequence spaces via the φ \varphi -transform of Frazier and Jawerth [J. Funct. Anal. 1990], the boundedness of almost diagonal operators and multi-parameter singular integrals under minimal assumptions, molecular and wavelet characterisations, and Sobolev-type embedding theorems. We identify matrix-weighted L p L^{p} spaces, Sobolev spaces of mixed smoothness, and multi-parameter BMO spaces of Chang–Fefferman type as examples of our general scale of spaces. Thus, our result on the boundedness of multi-parameter singular integrals on these spaces is seen as a far-reaching extension, with a different method, of a recent theorem of Domelevo et al. [J. Math. Anal. Appl. 2024] on matrix-weighted L p L^{p} spaces. For the needs of this theory, we develop several tools of independent interest in the broader contexts of matrix weights and multi-parameter harmonic analysis. In several cases, previous results were restricted to integrability exponents p ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p\in(1,\infty) , while the theory of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces naturally involves the full range p ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) p\in(0,\infty) . The inherent non-convexity in the range p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) p\in(0,1) requires new methods. We extend the definition of multi-parameter A p A_{p} matrix weights to p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] p\in(0,1] and establish their basic properties, culminating in the L p L^{p} -boundedness of a matrix-weighted strong maximal operator of Christ–Goldberg type (suitably rescaled when p ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] p\in(0,1] ) for all p ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) p\in(0,\infty) . For p ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p\in(1,\infty) , this is a recent result of Vuorinen [Adv. Math. 2024] based on convex-set-valued techniques of Bownik and Cruz-Uribe [arXiv 2022 or Math. Ann. (to appear)]; the lack of convexity requires us to develop a new approach that works for all p ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) p\in(0,\infty) . We also need and prove a multi-parameter extension of Carleson embedding-type inequalities from Frazier and Roudenko [Math. Ann. 2021] but attributed by them to F. Nazarov. As is typical for the multi-parameter theory, the condition for this embedding involves a supremum over all open sets. We show that this is necessary, but this requires a curious nontrivial elaboration of Carleson’s classical counterexample [Mittag-Leffler Rep. 1974], which we present in an appendix.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 42B25, 46E35;
Secondary 42B20, 42C40, 47A56, 35S05, 42B35

1.
Introduction

The topic of this work lies at the intersection of several broad themes of classical and modern harmonic analysis on Euclidean spaces:

(i)

Muckenhoupt’s theory of
A_{p}
weights and its
matrix-valued extension;

(ii)

theory of function spaces based on Littlewood–Paley theory;

(iii)

multi-parameter harmonic analysis.

Each of these topics has a long and distinguished history in its own right, and we will concentrate, in this introduction, on works that deal with at least two of the said themes simultaneously.

Theory of function spaces with matrix weights: (
i
) and (
ii
)

A more detailed account of the history of these two topics and their interaction has been given in the introduction of
[
12
]
, and we shall hence be relatively brief here.
The correct matrix-valued analogue of Muckenhoupt’s
A_{p}
class was identified by Nazarov, Treil, and Volberg
[
122
,
145
,
150
]
, and related matrix-weighted maximal function estimates were found by Christ and Goldberg
[
29
,
73
]
. The matrix
A_{p}
condition acquired its equivalent modern formulation in the work of Roudenko
[
133
]
, and was extended from
p\in(1,\infty)
to
p\in(0,1]
by Frazier and Roudenko
[
65
]
.

The theory of
L^{p}
spaces with matrix-weights is by now quite advanced, including diverse aspects like
weighted extrapolation
[
9
,
38
]
,
sparse domination
[
39
,
52
,
104
,
121
]
,
two-weight
[
35
]

and sharp one-weight inequalities
[
55
,
89
,
106
]
,
variational
[
57
]
and
multilinear estimates
[
99
]
,
compactness criteria
[
114
]
,
transference
[
123
]
,
and applications to partial differential equations
[
37
,
91
,
93
]
.
While
p\in(1,\infty)
in most cases, there are some results also for
0<p\leq 1

[
127
]
.

As for other functions spaces, the foundations for Besov spaces with matrix weights were already laid by Frazier and Roudenko
[
65
,
66
,
133
,
134
]
, with more recent contributions including
[
16
]
.
Restricted aspects of the theory of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces already appeared in the early works of Nazarov, Treil, and Volberg
[
122
,
150
]
and were revisited by Isralowitz
[
90
]
from the point-of-view of characterising the matrix-weighted
L^{p}
, but a systematic investigation of these spaces was only recently started by Frazier and Roudenko
[
67
]
. Since then, the theory of matrix-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces has been extended by many further works, including
[
4
,
5
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
18
,
19
,
109
,
119
,
152
,
161
]
.
Matrix-weighted BMO
[
92
]
,
Hardy
[
11
]
,
Campanato
[
28
]
,
Bourgain–Morrey
[
3
,
6
]
,
and modulation spaces
[
126
,
153
]

have also been considered.
The recent framework of
directional Banach function spaces

[
129
]
provides an abstraction of matrix-weighted
L^{p}
spaces including their variable-exponent versions.

Multi-parameter theory with weights: (
i
) and (
iii
)

For scalar weights, a starting point of their interaction with the multi-parameter theory would seem to be the work of R. Fefferman and Stein
[
62
]
, where the product
A_{p}
weights were defined
[
62
, bottom of page 127]
and the boundedness of some multi-parameter singular integrals of convolution type on the corresponding weighted
L^{p}
spaces established. This was then extended to general multi-parameter Calderón–Zygmund (or Journé) operators by R. Fefferman
[
60
,
61
]
, with estimates in the smaller weight class
A_{p/2}

[
60
]
as a stepping stone for the full result
[
61
]
.

A more direct approach to this boundedness, via a representation of Journé operators due to Martikainen
[
118
]
, was only recently found by Holmes, Petermichl, and Wick
[
79
]
, paving the way for its matrix-weighted extension by Domelevo et al.
[
54
]
, which laid the foundations for a systematic development of the multi-parameter theory of matrix weights.
(Some special cases of this theory has been treated earlier by Nielsen and Rasmussen
[
124
,
128
]
. In particular,
[
128
]
dealt with convolution type operators à la
[
62
]
under more restrictive assumptions on the matrix weights and conjectured the extension of their result to all matrix
A_{p}
weights.)

The boundedness of the matrix-weighted maximal operator of Christ and Goldberg
[
29
,
73
]
and the matrix-weighted Rubio de Francia extrapolation theorem of Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
9
]
have been extended to the multi-parameter setting by Vuorinen
[
151
]
, using the properties of a set-valued maximal operator established in
[
9
]
. A technical issue that arises in the multi-parameter theory of matrix weights is the measurable dependence on the remaining variable(s) of so-called reducing operators taken with respect to the other variable(s); this is studied in detail in
[
9
,
54
]
.
Different versions of the matrix-weighted multi-parameter BMO space have been studied by Kakaroumpas and Soler i Gibert
[
100
,
101
]
.

Multiparameter theory of function spaces: (
ii
) and (
iii
)

Here, we need to make a distinction between pure product-space theory and more complicated, “entangled” multi-parameter settings.

(a)

A theory of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces on product
domains was developed, and the boundedness of multi-parameter
singular integrals of convolution type on these spaces established
in
[
51
,
116
]
in the unweighted and scalar
A_{\infty}
-weighted
settings, respectively. In
[
27
,
156
]
, the boundedness of Fourier
multipliers (scalar
A_{\infty}
-weighted) and pseudo-differential operators
(unweighted) were obtained, respectively. The dual spaces of these spaces
(scalar
A_{\infty}
-weighted) have been identified in
[
47
,
50
]

for complementary ranges of the integrability parameter
p
.
The
\varphi
-transform characterization (unweighted)
in terms of related sequence spaces, the boundedness of
almost diagonal operators on the latter, and applications
to nonlinear wavelet approximation were obtained in
[
71
]
.

(b)

Beyond pure product theory, Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces adapted to different multi-parameter structures have been studied in the literature: a flag setting in
[
51
,
111
,
157
]
, a Zygmund dilation setting in
[
110
]
, a shearlet setting in
[
149
]
, and a mixture of Euclidean and parabolic homogeneities in
[
46
,
48
,
49
,
140
,
141
]
.

(c)

It is a well-known point-of-view to
interpret functions of variables
(x,y)
on a product domain
as vector-valued functions of, say,
x
only, taking values
in a space of functions of the other variable
y
.
In particular, this leads to the notion of
“Besov or Triebel–Lizorkin space-valued Besov
or Triebel–Lizorkin spaces”,
which have been studied for a long time

[
8
,
44
,
45
,
113
,
154
]
.
Such spaces arise as boundary traces of Sobolev spaces with
mixed integrability in space and time variables,
which are relevant for mixed order systems of partial differential equations.
A recurring theme in these studies is identifying (intersections of)
such vector-valued spaces with mixed-order one-parameter spaces,
where integrability is described by a vector of exponents
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
.

(d)

A theory of mixed-norm Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces has been developed in
[
31
,
32
,
33
,
70
,
72
,
125
]
.
Building upon these developments, Cleanthous and Georgiadis
[
30
]
introduced mixed-norm
\alpha
-modulation spaces, which include mixed-norm Besov spaces as a special case.
More recently, Nielsen
[
126
]
introduced the matrix-weighted
\alpha
-modulation spaces.

1.1.
Scope of this work

Against the background just described, we set as the goal of this work the development of a matrix-weighted multi-parameter theory of function spaces addressing all themes (
i
), (
ii
), and (
iii
)
at the same time. Given the diversity of different types of multi-parameter
theories as discussed above, attempting a comprehensive treatment in this
respect would be too much, and we concentrate here on the pure product theory on

\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}

(1.1)

with invariance with respect to the full family of independent dilations
(x_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{k}\mapsto(\delta_{\nu}x_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{k}
in each variable.
Thus, the entangled dilation systems, like the flag, Zygmund, shearlet,
and mixed homogeneities, will be left outside the scope of this work.
On the other hand, while keeping the primary focus on the “pure” Besov
and the “pure” Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, we set up a framework that also includes
spaces of “mixed” type, as in the vector-valued point-of-view mentioned above.

To be a little more precise, recall that the classical Triebel–Lizorkin and Besov quasi-norms are essentially based on iterated
L^{p}\ell^{q}
and
\ell^{q}L^{p}
quasi-norms, respectively;
and these are the only permutations of the position space
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and the scale space
\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z})
in the one-parameter situation.
However, on the product domain (
1.1
), we may in principle
have a different position space
L^{p_{\nu}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})
and scale
space
\ell^{q_{\nu}}(\mathbb{Z})
for each component variable
x_{\nu}
.
Then the
2k
-sequence of spaces
L^{p_{1}},\ldots,L^{p_{k}},\ell^{q_{1}},\ldots,\ell^{q_{k}}
has
(2k)!
different permutations. Among them, pure Besov
(or Besov-type) spaces
correspond to the
(k!)^{2}
sequences with all
L^{p_{\mu}}
on the right-hand side of
all
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
(all these permutations
are called
Besov-type permutations
), and pure Triebel–Lizorkin
(or Triebel–Lizorkin-type) spaces to the
(k!)^{2}

sequences with all
L^{p_{\mu}}
on the left of all
\ell^{q_{\nu}}

(all these permutations are called
Triebel–Lizorkin-type permutations
).
It is evident that these
2(k!)^{2}
pure spaces are but a fraction of all
(2k)!

possible mixed spaces when
k>1
. Already in the bi-parameter case,
2(2!)^{2}=8

and
(2\cdot 2)!=24
.

The reader will agree that in order to handle such a variety of spaces, a reasonably efficient notation will be necessary. As already hinted by the prose above, a key characteristic of a function space that we consider will be the associated permutation
\pi
of
[2k]:=\{1,\ldots,2k\}
.
With
X_{\nu}:=L^{p_{\nu}}
and
X_{\nu+k}:=\ell^{q_{\nu}}
for
\nu\in[k]
, we denote

(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{2k}X_{\pi(\nu)}:=X_{\pi(1)}\Bigg(\prod_{\nu=2}^{2k}X_{\pi(\nu)}\Bigg),

(1.2)

where the ordered “product” of
L^{p_{\mu}}
and
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
spaces is defined recursively by

Y(Z):=\Big\{f=f(y,z)\text{ measurable}:\big\|y\mapsto\|z\mapsto f(y,z)\|_{Z}\big\|_{Y}<\infty\Big\}.

A reader who feels, at this point, that this level of generality is an unnecessary complication, should consider the following: A large part of the existing theory of function spaces is written with separate statements and proofs for the Besov case and the Triebel–Lizorkin case, and so it takes roughly double the space to treat both situations. In our notation, these both pure types of spaces are conveniently covered at once, and the fact that we simultaneously cover a spectrum of other spaces is a nice bonus that we obtain almost for free.

Certainly, when it comes to the fine details of proving some of the more delicate results, we will occasionally need to pursue a case study, but even then we claim that our treatment, overall, will be more efficient than the traditional division into Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin cases, even if we only discussed these two.

Admissible permutations

Recall that, already in the classical theory, while Besov spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{p,q}
have a unified definition for all
p,q\in(0,\infty]
, Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{F}^{s}_{p,q}
are defined differently for finite and infinite
p
, essentially reflecting the fact that the “right” substitute for
L^{\infty}
is the space
\operatorname{BMO}
of functions of bounded mean oscillation, whose definition is qualitatively different from the
L^{p}
spaces. Thus, the basic one-parameter theory (excluding the separate treatment of
\dot{F}^{s}_{\infty,q}
) allows all other
L^{p}\ell^{q}
and
\ell^{q}L^{p}
except
L^{\infty}\ell^{q}
with
q\in(0,\infty)
.

This exclusion admits a rather natural generalisation in our setting of
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
spaces. We say that a permutation
\pi
is
admissible
for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
if the following holds: after the leftmost
L^{p_{\mu}}
space in the “product” (
1.2
), if this space itself or any space on its right-hand side (either
L^{p_{\nu}}
or
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
) has exponent
\infty
, then all other spaces further to the right must also have exponent
\infty
. One easily verifies that
L^{\infty}\ell^{q}
with
q\in(0,\infty)
is the unique non-admissible case in the one-parameter theory.
A strong maximal operator is bounded on
(L^{\vec{p}/a}\ell^{\vec{q}/a})_{\pi}

for some
a\in(0,\infty)
if and only if the permutation
\pi
is admissible.
Admissibility will be a standing assumption for our theory. This still allows
all combinations of finite exponents as well as some cases of infinite exponents,
and it is completely in line with the classical theory, as just discussed.

Depending on the particular results that we are proving, we will sometimes need
to impose other conditions on
(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})
besides admissibility. Nevertheless, in all main results, we always cover at least the following cases (we alway allow a general
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
):

(i)

matrix-weighted spaces of pure Besov-type or pure Triebel–Lizorkin-type, where
\vec{p}=p\cdot\vec{1}
,

(ii)

unweighted spaces of any admissible type and any mixed exponent
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
.

While our principal focus is in the matrix-weighted theory, some of the results seem to be new even in the unweighted case, it is therefore interesting to note that the range of indices can be completely general in this case, as stated in (
ii
).

The Morrey parameter
\tau

As we already mentioned, the classical theory requires a separate treatment of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
\dot{F}^{s}_{\infty,q}
, with
q<\infty
, or in other words a replacement of the inadmissible
L^{\infty}\ell^{q}
, and this is necessary to cover the important space
\operatorname{BMO}=\dot{F}^{0}_{\infty,2}
as part of the theory. However, instead of addressing this special case separately, a more recent approach is to equip our space with a fourth “Morrey” index
\tau\in[0,\infty)
, arriving at so-called Besov
-type
and Triebel–Lizorkin
-type
spaces
\dot{B}^{s,\tau}_{p,q}
and
\dot{F}^{s,\tau}_{p,q}
, where three-index spaces (other than
\dot{F}^{s}_{\infty,q}
) correspond to
\tau=0
. Introduced in
[
158
,
159
,
162
]
, these spaces cover not only the classical
\dot{F}^{s}_{\infty,q}=\dot{F}^{1/p}_{p,q}
(for any
p\in(0,\infty)
, by a version of the John–Nirenberg inequality), but also new examples outside the classical Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin scales, like so-called
Q
-spaces. The recent articles
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
already incorporate this Morrey index into the matrix-weighted one-parameter
theory, and we adopt this framework also here.

In the one-parameter theory, the Morrey index brings a new supremum over (dyadic) cubes as part of the related quasi-norms, in natural agreement with the classical
definition of the norm in
\operatorname{BMO}
. However, it is also well known that the norm of the most natural

[
25
,
26
]
(although not the only relevant; see
[
79
, p. 1708]
or
[
53
, Definitions 1.3–1.4]
) version of the multi-parameter
\operatorname{BMO}
space involves a supremum not just over rectangles (as the multi-parameter extension of some other one-parameter concepts based on cubes) but over general open sets.
The
\operatorname{BMO}
space defined in this way is the dual of the Hardy space.
Moreover, since the class of rectangles is not nested,
R. Fefferman
[
59
]
, building on Carleson’s construction
[
23
]
,
showed that in the bi-parameter setting the dual of the Hardy space
does not coincide with the so-called rectangular BMO space.

Viewing the Chang–Fefferman BMO as the primary example that a reasonable multi-parameter theory of function spaces should cover, we define our multi-parameter spaces using a similar supremum over open sets as a replacement of the cubes in the one-parameter theory.

The weight classes and other parameters

Recall that a key part of our goals is developing a matrix-weighted theory
of the spaces that we study. While it is known that several related results can be obtained for the largest Muckenhoupt class of
A_{\infty}
weights, both in the scalar-weighted multi-parameter theory
[
27
,
47
,
50
,
116
]
and in the matrix-weighted one-parameter theory
[
15
,
150
]
(some results on Besov spaces
[
133
]
being also available under
different assumptions from ones of Muckenhoupt type), we here restrict our
considerations to the more well-studied matrix
A_{p}
class. This is in line with the majority of recent literature on matrix-weighted spaces. Since results on
A_{p}
weights tend to be tied with estimates in
L^{u}
with
u
equal (or at least close, as in
[
73
, Theorem 5.1]
) to
p
, we are also led to assume isotropic integrability
\vec{p}=p\cdot\vec{1}
for the majority of our results.
In the unweighted setting, this restriction can be removed.
Even after making this restriction, we find it convenient to use the notation
\vec{p}
and
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
to emphasise the fact that we have
k
independent
L^{p}
quasi-norms that may assume any positions before, after, and between the
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
quasi-norms in (
1.2
). On the other hand, we rarely need to restrict the nature of the vector
\vec{q}
(other than the positions of possible infinities for admissibility), which is an increased generality compared to most of the
related multi-parameter articles quoted. Allowing vectorial smoothness
\vec{s}
seems to be standard in the existing multi-parameter theory, and we follow this practice without any trouble.

1.2.
An overview of content and methods

The first part of the paper,

consisting of Sections
2
through
5
,
is devoted to a general multi-parameter theory of matrix weights, maximal functions, and other inequalities; it is needed in the subsequent analysis of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces, but may have independent interest.

In Section
2
, we set up a theory of multi-parameter matrix
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights for the full range of exponents
p\in(0,\infty)
, which is relevant in the theory of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces. For
p\in(1,\infty)
, such a multi-parameter theory was already developed in detail by Domelevo et al.
[
54
]
, but the case of
p\in(0,1]
seems to be new in the multi-parameter setting, extending the one-parameter theory of Frazier and Roudenko
[
65
]
. The exponent
p=1
presents a genuine turning point in the nature of some results, like the useful characterisation of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition in terms of the boundedness of averaging operators.

Section
3
addresses an issue of measurable selection of reducing operators that arises in the multi-parameter theory. Namely, it is a standard consequence of the John ellipsoid theorem that any norm
e\mapsto r(e)
on
\mathbb{F}^{m}\in\{\mathbb{R}^{m},\mathbb{C}^{m}\}
is equivalent to
e\mapsto|Ae|
for some matrix
A
known as the reducing operator. If
r(x,\cdot)
is a family of such norms, measurable with respect to
x\in\Omega
, then one also wants to choose the reducing operators
A(x)
in a measurable way. For proper norms, this is known from the works of Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
9
]
and Domelevo et al.
[
54
]
. However, to deal with the full range of exponents
p\in(0,\infty)
again, we need an extension to
p
-seminorms, which requires modifications of the arguments in the lack of convexity, which plays a role in the existing results of
[
9
,
54
]
.

In Section
4
, we first identify the precise conditions for a vector-valued strong maximal inequality in the general iterated
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
spaces, leading to the notion of admissible permutations
\pi
already mentioned. More substantially, we obtain a multi-parameter extension of certain Carleson embedding-type inequalities of the form

\big\|\{\gamma_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}f_{j}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\leq C\big\|\{f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}

(1.3)

under suitable conditions on the multipliers
\gamma_{j}
,
when each function
f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
is piecewise constant on dyadic rectangles
R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
.
Such estimates were introduced by Frazier and Roudenko
[
67
]
(and attributed by them to F. Nazarov) and played an instrumental role in the one-paramater theory of matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in
[
67
]
and their extensions in
[
12
,
15
]
. Our multi-parameter extension presents several nontrivial novelties of independent interest:

(i)

The multi-parameter Carleson condition has the usual complication of involving arbitrary open sets instead of cubes as in
[
67
]
. The proof of its sufficiency for the embedding that we need depends on a new interpolation argument with respect to
p
instead of
q
as in
[
67
]
.

(ii)

We also prove the necessity of this condition. As expected, this necessity is related to Carleson’s famous counterexample
[
23
]
but, curiously, seems to require a nontrivial elaboration of this example, which we provide in Appendix
A
. This counterexample as such is independent of matrix weights, and may have other relevance for multi-parameter harmonic analysis.

(iii)

Finally, we need to check that multipliers arising from matrix weights satisfy the relevant Carleson condition. We prove that this is equivalent to a matrix-weighted strong maximal inequality. This latter is recently due to Vuorinen
[
151
]
for
p\in(1,\infty)
, but it is a new result requiring a new formulation and a new approach for
p\in(0,1]
. We present a unified method that works for all
p\in(0,\infty)
, thus also providing a new proof for
p\in(1,\infty)
. The boundedness of the strong maximal operator is a fundamental result in real analysis, and likewise this matrix-weighted extension should have broader relevance for matrix-weighted harmonic analysis beyond the theory of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin spaces.

The short Section
5
is independent of the previous ones and collects some basic lemmas of multi-parameter Fourier analysis, like Calderón reproducing formulas and decay estimates for pairings of test functions, that will be repeatedly used in the remainder of the paper.

The second part of the paper,

consisting of the remaining Sections
6
through
11
, undertakes a thorough study of the matrix-weighted multi-parameter Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces, making essential use of the tools developed in the first part.

In Section
6
, we introduce the main object of the paper, the matrix-weighted multi-parameter Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, and establish their basic properties:

(i)

Theorem
6.4
on the equivalence of different quasi-norms (in particular, ones where the weight function
V(x)
acts pointwise at each
x
, and “averaging” versions, where the weight acts via its associated sequence of reducing operators on each dyadic rectangle), and

(ii)

the
\varphi
-transform characterisation (Theorem
6.16
), which provides a correspondence between the function spaces
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
and their analogous discrete sequence spaces
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
.

With the new multi-parameter tools from Section
4
in place of their one-parameter counterparts, we could in principle follow the one-parameter theory of
[
12
]
step-by-step to achieve this extension; however, we take the opportunity to streamline the presentation, not only by covering Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces simultaneously within our framework of permuted mixed-norm spaces
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
, but also by treating function and sequence spaces in a more unified way. In fact, our Section
6
on pp.
6
–
7
, while essentially self-contained, is much shorter than the presentation of the corresponding one-parameter theory in
[
12
, Section 3, pp. 6140–6167]
.

Section
7
studies the boundedness of almost diagonal infinite matrices on the sequence spaces
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
; thanks to the
\varphi
-transform characterisation, this is immediately relevant also for the analysis of operators on the function spaces
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
. The main result of this section is Theorem
7.12
. For the usual Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with
\tau=0
, it provides a complete multi-parameter extension of the best available one-parameter results from
[
13
]
. For general Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces, our Theorem
7.12
extends the one-parameter results of
[
13
]
in the worst-case scenario among arbitrary
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights. While the one-parameter theory allows for a sharper statement by taking into account additional information about the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of the weight, which we are not yet able to fully incorporate into the multi-parameter theory, we show that our results are essentially optimal, in many cases, among uniform estimates for all
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights.

In Section
8
, we characterise the space
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
in terms of molecular decompositions (Theorem
8.9
), and provide their first applications to the boundedness of pseudo-differential operators on
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
and wavelet expansions of these spaces. With the multi-parameter tools of the previous sections at hand, these are obtained as natural extensions of their one-parameter analogues from
[
14
]
, but additional care is needed to address the new types of mixed cancellation that arise when pairing two multi-parameter molecules: in the one-parameter case, one typically uses the smoothness of one function and vanishing moments of the other one; in the multi-parameter case, we need to couple the smoothness of
f
with the vanishing moments of
g
with respect to some variables, and the vanishing moments of
f
with the smoothness of
g
in the remaining ones.

In Section
9
, we compare our general scale of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
spaces with other matrix-weighted function spaces. In particular, we show that matrix-weighted
L^{p}(V)
spaces (Theorem
9.1
), Sobolev spaces (Theorem
9.4
), and Chang–Fefferman-type multi-parameter BMO spaces (Theorem
9.10
) all fit into the general framework of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
spaces. To cover the last important example, the more complicated definition in terms of general open sets is essential. We also compare this definition with a simpler version involving dyadic cubes or rectangles only, both in the one-parameter and in the multi-parameter situations.

In Section
10
, we prove a multi-parameter
T(1)
theorem (Theorem
10.30
), a general criterion for the boundedness of multi-parameter singular integral operators, on the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
spaces. Thanks to the identification of other spaces as special cases in Section
9
, this recovers (Corollary
10.31
) the paraproduct-free case of the multi-parameter
T(1)
theorem on
L^{p}(V)
spaces, which was only recently obtained by Domelevo et al.
[
54
]
by different methods, but we simultaneously cover cases like Sobolev spaces, which are outside the scope of
[
54
]
. As in the first multi-parameter
T(1)
theorem on
L^{2}
by Journé
[
98
]
, our approach proceeds by induction on the number of parameters. To make this induction run smoothly, we also revisit and streamline the one-parameter results of
[
14
]
. In particular, we formulate a condition where the usual off-diagonal kernel bounds and the weak boundedness property are essentially merged. The proof also crucially depends on the molecular characterisation from Section
8
.

The final Section
11
presents a Sobolev-type embedding (Theorem
11.2
) for the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
spaces, with applications to the matrix-weighted
L^{p}(V)
and Sobolev spaces. These results are not difficult, but we note the failure of a naïve multi-parameter extension of an interpolation inequality that has been used in some related one-parameter results; this necessitates another approach.

Finally, the symbol
C
denotes a positive constant
that is independent of the main parameters involved,
but may vary from line to line. The symbol
A\lesssim B

means that
A\leq CB
for some positive constant

C
, while
A\sim B
means
A\lesssim B\lesssim A
.
Unless explicitly stated otherwise,

C

2.
Matrix weights

In this section, we set up a theory of multi-parameter matrix
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
weights to serve the needs of the rest of this work. The foundations for such a theory were laid down by Domelevo et al.
[
54
]
, who explicitly discussed the case of bi-parameter matrix weights. While the extension from two to several parameters is relatively routine, a key novelty of our treatment is incorporating the full scale of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights with
p\in(0,\infty)
, where
[
54
]
dealt with
p\in(1,\infty)
only. We recall that one-parameter
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights with
p\in(0,1]
were already introduced by Frazier and Roudenko
[
65
]
, but it seems that their multi-parameter version is here studied for the first time. This is highly relevant for building a weighted theory of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin type space in the full range of the integrability parameter
p\in(0,\infty)
, which is desirable for a complete theory of these spaces.

Especially when it comes to
p\in(0,1)
, a new difficulty is the following: When
p\in[1,\infty)
, the seemingly non-linear
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition is conveniently characterised in terms of the
L^{p}(V)
boundedness of the linear averaging operators
f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\fint_{Q}f
when
p\in[1,\infty)
(see
[
54
, Lemma 3.1]
for
p\in(1,\infty)
or Lemma
2.7
below for
p\in[1,\infty))
, and this linearity is key to a useful characterisation of the multi-parameter
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition by means of iterating the one-parameter
\mathscr{A}_{p}
conditions in each coordinate (see
[
54
, Lemmas 3.6 and 3.7]
, or Lemma
2.11
below).

For
p<1
, these averages are not even well defined on
L^{p}(V)
, and we need to look for a substitute. The surprisingly simple solution turns out to be to replace the averages
f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\fint_{Q}f
by the
extension operators

f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\otimes f
, where the right-hand side is the function of two variables
(\mathbf{1}_{Q}\otimes f)(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{Q}(x)f(y)
, and the characterising condition is the boundedness of these operators from
L^{p}(V)
to
L^{p}(V\otimes\mathbf{1})
. Thus, we are led to consider “decoupling” estimates of the form

\fint_{Q}\fint_{Q}|V(x)f(y)|^{p}dx\,dy\lesssim\fint_{Q}|V(x)f(x)|^{p}dx,

comparing the action of the weight
V
on the function
f
with independent and equal variables, respectively; see Lemma
2.11
. Reformulated in terms of the boundedness of suitable linear operators as in the case
p\in(1,\infty)
, this allows us to extend the iterative characterisation of multi-parameter matrix
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights to the full range
p\in(0,\infty)
. Conveniently, the same approach also gives an iterative characterisation of the classical reverse Hölder inequality (Lemma
2.26
) and a multi-parameter extension of the recent notion of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of matrix weights from
[
12
]
and its key application to the comparison of reducing operators over different rectangles (Lemma
2.20
).

The fact that these multi-parameter extensions can be derived as relatively quick corollaries of their one-parameter counterparts, without the need to rebuild the entire theory from scratch, illustrates the power of the iterative description of multi-parameter weights and, at the bottom, the characterisation of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition in terms of the boundedness of suitable linear operators as sketched above. The fact that this characterisation splits into two different forms at the index
p=1
has a curious consequence: a certain interpolation property of two weights
V_{p_{i}}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{i}}
holds whenever both
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,1]
or both
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
, and
only
in these cases (see Proposition
2.32
). In particular, we demonstrate by example that the interpolation property fails for
p_{0},p_{1}
on different sides of
1
, showing that the splitting of the theory into two ranges, which require slightly different considerations, is not an artefact of our choices in setting up the framework, but a necessity of the mathematical reality.

The structure of this section is organized as follows.
In Subsection
2.1
, we study
the reducing operator of multi-parameter matrix weights.
In Subsection
2.2
, we introduce
multi-parameter matrix
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
weights,
and Subsection
2.3
concerns their associated
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
-dimensions.
In Subsection
2.4
, we study the reverse Hölder inequality of matrix weights.
Finally, in Subsection
2.5
, we establish an interpolation theorem for matrix weights.

2.1.
Reducing operators

Recall that we denote by
\mathbb{F}
a generic scalar field, which may be
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
.
The basic idea of
reducing operators
is that a general norm (or even a semi-quasi-norm) on
\mathbb{F}^{m}
can be “reduced” to a norm of the form
x\mapsto|Ax|
for some matrix
A\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
, up to inessential multiplicative constants, and this is useful in reducing considerations with various norms to matrix manipulations.

To set the stage for the variety of norm-like expressions that we will encounter, we begin with:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X
be a vector space over the scalar field
\mathbb{F}
.

Let
r:X\to[0,\infty)
satisfy
r(\lambda u)=|\lambda|r(u)
for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
and
u\in X
.

For
0<p\leq 1\leq K<\infty
, we consider the following inequalities:

r(u+v)\leq\begin{cases}r(u)+r(v),&\text{triangle inequality},\\
[r(u)^{p}+r(v)^{p}]^{\frac{1}{p}},&p\text{-triangle inequality},\\
K[r(u)+r(v)],&\text{quasi-triangle inequality (with constant $K$)}.\end{cases}

(2.1)

If one of these holds for all
u,v\in X
, we say that
r
is
a
seminorm
, a
p
-seminorm
, or a
quasi-seminorm

(with constant
K
), respectively.
If, in addition,
r(u)=0
only if
u=0
, then we drop the word “semi” in each of these notions.

If
r:X\to[0,\infty]
, then we add the word “extended” in front of each of these notions.

A matrix
A\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
is said to be
positive definite
if
(Ax,x)>0

for every
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
, and

positive semidefinite
if
(Ax,x)\geq 0
for every
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
.
For every
x:=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
, we denote

|x|:=(\sum_{i=1}^{m}|x_{i}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proposition 2.2
.

If
\|\ \|
is a quasi-norm (resp. quasi-seminorm) on
\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
then there exists a positive definite (resp. semidefinite) matrix

A\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

\|x\|\sim|Ax|,

where the positive equivalence constants depend only on
m
and the quasi-triangle constant of
\|\ \|
.

Proof.

We first consider the case where
\|\ \|
is a quasi-norm.
By the Aoki–Rolewicz theorem, as stated e.g. in
[
117
, Theorem 1.2]
,
every quasi-norm
\|\ \|
is equivalent to a
p
-norm
\|\ \|^{*}
,
where both
p\in(0,1]
and the equivalence constants depend only on
the quasi-triangle constant of
\|\ \|
.
By an argument given in
[
65
, page 1237]
,
every
p
-norm
\|\ \|^{*}
on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
is equivalent to
a norm of the form
x\mapsto|Ax|
,
where the positive equivalence constants depend only on
p
and
m
.
A combination of these equivalences proves the present proposition in this case.

If
\|\ \|
is only a quasi-seminorm,
then
\mathcal{N}:=\{x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}:\ \|x\|=0\}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}^{m}

and
\|\ \|
is a quasi-norm on its orthogonal complement
\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
.
The previous argument provides us with a positive definite
linear mapping
\widetilde{A}
on
\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
such that

\|x\|\sim|\widetilde{A}x|
for every
x\in\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
.
If
P
is the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{F}^{m}
onto
\mathcal{N}^{\perp}
,
then
A:=\widetilde{A}\circ P
provides the desired semidefinite matrix.
This finishes the proof of Proposition
2.2
.
∎

For every measurable set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,
let
\mathscr{M}(\Omega)
denote the set of all
measurable functions on
\Omega
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\Omega
be a measurable set of
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
.
We say that
X(\Omega)\subset\mathscr{M}(\Omega)
is a
quasi-Banach lattice
if

(i)

there is an extended quasi-norm
\|\ \|_{X(\Omega)}
on
\mathscr{M}(\Omega)
, whose restriction to
X(\Omega)
is a quasi-norm;

(ii)

whenever
f,g\in\mathscr{M}(\Omega)
satisfy
|g|\leq|f|
a.e. on
\Omega
, then
\|g\|_{X(\Omega)}\leq\|f\|_{X(\Omega)}
;

(iii)

in the situation of (
ii
), if
f\in X(\Omega)
, then
g\in X(\Omega)
;

(iv)

X(\Omega)
equipped with
\|\ \|_{X(\Omega)}
is a quasi-Banach space.

If
\|\ \|_{X(\Omega)}
satisfies (
2.1
) with
K=1
, then
X(\Omega)
is called a

Banach lattice
.

If
X(\Omega)
is a quasi-Banach lattice of
\mathbb{F}
-valued functions, we define

X(\Omega;\mathbb{F}^{l\times m}):=\Big\{F:\ \Omega\to\mathbb{F}^{l\times m}\text{ measurable}:\ \|F\|_{X(\Omega)}:=\big\||F(\cdot)|\big\|_{X(\Omega)}<\infty\Big\}.

For
F\in X(\Omega;\mathbb{F}^{l\times m})
and
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
we have
F(\cdot)x:\ \Omega\to\mathbb{F}^{l}=\mathbb{F}^{l\times 1}
, and we can set

\|x\|:=\big\||F(\cdot)x|\big\|_{X(\Omega)}.

This
\|\ \|
defines a quasi-seminorm on
\mathbb{F}^{m}

and depends on
F
.
Proposition
2.2
provides us with a positive semidefinite matrix
A\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
such that,
for every
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

\|x\|\sim|Ax|,

(2.2)

where the positive equivalence constants are as in Proposition
2.2
.
Although
A
is not unique, we denote one such choice by
[F]_{X(\Omega)}
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
Q
be a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
In the case where
X(\Omega)=\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)

and
F\in\textit{\L}^{p}(Q;\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}_{+})
, (
2.2
) reduces to:
for every
x\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

\displaystyle\bigg[\fint_{Q}|F(y)x|^{p}dy\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\sim|[F]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}x|,

(2.3)

where the positive equivalence constants depend only on
m
and
p
.
In this case,
[F]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}
is precisely the reducing operator
of order
p
for
F
introduced by Volberg
[
150
, p. 450]
.
In the sense expressed by (
2.3
), the reducing operator is a linearisation of the norm induced by
F
, which enables us to deal with the matrix-valued function
F
and the vector
x
separately.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
X(\Omega)
be a quasi-Banach lattice
(of scalar-valued functions).
Then, for every
F\in X(\Omega;\mathbb{F}^{l\times m})
and
M\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times k}
,

\|F(\cdot)M\|_{X(\Omega)}\sim|[F]_{X(\Omega)}M|,

where the positive equivalence constants depend only on
k
,
m
,
and the quasi-triangle constant of
\|\ \|_{X(\Omega)}
.

Proof.

For all functions
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
in
X
,
it follows from the properties of a
quasi-Banach lattice quasi-norm that, for each
j\in[k]
,

\|\phi_{j}\|_{X(\Omega)}=\big\||\phi_{j}|\big\|_{X(\Omega)}\leq\Bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\phi_{i}|\Bigg\|_{X(\Omega)}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\phi_{i}\|_{X(\Omega)}

and hence

\Bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\phi_{i}|\Bigg\|_{X(\Omega)}\sim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\phi_{i}\|_{X(\Omega)}.

If
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
are the standard unit vectors in
\mathbb{F}^{k}
, then

|M|\sim\sum_{i=1}^{k}|Me_{i}|.

Thus, using this bound with either
F(\cdot)M
or
[F]_{X(\Omega)}M
in place of
M
, and combining with the other bound above, we obtain

\displaystyle\|F(\cdot)M\|_{X(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\Bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{k}|F(\cdot)Me_{i}|\Bigg\|_{X(\Omega)}\sim\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big\|F(\cdot)Me_{i}\big\|_{X(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\sim\sum_{i=1}^{k}|[F]_{X(\Omega)}Me_{i}|\sim|[F]_{X(\Omega)}M|.

This finishes the proof of Lemma
2.4
.
∎

Before proving the following “Fubini theorem” for matrix functions, we first introduce some notation.
For every measurable

F:\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}\to\mathbb{F}^{k\times m},

let

\|(x,y)\mapsto F(x,y)\|_{X(dx;Y(dy))}:=\big\|\|y\mapsto F(\cdot,y)\|_{Y(dy)}\big\|_{X(\Omega_{1})},

where, for every
x\in\Omega_{1}
,

\|y\mapsto F(x,y)\|_{Y(dy)}:=\|F(x,\cdot)\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}.

Below, we will be concerned with
F(x,y)=A(x)B(y)
, where
A\in X(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{F}^{k\times l})
and
B\in Y(\Omega_{2};\mathbb{F}^{l\times m})
.

In what follows, for all quasi-normed linear spaces
\mathscr{X},\mathscr{Y}

and for every linear or sublinear operator

T:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
, let

\|T\|_{\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}}:=\sup_{\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}}=1}\|Tf\|_{\mathscr{Y}}.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
X(\Omega_{1})
and
Y(\Omega_{2})
be quasi-Banach lattices.
Then the following two assertions hold.

(i)

For every
A\in X(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{F}^{k\times l})
and
B\in Y(\Omega_{2};\mathbb{F}^{l\times m})
,

\displaystyle\|A,B\|_{X(\Omega_{1}),Y(\Omega_{2})}

\displaystyle:=\|(x,y)\mapsto A(x)B(y)\|_{X(dx;Y(dy))}\sim\|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}B\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}\sim\|A[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}\|_{X(\Omega_{1})}\sim\|B^{*},A^{*}\|_{Y(\Omega_{2}),X(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\phantom{:}=\|(x,y)\mapsto A(x)B(y)\|_{Y(dy;X(dx))}\sim|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}|,

where the positive equivalence constants are independent of
A
and
B
.

(ii)

Suppose further that

(Y(\Omega_{2}),Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime})
is a

dual pair
of Banach lattices in the following sense:

(a)

For all
f\in Y(\Omega_{2})
and
g\in Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}
, their pointwise product satisfies

\|fg\|_{L^{1}(\Omega_{2})}\leq\|f\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}\|g\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}}.

(2.4)

(b)

For all
f\in Y(\Omega_{2})
, its quasi-norm can be recovered by

\|f\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}=\sup\bigg\{\Big|\int_{\Omega_{2}}fg\Big|:\ g\in Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime},\ \|g\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}}\leq 1\bigg\}.

(2.5)

Then, for every
A\in X(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{F}^{k\times l})
and
B\in Y(\Omega_{2};\mathbb{F}^{l\times m})
,

\|A,B\|_{X(\Omega_{1}),Y(\Omega_{2})}\sim\Big\|f\mapsto A\int_{\Omega_{2}}Bf\Big\|_{Y(\Omega_{2};\mathbb{F}^{m\times 1})^{\prime}\to X(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{F}^{k\times 1})},

where the positive equivalence constants are independent of
A
and
B
.

Note that, if
Y^{*}
denotes the Banach space dual of
Y
,
our assumption that
(Y,Y^{\prime})
is a dual pair implies that
Y^{\prime}

can be identified with a subspace of
Y^{*}
, but
does not

imply that
Y^{\prime}
should consist of all of
Y^{*}
.
This is relevant to cover the case of
Y=L^{\infty}(Q)
and
Y^{\prime}=\textit{\L}^{1}(Q)
.
The following remark relates the notion of a dual pair to some other concepts used in the literature that go beyond our present needs.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
Y(\Omega)
be a quasi-Banach lattice.

(i)

Recall that the
associate space (Köthe dual)

Y(\Omega)_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}
is defined
by setting

\displaystyle Y(\Omega)_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}:=\Big\{f\in\mathscr{M}(\Omega):\ \|f\|_{Y(\Omega)_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}}:=\sup_{\|g\|_{Y(\Omega)}=1}\|fg\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}<\infty\Big\}.

Using this definition, we find that inequality (
2.4
) holds in particular with

Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}=Y(\Omega_{2})_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}
.

(ii)

Recall that
Y(\Omega)
is said to have the
Fatou property

if for every sequence of positive-valued functions

\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\subset Y(\Omega)
with
\liminf_{j\to\infty}\|f_{j}\|_{Y(\Omega)}<\infty
,
one has
\liminf_{j\to\infty}f_{j}\in Y(\Omega)
and

\Big\|\liminf_{j\to\infty}f_{j}\Big\|_{Y(\Omega)}\leq\liminf_{j\to\infty}\|f_{j}\|_{Y(\Omega)}.

If
Y(\Omega)
is a Banach lattice with the
saturation property

(see
[
115
, p. 249]
for its definition), then
Y(\Omega)

has the Fatou property if and only if
(Y(\Omega)_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime})_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}=Y(\Omega)

(see
[
115
, Theorem 3.6]
).
When
\Omega=\Omega_{2}
, the last identity implies that (
2.5
) holds with
Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}=Y(\Omega_{2})_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime}
.
Consequently, if
Y(\Omega_{2})
is a Banach lattice with
the Fatou and saturation properties, then
(Y(\Omega_{2}),Y(\Omega_{2})_{\textrm{KD}}^{\prime})
is a
dual pair of Banach lattices in the sense of Lemma
2.5
.
The point of the assumptions of Lemma
2.5
is simply to state the minimal
ad hoc
condition that we need; as we just argued, this
ad hoc
condition can be verified via usual conditions appearing elsewhere in the literature.

(iii)

We point out that the Fatou property of
Y(\Omega)
and the associate space
enable one to escape the difficulties caused by
the lack of an explicit expression of the quasi-norm of
Y(\Omega)
under consideration,
which have proved to play an important role in
[
24
,
78
,
80
,
135
]
.
In the same way, Lemma
2.5
plays a key role in the proofs of Lemmas

2.7
and
2.20
, which are the fundamental tools of this article.

Proof of Lemma
2.5
.

For all matrices of compatible size, we have

|AB|=|(AB)^{*}|=|B^{*}A^{*}|.

(2.6)

From this identity and Lemma
2.4
, for each point
x
, it follows that

\|A(x)B(\cdot)\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}=\|B(\cdot)^{*}A(x)^{*}\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}\sim|[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}A(x)^{*}|=|A(x)[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}^{*}|=|A(x)[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}|,

using the self-adjointness of
[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}
in the last step.
Taking quasi-norms in
X(\Omega_{1})
on both sides,

\|(x,y)\mapsto A(x)B(y)\|_{X(dx,Y(dy))}\sim\|A(\cdot)[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}\|_{X(\Omega_{1})}\sim|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}|.

By (
2.6
) pointwise, we have

\|B^{*},A^{*}\|_{Y(\Omega_{2}),X(\Omega_{1})}=\|(x,y)\mapsto B(y)^{*}A(x)^{*}\|_{Y(dy;X(dx))}=\|(x,y)\mapsto A(x)B(y)\|_{Y(dy;X(dx))}.

Here

\|A(\cdot)B(y)\|_{X(\Omega_{1})}\sim|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}B(y)|=|B(y)^{*}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}|.

Taking
Y(\Omega_{2})
quasi-norms of both sides gives

\displaystyle\|(x,y)\mapsto A(x)B(y)\|_{Y(dy,X(dx))}

\displaystyle\sim\|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}B\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}=\|B^{*}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}

\displaystyle\sim|[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}|=|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}|.

Combining the several identities above, we complete the proof of (
i
).

Next, we show (
ii
).
For every
f\in Y(\Omega_{2};\mathbb{C}^{m\times 1})^{\prime}
,

\Big\|A\int_{\Omega_{2}}Bf\Big\|_{X(\Omega_{1})}\sim\Big|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}\int_{\Omega_{2}}Bf\Big|=\Big|\int_{\Omega_{2}}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}Bf\Big|.

(2.7)

Using the assumption on the duality of
(Y(\Omega_{2}),Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime})
,

\begin{split}\sup\{\eqref{eq:AintB}:\|f\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})^{\prime}}\leq 1\}&\sim\|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}B\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}=\|B^{*}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}\|_{Y(\Omega_{2})}\\
&\sim|[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}|=|[A]_{X(\Omega_{1})}[B^{*}]_{Y(\Omega_{2})}|,\end{split}

which is one of the equivalent quantities in part (
i
).
This finishes the proof of (
ii
) and hence Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Of particular interest to us will be the following situation:

(i)

X=\textit{\L}^{p}(P)
is the
L^{p}
space on some
P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, with
p\in(0,\infty)
and the normalized quasi-norm

\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}:=\frac{\|\mathbf{1}_{P}f\|_{L^{p}}}{\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\|_{L^{p}}},

(ii)

Y=\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)
is a similar space related to the conjugate exponent

p^{\prime}:=\begin{cases}\infty,&\text{if }p\in(0,1],\\
p/(p-1),&\text{if }p\in(1,\infty),\end{cases}

(iii)

B=A^{-1}
is the pointwise inverse of
A\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}_{+})
, where
\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}_{+}
is the set of all positive definite
m\times m
matrices
and
A\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}_{+})
means
|A|^{p}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

(the set of all locally integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
).

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
V\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{F}_{+}^{m\times m})
.
Then, for every
P,R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
the quantity
\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}

has the following representations as norms of averaging operators:

\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}\sim\begin{cases}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)},&\text{if }p\in[1,\infty),\\
\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V;\textit{\L}^{\min\{1,p\}}(R))},&\text{if }p\in(0,\infty),\end{cases}

(2.8)

where
\otimes
is the tensor product and
the positive equivalence constants are independent of
V
.

Proof.

If
p\in[1,\infty)
, then
Y=\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)
is a Banach lattice with a dual pair
Y^{\prime}=\textit{\L}^{p}(R)
, with respect to the normalised integration
\langle\ \rangle_{R}:=\fint_{R}
over
R
.
Hence it follows from Lemma
2.5
and a change of variables that

\displaystyle\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}\sim\|f\mapsto V\langle V^{-1}f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}=\|f\mapsto\langle f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)}.

For the other bound, let
s:=\min(1,p)
,
and note that
\frac{1}{s}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}
.
Then using Lemma
2.5
again, we obtain

\displaystyle\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}

\displaystyle\sim\|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}V^{-1}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R)}:\ \|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}\leq 1\Big\}

\displaystyle\sim\sup\Big\{\|V,V^{-1}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{s}(R)}:\ \|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}\leq 1\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V;\textit{\L}^{s}(R))}:\ \|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R,V)}\leq 1\Big\},

and this is the desired estimate,
which completes the proof of Lemma
2.7
.
∎

The flexibility of the operator norm in Lemma
2.7

plays a key role in the remainder of this article.
It enables us to establish a deep connection between
one-parameter and multi-parameter weights (see Lemma
2.11
),
thereby reducing questions on multi-parameter weights to the
corresponding one-parameter ones.
Moreover, using Lemma
2.7
, we show some conclusions about
the weighted geometric mean of two multi-parameter weights
(see Propositions
2.32
and
2.34
),
which play an important role in the proof of
the extrapolation theorem (see
[
9
, p. 49]
)
and in part of the Jones factorization theorem (see Corollary
2.35
below).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
p,u\in(0,\infty]
and
P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
If
V\in\textit{\L}^{p}(P;\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})
and
V^{-1}\in\textit{\L}^{u}(P;\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})
, then
[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}
is invertible, and for all
M\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times k}
,

|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}M|\lesssim|[V^{-*}]_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}M|,

where
V^{-*}:=(V^{-1})^{*}
.

Proof.

By the definition of the matrix norm, it suffices to prove this with
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
in place of
M
.
Let
s\in(0,\infty]
be defined by setting

\frac{1}{s}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{u},

so that we have access to Hölder’s inequality:

|e|=\|V^{-1}Ve\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(P)}\leq\|V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}\|Ve\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}\lesssim\|V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}e|.

Hence
[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}e=\mathbf{0}
implies
e=\mathbf{0}
,
thus
[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}
is injective and therefore invertible.
Then we can compute

\displaystyle|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}e|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|V^{-*}e\cdot V[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-2}e\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(P)}\leq\|V^{-*}e\|_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}\|V[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-2}e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}

\displaystyle\lesssim|[V^{-*}]_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}e|\,|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-2}e|=|[V^{-*}]_{\textit{\L}^{u}(P)}e|\,|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}e|.

The claimed bound is trivial for
e=\mathbf{0}
, and otherwise it follows after dividing the previous computation by
|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}e|
.
∎

2.2.
Multi-parameter matrix weights

To define an
L^{p}
space with a weight, two natural definitions suggest themselves: one might think of the weight as modifying either the underlying measure, or each function in the space, as in

\bigg[\int|f(x)|^{p}w(x)\,dx\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}\ \ \text{or}\ \ \bigg[\int|v(x)f(x)|^{p}\,dx\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}}.

If the weights are scalar-valued, the two versions are obviously equivalent, via the correspondence
w=v^{p}
. When
f
is vector-valued and the weight is matrix-valued, only the second version makes sense. Even then, it has been customary to use notation derived from the first version, viewing
w
as the primary object and writing
v
as
v=w^{\frac{1}{p}}
.
However, in an increasing number of studies, good reasons have been noted to reverse this philosophy, and we follow this trend here.
Expressed in terms of this formalism, the matrix Muckenhoupt condition for
V
takes the following form.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\mathscr{P}
be a collection of pairs
(P,R)
of rectangles
P,R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, let
p\in(0,\infty)
, and let
V:\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\to\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
be (a.e.) positive-definite-valued. Then

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathscr{P})}:=\sup_{(P,R)\in\mathscr{P}}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}.

We say that
V
belongs to
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathscr{P})
and write
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathscr{P})
if
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathscr{P})}<\infty
.

In the specific cases

\mathscr{P}\in\left.\begin{cases}\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\{(Q,Q):Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\},\\
\mathrm{Rect}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}):=\{(R,R):R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\}\end{cases}\right\},

we denote
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
and
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}):=\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
.

Remark 2.10
.

We use the script-font
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
to make a distinction from the usual
A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
; these two classes are closely related but not identical. The relation is very simply:

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad W:=V^{p}\in A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}),

i.e.,
W\in A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

\Longleftrightarrow

V:=W^{\frac{1}{p}}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Thus, the class
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
is the natural one in our formalism, where
V
, instead of
W
, is the primary object of study. Note that this distinction of
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
remains even when specialised to scalar-valued weights.

As in the two key examples above, most of the time, the choice of
\mathscr{P}
will consist of pairs
(P,P)
of two equal sets, but other cases will also be relevant. In
[
12
]
, the notion of
A_{p}
-dimensions was defined, effectively involving a condition like above for

\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):=\{(Q,2^{j}Q):Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\}.

Since rectangles have
k
independent edge-lengths, we define

\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}):=\{(R,2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R):R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\},\qquad 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R:=2^{j_{1}}R_{1}\times\cdots\times 2^{j_{k}}R_{k}.

Thus,
\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathrm{Rect}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})=\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

The following lemma is a generalization of
[
54
, Lemmas 3.6 and 3.7]
, where the case
p\in(1,\infty)
and
k=2
is contained.
This can be regarded as a coordinate-wise (Fubini-type)
characterisation of multi-parameter matrix weights. Here and throughout, we denote

\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{i-1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{i+1}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}},\qquad i\in[k].

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[2,\infty)
. Then

\begin{split}\max_{i\in[k]}&\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}\\
&\lesssim[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))},\end{split}

where the implicit positive constants are independent of
V
.

Proof.

For both estimates, we make use of Lemma
2.7
. Let
s:=\min(1,p)
.

The lower bound:
Fix an
i\in[k]
. In the definition of
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))
, it is enough to consider all pairs
(Q,2^{j_{i}}Q)
, where
Q
belongs to a sufficiently rich countable subcollection
\mathscr{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})\subset\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
, say all cubes with rational corners. By Lemma
2.7
, we want to estimate the norms of the corresponding operators

f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\otimes f,\quad\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}Q,V(z^{\prime}\oplus\cdot))\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V(z^{\prime}\oplus\cdot);\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q))

(2.9)

for all
Q\in\mathscr{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
and almost all
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
.
In order to estimate these operator norms, it is enough to consider
all
f
in a suitable countable collection
\mathscr{F}\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}};\mathbb{F}^{m})
,
say of all rational linear combinations of
\mathbf{1}_{Q}
with
Q\in\mathscr{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
.
Let us then consider the countably many functions

\displaystyle z^{\prime}\mapsto

\displaystyle\|(x_{i},y_{i})\mapsto V(z^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,dx_{i};\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i}))}^{p},

\displaystyle z^{\prime}\mapsto

\displaystyle\|y_{i}\mapsto V(z^{\prime}\oplus y_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i})}^{p},

where
Q\in\mathscr{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
and
f\in\mathscr{F}
. They are all in
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
, and hence almost every
z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
is a Lebesgue point of each of these functions. For any such
z^{\prime}
and any sequence of cubes
P\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
shrinking to
z^{\prime}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{1}_{Q}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,V(z^{\prime}\oplus\cdot);\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q))}

\displaystyle=\|V(z^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,dx_{i};\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i}))}

\displaystyle=\lim_{P\to z^{\prime}}\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times Q,dx^{\prime}dx_{i};\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i}))},

moreover, with
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}:=(j_{h})_{h\in[k]\setminus\{i\}}
,
we can freely change
\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i})
to

\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P\times 2^{j_{i}}Q,dy^{\prime}dy_{i})=\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(P\times Q)),

since the integrand is independent of the additional variable
y^{\prime}\in 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P
.
Clearly

(P\times Q,2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(P\times Q))\in\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}),

and hence

\displaystyle\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times Q,dx^{\prime}dx_{i};\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i}))}

\displaystyle\quad=\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times Q,dx^{\prime}dx_{i};\textit{\L}^{s}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(P\times Q),dy^{\prime}dy_{i}))}

\displaystyle\quad\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\|V(y^{\prime}\oplus y_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(P\times Q),dy^{\prime}dy_{i})}.

Noting that
2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P
also shrinks regularly to
z
(although not necessarily cubes, these rectangles still have bounded eccentricity, since the dilation parameter
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}
is fixed), the other half of the Lebesgue point assumption implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to z^{\prime}}\|V(y^{\prime}\oplus y_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(P\times Q),dy^{\prime}dy_{i})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{P\to z^{\prime}}\|V(y^{\prime}\oplus y_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P,dy^{\prime};\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i}))}

\displaystyle=\|V(z^{\prime}\oplus y_{i})f(y_{i})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}Q,dy_{i})}.

Thus, we obtained the boundedness of (
2.9
), and hence the lower bound claimed in the Lemma.

The upper bound:

Let first
p\in(0,1]
. For all
P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, we should now establish the boundedness of

f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\otimes f,\quad\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V;\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P))=\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V\otimes\mathbf{1}).

(2.10)

Let
P=P^{\prime}\times P_{k}
and
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}=(\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime},j_{k})
. From Fubini’s theorem, the uniform
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-property of weights
x_{k}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})
, and the resulting uniform boundedness of the related operators in the
x_{k}
direction, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V\otimes\mathbf{1})}=\|\mathbf{1}_{P}(x)f(y)\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V(x),dxdy)}

\displaystyle\quad=\Big\|\mathbf{1}_{P^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})\|\mathbf{1}_{P}(x_{k})f(y^{\prime},y_{k})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{k}\times 2^{j_{k}}P_{k},V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})\otimes\mathbf{1},dx_{k}dy_{k})}\Big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime}\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P^{\prime},dx^{\prime}dy^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\Big\|\mathbf{1}_{P^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}\|f(y^{\prime},y_{k})\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{k}}P_{k},V(x^{\prime}\oplus y_{k})dy_{k})}\Big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime}\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}P^{\prime},dx^{\prime}dy^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\quad=\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}\|\mathbf{1}_{P^{\prime}}(x^{\prime})f(y)\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime}\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V(x^{\prime},y_{k}),dx^{\prime}dy)}.

Since all exponents are equal to
p
, we can permute the order of the quasi-norms as we like, bringing the next
\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{i}\times 2^{j_{i}}P_{i})
into the innermost position,
and using the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-property of the
x_{i}
direction to replace the quasi-norm over this space by a quasi-norm over just
\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}P_{i})
. Repeating this for each
i\in[k]
, we end up with

\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P\times 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V\otimes\mathbf{1})}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V)},

which gives the required estimate for
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}
by the equivalence of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
constant and the norm of the operators (
2.10
).

For
p\in(1,\infty)
, the argument is similar, except that in place of (
2.10
), we consider the operators

A_{P,\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}:\ f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P},\quad\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V).

Note that

A_{P,\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}A_{P_{i},j_{i}}^{(i)}=A_{P^{\prime},\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}^{\prime}\,A_{P_{k},j_{k}}^{(k)}

where the operator
A_{P_{i},j_{i}}^{(i)}
acts in the
x_{i}
direction, and thus these operators commute. Hence

\displaystyle\|A_{P,\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)}

\displaystyle=\Big\|\|A_{P_{k},j_{k}}^{(k)}(A_{P^{\prime},\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}^{\prime}f)\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{k},V(x^{\prime},\cdot))}\Big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime},dx^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}\|A_{P^{\prime},\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}^{\prime}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{k}}P_{k},V(x^{\prime},\cdot))}\Big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime},dx^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{k}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}\|A_{P^{\prime},\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,{}^{\prime}}^{\prime}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P^{\prime}\times 2^{j_{k}}P_{k},V)}.

Again, we can permute the order of the quasi-norms. For each
i\in[k]
in its turn, we bring the quasi-norm over
\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{i})
as the innermost object, and use the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition in the
x_{i}
direction to remove the
operator
A_{P_{i},j_{i}}^{(i)}
while changing the said innermost
\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{i})
quasi-norm to the quasi-norm of
\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{j_{i}}P_{i})
. In the end, we are left with

\|A_{P,\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P,V)},

which is equivalent to the claimed bound for
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}
.
∎

2.3.
The
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of multi-parameter weights

In
[
12
]
, the notion of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions of weights was introduced,
which played an important role in the theory of matrix-weighted Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces
in the one-parameter context
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
.
We now wish to generalise this notion to the multi-parameter weights.
Rather than repeating the theory from scratch,
we aim at using the coordinate-wise (Fubini-type) characterization of
product
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-weights from Lemma
2.11
,
so as to be able to build on the existing theory as much as possible.
Thus, we start by collecting some useful facts about matrix weights in the isotropic case.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and suppose that
U\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})
. If

M\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times l}\setminus\{\mathbf{O}\}
, then

[|UM|^{p}]_{A_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim[|UM|^{p}]_{A_{\max(1,p)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq(c[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})^{p},

where the implicit positive constant in the first bound depends only on
n
, and the positive constant
c
in the second bound only on
m
and
l
.

Proof.

The first bound is a purely scalar result for the weight
w:=|UM|^{p}
. It follows from

[w]_{A_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim[w]_{A_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\exp}:=\sup_{Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle w\rangle_{Q}\exp\langle\log w^{-1}\rangle_{Q}\leq[w]_{A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

where the second estimate is an application of Jensen’s inequality, and the first one is
[
87
, Proposition 2.2]
(where the notation for the two
A_{\infty}
constants is different).

We then consider the second bound. Let first
p\in(0,1]
. Then, for almost all
y\in Q
,

\displaystyle\fint_{Q}|U(x)M|^{p}dx

\displaystyle=\fint_{Q}|U(x)U(y)^{-1}U(y)M|^{p}dx

\displaystyle\leq\fint_{Q}|U(x)U(y)^{-1}|^{p}dx\,|U(y)M|^{p}\leq[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}|U(y)M|^{p},

which is precisely the classical
A_{1}
condition on
|UM|^{p}
with constant
[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}
.

We turn to the second bound with
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Let first
l=1
, i.e.,
M=e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
The result in this case is stated in
[
54
, Lemma 3.2(2)]
, referring to a “slightly different” formulation in
[
92
, Lemma 2.2]
, whose proof in turn refers to
[
122
,
150
]
. For completeness, we give a short argument here, based on Lemma
2.7
. By that lemma, we have

\begin{split}[|Ue|^{p}]_{A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{\frac{1}{p}}=[|Ue|]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\sim\sup_{Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)},\\
[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}&\sim\sup_{Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle F\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)}.\end{split}

(2.11)

Given
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
and
f\in\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)
, we consider the vector-valued function
F:=f(\cdot)e
. Then

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)}&=\big\|\mathbf{1}_{Q}|U\langle f(\cdot)e\rangle_{Q}|\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}=\|\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f(\cdot)e\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)}\\
&\leq\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle F\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)}\|f(\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)},\end{split}

where

\|f(\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)}=\|f(\cdot)U(\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}=\big\|f(\cdot)|U(\cdot)e|\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q)}=\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)}.

This shows that

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle f\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,|Ue|)}\leq\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{Q}\langle F\rangle_{Q}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(Q,U)},

which completes the proof by (
2.11
).

Let finally
p\in(1,\infty)
and
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be general. Let
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{l}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{F}^{l}
. Then

|UM|\leq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{l}|UMe_{i}|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{l}|UMe_{i}|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}}\leq(l|UM|^{p})^{\frac{1}{p}}l^{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})_{+}}=l^{\frac{1}{2}\vee\frac{1}{p}}|UM|.

With
w:=|UM|^{p}
and
w_{i}:=|UMe_{i}|^{p}
, it follows that
w^{-1}\leq l(\sum_{j=1}^{l}w_{j})^{-1}\leq lw_{i}^{-1}
. Hence

\fint_{Q}w\Big(\fint_{Q}w^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)^{p-1}\leq l^{(\frac{p}{2}-1)_{+}}\sum_{i=1}^{l}\fint_{Q}w_{i}\Big(\fint_{Q}l^{\frac{1}{p-1}}w_{i}^{-\frac{1}{p-1}}\Big)^{p-1}\leq l^{\frac{p}{2}\vee 1}\sum_{i=1}^{l}[w_{i}]_{A_{p}},

and thus
[w]_{A_{p}}\leq l^{\frac{p}{2}\vee 1+1}\max_{i}[w_{i}]_{A_{p}}\leq l^{2p}\max_{i}[w_{i}]_{A_{p}}
, recalling that
p\in(1,\infty)
.
By the previous part of the proof with
e=Me_{i}
, we have
[w_{i}]_{A_{p}}\leq(c[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}})^{p}
, and hence
[w]_{A_{p}}\leq l^{2p}(c[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}})^{p}=(c^{\prime}[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}})^{p}
, where
c^{\prime}:=l^{2}c
depends at most on
m
(since
c
does) and
l
. This completes the proof.
∎

The next reverse Hölder inequality for matrix weights is essentially
[
120
, Lemma 2]
, but we revisit the argument to cover the case
p\in(0,1)
, which is not considered in
[
120
]
.

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
U\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})
. Then for all
M\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times k}
and all
r\in(0,1+c/[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{p}]
, where
c
depends at most on the dimensions and
p
, we have,
for every
Q\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\bigg[\fint_{Q}|U(x)M|^{pr}\,dx\bigg]^{\frac{1}{pr}}\lesssim\bigg[\fint_{Q}|U(x)M|^{p}\,dx\bigg]^{\frac{1}{p}},

where the implicit positive constant is universal.

Proof.

According to Lemma
2.12
, each
|U(x)M|^{p}
is an
A_{\infty}
weight with
A_{\infty}
constant at most
C[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}^{p}
, absorbing the exponent
p
by allowing
p
-dependence of the constant
c
. By
[
88
, Theorem 2.3]
, such a weight satisfies the reverse Hölder inequality for all

r\leq 1+\frac{1}{2^{n+1}C[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}}^{p}-1},

and then in particular for all
r
as in the assumptions of the lemma. This completes the proof.
∎

After these preliminaries, we turn to discuss the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of multi-parameter weights, extending
[
12
, Definition 2.22]
in the one-parameter case.

Definition 2.14
.

We say that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
\vec{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
if

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}^{p}\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{d}}\quad\text{for all}\quad\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}.

Remark 2.15
.

Note that, if
k=1
and
V=W^{\frac{1}{p}}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then

V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
d
if and only if

W\in A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has
A_{p}
-dimension
d
in the sense of
[
12
, Definition 2.22]
.
When
m=1
, this characterises the reverse doubling property of the weight
W

(see
[
12
, Proposition 2.36]
).

Proposition 2.16
.

Every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
has some
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
\vec{d}
with

\vec{0}\leq\vec{d}<\vec{n},

where both inequalities are understood pointwise for each component.

Proof.

By
[
12
, Proposition 2.27]
, every
U\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has some
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
d\in[0,n)
. Moreover, the proof of
[
12
, Proposition 2.27]
shows that one can take
d(U)=\frac{n}{r(U)}
, where
r(U)>1
is any number for which the reverse Hölder inequality of Lemma
2.13
is satisfied. The said lemma shows in particular that we can choose the same
r>1
, and hence the same
d<n
for any family of weights
U\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with uniform bound on
[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

We apply this observation to each of the families
\mathscr{V}_{i}:=\{x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i}):x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}\}
with
i\in[k]
,
where
[U]_{\mathscr{A}_{p_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})}
, for
U\in\mathscr{V}_{i}
, is uniformly bounded by the lower bound in Lemma
2.11
. Thus, all
U\in\mathscr{V}_{i}
have some
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
d\in[0,n)
, or in other words

\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})]_{A_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}^{2}_{j_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}\lesssim 2^{d_{i}j_{i}}\quad\text{for all}\quad j_{i}\in\mathbb{N},

where
d_{i}\in[0,n_{i})
.
Substituting these bounds into the upper bound of Lemma
2.11
, we obtain

[V]_{A_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}2^{d_{i}j_{i}}=2^{\vec{d}\cdot\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}},

and hence
V
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
\vec{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{k})
.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

In
[
12
, Section 2.2]
, several examples are given of
the possible behaviour of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-dimension of
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
All these examples are scalar-valued, and can be readily lifted to
provide analogous examples concerning the behaviour of
the
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
-dimension of
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Indeed, for scalar-valued weights
V_{i}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
,
it is easy to see that
V(x):=\prod_{i=1}^{k}V_{i}(x_{i})
is in
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

if and only if
V_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
for every
i\in[k]
,
and that
V
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
-dimension
\vec{d}
if and only if

V_{i}
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
-dimension
d_{i}
for every
i\in[k]
.
From the results of
[
12
, Section 2.2]

it follows in particular that:
For every
\vec{d}
with
\vec{0}\leq\vec{d}<\vec{n}
and for every
I\subset[k]
,
there exists a weight
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
such that

V
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
-dimension
\vec{e}
if and only if

(i)

e_{i}\geq d_{i}
for all
i\in I
, and

(ii)

e_{j}>d_{j}
for all
j\in[k]\setminus I
.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Then for
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\lesssim\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }p\in(0,1],\\
