2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{e}/p^{\prime}},&\text{if }p\in(1,\infty)\text{ and }V^{-1}\text{ has }\mathscr{A}_{p^{\prime}}\text{-dimension }\vec{e}.\end{cases}

When
p\in(1,\infty)
, the condition on the
\mathscr{A}_{p^{\prime}}
-dimension is also necessary.

Proof.

The quantity on the left is

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}=\sup_{R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R)}.

If
p\in(0,1]
, then
p^{\prime}=\infty
, and
\|\ \|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R)}\leq\|\ \|_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(R)}
, so that

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\lesssim 1.

If
p\in(1,\infty)
, noting that
R\mapsto 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R
is a bijection of
\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, we have

\displaystyle[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}

\displaystyle=\sup_{R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}

\displaystyle\sim\sup_{R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|V^{-1},V\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R),\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}R)}=[V^{-1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p^{\prime}}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}.

By the definition of dimensions, the right-hand side is bounded by
2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{e}/p^{\prime}}
if and only if
V^{-1}
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
\vec{e}
,
which completes the proof of Lemma
2.18
.
∎

Definition 2.19
.

We say that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})
if

(1)

V
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
\vec{d}
,

(2)

p\in(1,\infty)
and
V^{-1}
has
\mathscr{A}_{p^{\prime}}
-dimension
\vec{e}
, or
p\in(0,1]
and
\vec{e}=\vec{0}
, and

(3)

\vec{\Delta}=\vec{d}/p+\vec{e}/p^{\prime}
.

The following result is a multi-parameter extension of
[
12
, Lemma 2.28]
, where the case
k=1
was considered:

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Then
V
have
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})

if and only if, for every
P,R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\max\bigg\{\bigg[\frac{\ell(R_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{d_{i}}{p}},\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{i})}{\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{e_{i}}{p^{\prime}}}\bigg\}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}}|}{\max\{\ell(P_{i}),\ell(R_{i})\}}\bigg]^{\Delta_{i}},

where the implicit positive constant is independent of
P
and
R
.

Proof.

We first prove “
\Longleftarrow
”.
From Lemma
2.5
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S)}

\displaystyle=|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}|\leq|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|\,|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}|

\displaystyle\sim|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|\,\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(R)}\lesssim|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|.

Thus, for every
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,

\displaystyle[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}=\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P)}\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{d}/p}

and

\displaystyle[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}^{2}_{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}=\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P)}\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{e}/p^{\prime}}.

These, together with Lemma
2.18
,
further imply that
V
have
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})
.

Next, we show “
\Longrightarrow
”.
Let
S\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
be the minimal rectangle that contains
P\cup R
; thus,
\ell(S_{i})\sim\ell(P_{i})+\ell(R_{i})+|c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}}|
for every
i\in[k]
. We then write

[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}=[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}^{-1}\times[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}.

By Lemma
2.8
, we obtain

\begin{split}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}^{-1}|&=|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}^{-1}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}|\\
&\leq|[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}|=|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S)}|.\end{split}

From Lemma
2.5
, it follows that

|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S)}|\sim\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(S)}\lesssim\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P)},

where
2^{j_{i}}\sim\ell(S_{i})/\ell(P_{i})
. By definition,

\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}P)}\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{d}/p}.

Similarly,

\begin{split}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|&\leq|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}|\\
&\sim\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(S),\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}\lesssim\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{h}}R),\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{-\vec{h}}(2^{\vec{h}}R))},\end{split}

where
2^{h_{i}}\sim\ell(S_{i})/\ell(R_{i})
, and by Lemma
2.18
,

\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{\vec{h}}R),\textit{\L}^{p}(2^{-\vec{h}}(2^{\vec{h}}R))}\lesssim 2^{\vec{h}\cdot\vec{e}/p^{\prime}}.

Combining the estimates, we have

\begin{split}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|&\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{d}/p}2^{\vec{h}\cdot\vec{e}/p^{\prime}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}2^{j_{i}d_{i}/p+h_{i}e_{i}/p^{\prime}}\sim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg[\frac{\ell(S_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{d_{i}}{p}}\bigg[\frac{\ell(S_{i})}{\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{e_{i}}{p^{\prime}}}\\
&=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{i})\vee\ell(R_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{d_{i}}{p}}\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{i})\vee\ell(R_{i})}{\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{e_{i}}{p^{\prime}}}\bigg[\frac{\ell(S_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})\vee\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{d_{i}}{p}+\frac{e_{i}}{p^{\prime}}}\\
&\sim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\max\bigg\{\bigg[\frac{\ell(R_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{d_{i}}{p}},\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{i})}{\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\frac{e_{i}}{p^{\prime}}}\bigg\}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}}|}{\ell(P_{i})\vee\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{\Delta_{i}},\end{split}

which is exactly the claimed bound.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

It was shown in
[
12
, Lemma 2.47]
that Lemma
2.20
is sharp in the case
k=1
. The example there is scalar-valued, and hence allows readily the construction of analogous examples with arbitrary
k
by considering scalar-valued weights of the form
V(x)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}V_{i}(x_{i})
, which satisfy

|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|=\prod_{i=1}^{k}|[V_{i}]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P_{i})}[V_{i}]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R_{i})}^{-1}|.

Since the estimate of each factor is sharp by
[
12
, Lemma 2.47]
, it follows that the full estimate of Lemma
2.20
is sharp as well.

It is convenient to record the following special case of Lemma
2.20
. Note in particular that the bound on the right is independent of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
dimensions and holds for every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. This simple bound is already enough for many purposes.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
have
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})
.
Then, for every
P,R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|\lesssim\Big[1+|2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(c_{P}-c_{R})|\Big]^{|\vec{\Delta}|}\leq\Big[1+|2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(c_{P}-c_{R})|\Big]^{|\vec{n}|},

where the implicit positive constant is independent of
P
and
R
.

Proof.

Now
\ell(P_{i})=\ell(R_{i})=2^{-j_{i}}
. From Lemma
2.20
and the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality with weights
\Delta_{i}/|\vec{\Delta}|
, we obtain

\begin{split}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R)}^{-1}|&\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Big[1+|2^{j_{i}}(c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}})|\Big]^{\Delta_{i}}\\
&\leq\Bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Big[1+|2^{j_{i}}(c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}})|\Big]\Bigg\}^{|\vec{\Delta}|}\sim\Big[1+|2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(c_{P}-c_{R})|\Big]^{|\vec{\Delta}|}.\end{split}

Finally, since
\vec{0}\leq\vec{d},\vec{e}<\vec{n}
, we have
\vec{0}\leq\vec{\Delta}=\vec{d}/p+\vec{e}/p^{\prime}<\vec{n}
, and hence
|\vec{\Delta}|<|\vec{n}|
.
∎

For some applications, it will be enough to have a family of matrices
\{A_{P}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
with bounds like those satisfied by
[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}
in Lemma
2.20
, but not necessarily arising from reducing operators of some
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Adapting
[
134
, Definition 1.3]
and
[
67
, Definition 2.1]
, we give this property a name:

Definition 2.23
.

A sequence of non-negative matrices
\{A_{P}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
is said to be

(i)

strongly doubling of order

(\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c})\in[0,\infty)^{k}\times[0,\infty)^{k}\times[0,\infty)^{k}
if

|A_{P}A_{R}^{-1}|\lesssim\prod_{i=1}^{k}\max\bigg\{\bigg[\frac{\ell(R_{i})}{\ell(P_{i})}\bigg]^{a_{i}},\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{i})}{\ell(R_{i})}\bigg]^{b_{i}}\bigg\}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{P_{i}}-c_{R_{i}}|}{\max\{\ell(P_{i}),\ell(R_{i})\}}\bigg]^{c_{i}}

for all
P,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
;

(ii)

weakly doubling of order

d\in[0,\infty)
if

|A_{P}A_{R}^{-1}|\lesssim\Big[1+|2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(c_{P}-c_{R})|\Big]^{d}

for all
P,R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, for all
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

Here, the implicit positive constants are independent of
P
and
R
.

Remark 2.24
.

In the language of Definition
2.23
, the last two Lemmas
2.20
and
2.22
say that, for each
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})
, the sequence of reducing operators
\{[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
is strongly doubling of order
(\vec{d}/p,\vec{e}/p^{\prime},\vec{\Delta})
and weakly doubling of order
|\vec{\Delta}|
. Moreover, the proof of Lemma
2.22
shows that every strongly doubling sequence of order
(\vec{a},\vec{b},\vec{c})
is weakly doubling of order
|\vec{c}|
.

Remark 2.25
.

Let us observe some alternative ways of writing the upper bound in the condition of strong doubling when
P\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Then

\frac{1}{\max\{\ell(P_{h}),\ell(R_{h})\}}=\frac{1}{\max\{2^{-i_{h}},2^{-j_{h}}\}}=2^{\min(i_{h},j_{h})},

and thus

\begin{split}\prod_{h=1}^{k}\bigg(1+\frac{|c_{P_{h}}-c_{R_{h}}|}{\max\{\ell(P_{h}),\ell(R_{h})\}}\bigg)^{c_{h}}&=\prod_{h=1}^{k}[1+|2^{\min(i_{h},j_{h})}(c_{P_{h}}-c_{R_{h}})|]^{c_{h}}\\
&=\Omega^{\vec{c}}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\wedge\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}[c_{P}-c_{R}]),\end{split}

where

\Omega^{\vec{c}}(x):=\prod_{h=1}^{k}(1+|x|)^{c_{h}}.

(2.12)

We also have

\frac{\ell(R_{h})}{\ell(P_{h})}=\frac{2^{-j_{h}}}{2^{-i_{h}}}=2^{i_{h}-j_{h}},

hence

\max\bigg\{\bigg[\frac{\ell(R_{h})}{\ell(P_{h})}\bigg]^{a_{h}},\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{h})}{\ell(R_{h})}\bigg]^{b_{h}}\bigg\}=\max\{2^{(i_{h}-j_{h})a_{h}},2^{(j_{h}-i_{h})b_{h}}\}=2^{(i_{h}-j_{h})_{+}a_{h}+(i_{h}-j_{h})_{-}b_{h}},

and thus

\prod_{h=1}^{k}\max\bigg\{\bigg[\frac{\ell(R_{h})}{\ell(P_{h})}\bigg]^{a_{h}},\bigg[\frac{\ell(P_{h})}{\ell(R_{h})}\bigg]^{b_{h}}\bigg\}=2^{(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}})_{+}\cdot\vec{a}+(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}})_{-}\cdot\vec{b}}\leq 2^{|\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}|\max(\vec{a},\vec{b})}.

2.4.
Reverse Hölder inequalities

We have already encountered a version of the reverse Hölder inequality in Lemma
2.13
. We now perform a more systematic study of this concept. For exponents
0<p\leq s<\infty
, a set
E
of positive finite measure, and a function
V:E\to\mathbb{F}^{m\times m}
, we define

[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(E)}:=\sup_{e\in\mathbb{C}^{m}}\frac{\|V(\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(E)}}{\|V(\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(E)}},

taking
0/0:=0
and
\infty/\infty:=\infty
if necessary. For any collection
\mathscr{E}
of such sets, we can then define

[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathscr{E})}:=\sup_{E\in\mathscr{E}}[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(E)}.

For these quantities, the following lemma is an analogue
of Lemma
2.11
, which can be regarded as a coordinate-wise (Fubini-type)
characterisation of multi-parameter matrix weights.

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[2,\infty)
. Then

\begin{split}\sup_{i\in[k]}&\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}\\
&\leq[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{k}\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}}[x_{i}\mapsto V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{i})]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))}.\end{split}

Proof.

The lower bound.
We may assume that
[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}<\infty
, for otherwise there is nothing to prove. Thus,
V\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})\subset L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{F}^{m\times m})
. In the defining conditions, it is enough to consider suitable countable families of
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
and
Q_{i}\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
. Fix some
i\in[k]
and
Q_{i}\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
.
Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
be a Lebesgue point of each

x^{\prime}\mapsto\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{L^{s}(Q_{i})}^{s},\qquad x^{\prime}\mapsto\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{L^{p}(Q_{i})}^{p}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(Q_{i})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{Q^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}}\|Ve\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(Q^{\prime}\oplus Q_{i})}\leq\lim_{Q^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}}[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\|Ve\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q^{\prime}\oplus Q_{i})}

\displaystyle=[V]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))}\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q_{i})},

which is the claimed lower estimate.

The upper bound.
Let
R=R^{\prime}\times Q_{k}
, where
R^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}})
and
Q_{k}\in\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}})
. Then

\displaystyle\|Ve\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R)}

\displaystyle=\bigg[\fint_{R^{\prime}}\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(Q_{k})}^{s}\,dx^{\prime}\bigg]^{1/s}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg[\fint_{R^{\prime}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}^{s}\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)e\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q_{k})}^{s}\,dx^{\prime}\bigg]^{1/s}

\displaystyle\leq\operatornamewithlimits{ess\,sup}_{x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}}[V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)]_{\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{k}}))}\big\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R^{\prime},dx^{\prime};\textit{\L}^{p}(Q_{k},dx_{k}))}.

Since
p\leq s
, it follows from Minkowski’s inequality that

\big\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R^{\prime},dx^{\prime};\textit{\L}^{p}(Q_{k},dx_{k}))}\leq\big\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(Q_{k},dx_{k};\textit{\L}^{s}(R^{\prime},dx^{\prime}))}.

Here, for each fixed
x_{k}\in Q_{k}
, the inner quasi-norm
\|V(x^{\prime}\oplus x_{k})\|_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R^{\prime},dx^{\prime})}
has the same form as what we started with, but in a lower dimension. Thus, we can iterate the same argument, arriving at the claimed conclusion.
∎

Combining the product characterizations of both
\mathscr{A}_{p}
(Lemma
2.11
) and reverse Hölder properties (Lemma
2.26
), we can deduce a reverse Hölder property of multi-parameter
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights as a quick corollary of the corresponding one-parameter result (Lemma
2.13
):

Corollary 2.27
.

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
, then
V\in\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
for some
s\in(p,\infty)
, uniformly over bounded subsets of
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
.

Proof.

Suppose that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
. By Lemma
2.11
, for each
i\in[k]
and almost all
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}
, we have
V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))
and, moreover, there is a uniform bound on the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
constant. By Lemma
2.13
, there is some
s\in(p,\infty)
, uniform over bounded subsets of
\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
, such that these same weights satisfy
V(x^{\prime}\oplus\cdot)\in\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Cubes}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}}))
with a universal bound on the
\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}
constants. By Lemma
2.26
, it then follows that
V\in\mathrm{RHI}_{p,s}(\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}))
.
∎

2.5.
Interpolation of matrix-weighted Lebesgue spaces

We first recall the concept of the Peetre
K
-functional.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
be quasi-Banach spaces.
Assume that both
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
are subspaces of the same Hausdorff topological vector spaces.
For all
t\in(0,\infty)
and

f\in A_{0}+A_{1}:=\{f_{0}+f_{1}:\ f_{0}\in A_{0},\ f_{1}\in A_{1}\}
,
the
Peetre
K
-functional
is defined by setting

K(t,f):=\inf\{\|f_{0}\|_{A_{0}}+t\|f_{1}\|_{A_{1}}:\ f=f_{0}+f_{1},\ f_{0}\in A_{0},\ f_{1}\in A_{1}\}.

For all
\theta\in(0,1)
and
p\in(0,\infty)
,
the
interpolation space

(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,p}
is defined by setting

(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,p}:=\{f\in A_{0}+A_{1}:\ \|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,p}}<\infty\},

where, for all
f\in A_{0}+A_{1}
,

\|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,p}}:=\bigg\{\int_{0}^{\infty}\Big[t^{-\theta}K(t,f)\Big]^{p}\,\frac{dt}{t}\bigg\}^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
A
and
B
be two positive definite matrices.
For all
\theta\in(0,1)
, let

A\#_{\theta}B:=A^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(A^{-\frac{1}{2}}BA^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Big)^{\theta}A^{\frac{1}{2}}

be the
weighted geometric mean
of
A
and
B
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty]
and
V:\ \mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\to\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
be measurable.
The
Lebesgue space

L^{p}(V)
is defined to be the set of
all vector-valued measurable functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}

such that
\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}:=\|\,|Vf|\,\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}<\infty
.

The following theorem extends a classical interpolation theorem of

L^{p}
spaces with change of measures
[
7
, Theorem 5.5.1]

to the matrix-weighted setting, which is precisely
a part of
[
20
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,\infty)
and
V_{0},V_{1}
be two matrix weights
on a
\sigma
-finite measure space
(\Omega,\mu)
.
Assume that
\theta\in(0,1)
and

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}.

Then the real interpolation method
(\ ,\ )_{\theta,p}
satisfies

(L^{p_{0}}(V_{0}),L^{p_{1}}(V_{1}))_{\theta,p}=L^{p}\Big((V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big).

(2.13)

Remark 2.31
.

Let all the notation be as in Theorem
2.30
.

(i)

Recall that
[
20
, Theorem 4.4]
showed that,
if
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
, then

(L^{p_{0}}(V_{0}),L^{p_{1}}(V_{1}))_{\theta,p}=L^{p}\Big(|V_{1}V_{0}^{-1}|^{\theta}V_{0}\Big),

(2.14)

where
|V_{1}V_{0}^{-1}|:=[(V_{1}V_{0}^{-1})^{*}V_{1}V_{0}^{-1}]^{\frac{1}{2}}=(V_{0}^{-1}V_{1}^{2}V_{0}^{-1})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
A careful checking indicates that their proof still works
for all
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,\infty)
. Note that

\displaystyle V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2}=V_{0}\Big(V_{0}^{-1}V_{1}^{2}V_{0}^{-1}\Big)^{\theta}V_{0}=V_{0}\Big|V_{1}V_{0}^{-1}\Big|^{2\theta}V_{0}=\Big(|V_{1}V_{0}^{-1}|^{\theta}V_{0}\Big)^{2}

and hence (
2.14
) coincides with (
2.13
).

(ii)

If
V_{0}V_{1}=V_{1}V_{0}
almost everywhere, then in this case one can show that

(V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}=V_{0}^{1-\theta}V_{1}^{\theta}

(see also
[
20
, Section 4.1.1]
) and hence
(
2.13
) in this case has the following simpler version

(L^{p_{0}}(V_{0}),L^{p_{1}}(V_{1}))_{\theta,p}=L^{p}\Big(V_{0}^{1-\theta}V_{1}^{\theta}\Big).

Theorem
2.30
has the following application to
\mathscr{A}_{p}
weights:

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,\infty)
,
\theta\in(0,1)
, and
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1-\theta}{p_{0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}
.
Assume that
V_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
for both
i\in\{0,1\}
,
and
V:=(V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

If either both
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
or
both
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,1]
, then
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim[V_{0}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}^{1-\theta}[V_{1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}^{\theta},

where the implicit positive constant is independent of
\theta
.

(ii)

The assumption of (
i
) is necessary for the conclusion of (
i
),
that is, if
0<p_{0}<1<p_{1}<\infty
, then there exist

V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
V_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

such that
V\notin\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

Proof.

We first consider the case where
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
.
In this case,
p\in[1,\infty)
. By Lemma
2.7
, we find that

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\sim\sup_{R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\langle f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)}

and likewise with each
(V_{i},p_{i})
in place of
(V,p)
.
From Theorem
2.30
, it follows that

L^{p}(R;V)=(L^{p_{0}}(R;V_{0}),L^{p_{1}}(R;V_{1}))_{p,\theta}.

(2.15)

This, together with
[
7
, Theorem 3.11.2]
,
further implies that, for each
R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\langle f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)}\leq\prod_{i=0}^{1}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\langle f\rangle_{R}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{i}}(R;V_{i})\to\textit{\L}^{p_{i}}(R;V_{i})}^{\theta_{i}},

where
\theta_{0}:=1-\theta
and
\theta_{1}:=\theta
. Taking the supremum over
R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
shows that

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim[V_{0}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}^{1-\theta}[V_{1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}^{\theta}.

(2.16)

Next, we consider the case where
p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,1]
.
In this case,
p\in(0,1]
. By Lemma
2.7
, we conclude that

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\sim\sup_{R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(R\times R;V\otimes\mathbf{1})},

where
(V\otimes\mathbf{1})(x,y):=V(x)
, and likewise with each
(V_{i},p_{i})
in place of
(V,p)
. As before, Theorem
2.30
gives interpolation property (
2.15
), and also the analogue with
R
replaced by
R\times R
, each
V_{i}
by
V_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}
, and
V
by

\Big[(V_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1})^{2}\#_{\theta}(V_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1})^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Big(V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\otimes\mathbf{1},

where the last identity is immediate from the pointwise definition of the geometric mean. Hence
[
7
, Theorem 3.11.2]
implies that

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(R;V)\to\textit{\L}^{p}(R\times R;V\otimes\mathbf{1})}\leq\prod_{i=0}^{1}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{R}\otimes f\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{i}}(R;V_{i})\to\textit{\L}^{p_{i}}(R\times R;V_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1})}^{\theta_{i}},

and taking the supremum over
R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
gives (
2.16
) as before.

Finally, without loss of generality,
we may only consider the case where
0<p_{0}<1<p_{1}<\infty
.
The reason that we cannot repeat a reasoning as above in this case
is the qualitative turning point of the characterizing condition of
Lemma
2.7
at the exponent
1
. Let us now see that
there is a genuine obstacle, not just an issue with the chosen method of proof.

Our counterexample will be given in terms of scalar-valued weights
v_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{i}}
.
(Note that, even for scalar-valued weights, there is a distinction between the classical notion of
A_{p}
and our
\mathscr{A}_{p}
, which is a rescaling of the classical definition.) Then the geometric mean takes the simple form
v=v_{0}^{1-\theta}v_{1}^{\theta}
.
Let us also consider the one-dimensional domain
\mathbb{R}
for simplicity. Let
w_{i}:=v_{i}^{p_{i}}\in A_{p_{i}}(\mathbb{R})
and

w:=v^{p}=w_{0}^{(1-\theta)\frac{p}{p_{0}}}w_{1}^{\theta\frac{p}{p_{1}}}.

For scalar weights, the qualitative turning point of the
A_{q}
conditions
at
q=1
is reflected in the fact that all scalar-valued
A_{q}
classes
for all
q\in(0,1]
collapse to just the classical
A_{1}
.
Hence
w_{0}\in A_{1}(\mathbb{R})
,
w_{0}\in A_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})
,
and we need to show that it can happen that
w\notin A_{\max\{1,p\}}(\mathbb{R})
.
We choose
w_{0}\equiv 1\in A_{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
w_{1}(x):=|x|^{\alpha}

for some
\alpha\in(0,p_{1}-1)
so that
w_{1}\in A_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then
w(x)=|x|^{\alpha\theta p/p_{1}}
.

If
p\in(0,1]
, it suffices to observe that
w
is
|x|
raised to some positive power, and hence
w\notin A_{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
If
p\in(1,\infty)
, we need to investigate this power a little more carefully. From the defining equality of
p
and the assumption that
p_{0}\in(0,1)
, hence
1-\frac{1}{p_{0}}<0
, it follows that

1-\frac{1}{p}=(1-\theta)\bigg(1-\frac{1}{p_{0}}\bigg)+\theta\bigg(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\bigg)<\theta\bigg(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\bigg),

and therefore
p-1<(p_{1}-1)\theta p/p_{1}
.
Due to this strict inequality, we can choose
\alpha\in(0,p_{1}-1)

so that
p-1<\alpha\theta p/p_{1}

and hence
w\notin A_{p}(\mathbb{R})
.
This finishes the proof of Proposition
2.32
.
∎

Remark 2.33
.

Case
p_{0},p_{1}\in[1,\infty)
of Proposition
2.32
(
i
) is due to

[
9
, Proposition 8.8]
by a different method,
although they also indicated in
[
9
, Remark 8.10]

an alternative proof based on interpolation.
However, both their first proof of
[
9
, Proposition 8.8]

and the alternative indicated in
[
9
, Remark 8.10]

rely heavily on duality considerations, which are not available
for exponents below
1
. Our new approach in the proof of
Proposition
2.32
(
i
) has the advantage of covering both cases by simple variants of the same method. Another advantage is that it allows us to stay with matrix weights throughout the argument, in contrast to changing the
point of view to their induced norms, as in the proof of
[
9
, Proposition 8.8]
.

The negative result of (
ii
) suggests some trouble to prospective
extrapolation results in the full scale
p\in(0,\infty)
,
while extrapolation in the range
p\geq 1

was the main application of
[
9
, Proposition 8.8]
.

Indeed,
[
9
, Proposition 8.8]
includes more conclusions.
Proposition
2.32
together with the following Proposition
2.34

contains
[
9
, Proposition 8.8]
.

Proposition 2.34
.

Let
V_{0},V_{1}
be two matrix weights on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
.
Assume that
\theta\in(0,1)
and
V:=(V_{0}^{2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Then the following assertions hold.

(i)

If
p\in(0,\infty)
and
V_{0}^{-1},V_{1}^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
then
V^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

(ii)

If
p\in[1,\infty)
,
V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
and
V_{1}^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
then
V\in\mathscr{A}_{\frac{p}{1-\theta}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

Proof.

(
i
): Note that

\begin{split}V^{-1}&=\Big[V_{0}\Big(V_{0}^{-1}V_{1}^{2}V_{0}^{-1}\Big)^{\theta}V_{0}\Big]^{-\frac{1}{2}}=\Big[V_{0}^{-1}\Big(V_{0}^{-1}V_{1}^{2}V_{0}^{-1}\Big)^{-\theta}V_{0}^{-1}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\
&=\Big[V_{0}^{-1}\Big(V_{0}V_{1}^{-2}V_{0}\Big)^{\theta}V_{0}^{-1}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Big(V_{0}^{-2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{-2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(2.17)

This, together with
V_{0},V_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
Proposition
2.32
(i), further implies that
V^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
This finishes the proof of (
i
).

(
ii
), case
p\in(1,\infty)
: In this case, the assumption
V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
is equivalent to
V_{0}^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Thus, Proposition
2.32
applies to
V_{0}^{-1}
and
V_{1}^{-1}
in place of
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
to show that

(V_{0}^{-2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{-2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}),\qquad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1-\theta}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{\theta}{1}=\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{\theta}{p}.

By (
2.17
), the weight above is
V^{-1}
, and hence

V=(V_{0}^{-2}\#_{\theta}V_{1}^{-2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathscr{A}_{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}),\qquad\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=1-\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1-\theta}{p}.

Thus,
V\in A_{\frac{p}{1-\theta}}
, as claimed.

(
ii
), general case
p\in[1,\infty)
: It remains to consider
p=1
, but the argument that this requires will also provide another proof in the case
p\in(1,\infty)
. Feeding the assumption
V_{1}^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
into Corollary
2.27
, we find that
V_{1}^{-1}\in\operatorname{RHI}_{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
for some
s\in(1,\infty)
. Fixing one such
s_{0}\in(1,\infty)
, it is clear from the definition of the reverse Hölder property that
[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\operatorname{RHI}_{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\leq[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\operatorname{RHI}_{1,s_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
s\in(1,s_{0})
. Then, for all
R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\begin{split}|[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{s}(R)}[V_{1}]_{\textit{\L}^{s^{\prime}}(R)}|&\sim\bigg[\fint_{R}|V_{1}^{-1}(x)[V_{1}]_{\textit{\L}^{s^{\prime}}(R)}|^{s}dx\bigg]^{\frac{1}{s}}\\
&\lesssim\fint_{R}|V_{1}^{-1}(x)[V_{1}]_{\textit{\L}^{s^{\prime}}(R)}|dx\qquad\text{since }[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\operatorname{RHI}_{1,s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1\\
&\lesssim\fint_{R}|V_{1}^{-1}(x)[V_{1}]_{\textit{\L}^{\infty}(R)}|dx\qquad\text{by H\"{o}lder's inequality}\\
&\lesssim[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1\qquad\text{by definition and assumption}.\end{split}

Taking the supremum over
R\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
shows that
[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
, uniformly in
s\in(1,s_{0})
. (For this uniformity, it is important to observe that the implicit positive constants above depend only on the dimensions of the matrices and the quasi-triangle constants of the spaces involved; since all involved spaces
\textit{\L}^{p}(R)
, with
p\in[1,\infty]
, satisfy the usual triangle inequality with constant
1
, the latter dependence is not an issue, and we only have dependence on the dimensions of the matrices.)
By duality of the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
conditions with
p\in(1,\infty)
, the uniform estimate
[V_{1}^{-1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
is equivalent to
[V_{1}]_{\mathscr{A}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
s^{\prime}\in(s_{0}^{\prime},\infty)
.

We can hence apply Proposition
2.32
to
V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
(assumption) and
V_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
(as we just proved) to conclude that

V\in\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}),\qquad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1-\theta}{p}+\frac{\theta}{s^{\prime}},

(2.18)

with
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
uniformly in
s^{\prime}\in(s_{0}^{\prime},\infty)
, hence
r\in(r_{0},\frac{p}{1-\theta})
for some
r_{0}\in(0,\frac{p}{1-\theta})
. To conclude, we would like to be able to pass to the limit
r\to\frac{p}{1-\theta}
. The rest of the proof consists of implementing this in a precise way.

Denote
q:=\frac{p}{1-\theta}
.
For
R\in\operatorname{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, dominated convergence shows that

\begin{split}\fint_{R}\min(|V(x)[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q^{\prime}}(R)}|^{q},N)dx&=\lim_{r\to q}\fint_{R}\min(|V(x)[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q^{\prime}}(R)}|^{r},N)dx\\
&\lesssim\limsup_{r\to q}\fint_{R}|V(x)[V]_{\textit{\L}^{r^{\prime}}(R)}|^{r}dx\\
&\lesssim\limsup_{r\to q}\ [V]_{\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}^{r}\lesssim 1\end{split}

by Hölder’s inequality
(q^{\prime}<r^{\prime}
) and dropping the minimum in the first estimate, followed by the definition of
\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and the uniform bound on
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
observed after (
2.18
). Letting
N\to\infty
and using monotone convergence, we can also drop the minimum from the left-hand side to see that
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim 1
. Since
q=\frac{p}{1-\theta}
, this finishes the proof of (
ii
).
∎

Applying Proposition
2.34
with
p=1
and
\theta=\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}
,
we obtain a part of Jones factorisation theorem for multi-parameter matrix weights; we omit the details.

Corollary 2.35
.

Let
q\in(1,\infty)
,
V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
and
V_{1}^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
If
V_{0}V_{1}=V_{1}V_{0}
almost everywhere,
then
V_{0}^{\frac{1}{q}}V_{1}^{\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}}\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

In the case where
k=1
, Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
9
, Theorem 1.3]

established the full Jones factorisation theorem for matrix weights. Since we do not have any need for it here, we will not consider the multi-parameter extension.

3.
Measurable selection of reducing operators

The goal of this section is to obtain a measurability result
to justify various analytic manipulations that
we will perform with the reducing operators.
Recall the concept of
p
-seminorm from Definition
2.1
.
The following theorem is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a measure space and
p\in(0,1]
.
Suppose that

r:\Omega\times\mathbb{F}^{m}\to[0,\infty)

is a mapping such that

(i)

for every
x\in\Omega
,
the mapping
e\mapsto r(x,e)
is a
p
-seminorm;

(ii)

for every
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
the mapping
x\mapsto r(x,e)
is measurable.

Then there exists a measurable mapping
A:\ \Omega\to\mathbb{F}_{+}^{m\times m}

such that
r(x,e)\sim|A(x)e|
for every
x\in\Omega
and
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
where the positive equivalence constants depend at most on
p
and
m
.

Closely related results are due to
Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
9
, Sections 3–4]

and Domelevo et al.
[
54
, Appendix A.4]
, but they both deal with slightly
more restrictive assumptions and hence cannot be simply quoted
for our needs. The treatment of
[
54
]
discusses in detail
both
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, but only deals with proper norms,
and the fact that their closed unit balls are compact subsets of

\mathbb{F}^{m}
seems to be somewhat crucial for the argument.
The approach of
[
9
]
allows seminorms, but only deals with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.
Assuming only the
p
-triangle inequality in Theorem
3.1

is a generalisation compared to both
[
9
,
54
]
.

Our plan is to adapt the techniques of Bownik and Cruz-Uribe
[
9
]
which,
as said, are presented in the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
. Rather than modify
each intermediate result to the complex case, we begin by showing that
the real version of Theorem
3.1
already implies its complex version,
which then permits concentrating on
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
for the majority of the argument.
Some lemmas can then be directly quoted from
[
9
]
, but we
provide the necessary details for the remaining part of the argument.

Lemma 3.2
.

If Theorem
3.1
holds for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, then it also holds for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

As usual, we may identify
\mathbb{C}^{m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2m}
.
Among the properties of a
p
-seminorm, only the homogeneity

r(x,\lambda e)=|\lambda|r(x,e)
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
,
refers to the scalar field
\mathbb{F}
, and this requirement is clearly stronger for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
than for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
. Thus, under the assumptions in the
\mathbb{C}^{m}
case
of Theorem
3.1
, all the assumptions of the
\mathbb{R}^{2m}
case are satisfied, and hence also the conclusions of the
\mathbb{R}^{2m}
case
of Theorem
3.1
hold, under the assumption that Theorem
3.1
holds in this case. On the other hand, these conclusions are
a priori
weaker than the claims in the
\mathbb{C}^{m}
case: identifying
A_{\mathbb{R}}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{2m\times 2m}
with an operator on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we only obtain an
\mathbb{R}
-linear operator in general. Expressed in the complex notation, such an operator has the form

e\mapsto A_{0}(x)e+\overline{A_{1}(x)e}

for some
A_{0}(x),A_{1}(x)\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
, which are easily seen to depend measurably on
x\in\Omega
when
A_{\mathbb{R}}(x)
does. Hence the conclusion of Theorem
3.1
in the real case says that, for every
x\in\Omega
and
e\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,

r(x,e)\sim\big|A_{0}(x)e+\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|.

In particular, we may also apply this to
ie
in place of
e
, where
i=\sqrt{-1}
.
Combined with the complex homogeneity of both
r(x,\cdot)
and
|\ |
, it follows that

\displaystyle r(x,e)

\displaystyle=r(x,ie)\sim\big|A_{0}(x)ie+\overline{A_{1}(x)ie}\big|

\displaystyle=\big|i\,A_{0}(x)e-i\,\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|=\big|A_{0}(x)e-\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|.

Adding the squares of the two approximate identities for
r(x,e)
and using the parallelogram law, we further deduce that

\displaystyle 2r(x,e)^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\big|A_{0}(x)e+\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|^{2}+\big|A_{0}(x)e-\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=2\Big(|A_{0}(x)e|^{2}+\big|\overline{A_{1}(x)e}\big|^{2}\Big)=2\Big(|A_{0}(x)e|^{2}+|A_{1}(x)e|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=2\Big(\big(A_{0}(x)^{*}A_{0}(x)+A_{1}(x)^{*}A_{1}(x)\big)e,e\Big)=2|A(x)e|^{2},

where
A(x):=\sqrt{A_{0}(x)^{*}A_{0}(x)+A_{1}(x)^{*}A_{1}(x)}
.
This is the claimed conclusion of Theorem
3.1
in the
\mathbb{C}^{m}
case, with the new matrix
A(x)\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
as defined above; it is clearly positive semidefinite and depends measurably on
x\in\Omega
, when
A_{0}(x)
and
A_{1}(x)
do.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
3.2
.
∎

We then turn to the proof of Theorem
3.1
in the real case. However, the first couple of observations are elementary,
and we still state them with generic
\mathbb{F}
, as this makes no difference in the proofs.
Related to the
p
-seminorms
r(x,\cdot)
, we denote their
closed unit balls

K(x)
and their so-called
polar sets

K^{\circ}(x)
, respectively, by

K(x):=\{e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}:\ r(x,e)\leq 1\}

(3.1)

and

K^{\circ}(x):=\{u\in\mathbb{F}^{m}:\ |(u,e)|\leq 1\text{ for all }e\in K(x)\}.

(3.2)

The
interior of
K(x)
is denoted by
\operatorname{Int}K(x)
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
.
For
p
-seminorm
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}\mapsto r(x,e)
, its closed unit ball

K(x)
in (
3.1
) satisfies

(i)

\lambda K(x)=K(x)
for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
with
|\lambda|=1
;

(ii)

K(x)
is closed
and
K(x)=\overline{\operatorname{Int}K(x)}
;

(iii)

\overline{B(\mathbf{0},R(x)^{-1})}\subset K(x)

for some
R(x)\in[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

Assertion (
i
) is immediate from the homogeneity

r(x,\lambda e)=|\lambda|r(x,e)
of the
p
-seminorm.

We now prove (
iii
).
Let
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{m}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{F}^{m}
.
By the
p
-triangle inequality of
r(x,\cdot)

and Hölder’s inequality, we conclude that, for every
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

\begin{split}r(x,e)^{p}&=r\Bigg(x,\sum_{i=1}^{m}(e,e_{i})e_{i}\Bigg)^{p}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}|(e,e_{i})|^{p}r(x,e_{i})^{p}\\
&\leq\Bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{m}r(x,e_{i})^{\frac{2p}{2-p}}\Bigg]^{1-\frac{p}{2}}|e|^{p}=:R(x)^{p}|e|^{p},\end{split}

(3.3)

where each
r(x,e_{i})
, and hence
R(x)
, is some finite number.
Thus, every
e
with
|e|\leq R(x)^{-1}

satisfies
e\in K(x)
, i.e., the closed ball
\overline{B(\mathbf{0},R(x)^{-1})}

is contained in
K(x)
.
(The conclusion is also valid if
R(x)=0
with
R(x)^{-1}=\infty
.)
This proves (
iii
).

Next, we prove that
K(x)
is closed.
From the
p
-triangle inequality of
r(x,\cdot)
and (
3.3
),
it follows that, for any
e,u\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

r(x,e+u)^{p}\leq r(x,e)^{p}+r(x,u)^{p}\leq r(x,e)^{p}+R(x)^{p}|u|^{p}.

With
e+u
in place of
e
and
-u
in place of
u
, we also obtain

r(x,e)^{p}\leq r(x,e+u)^{p}+R(x)^{p}|u|^{p}

and hence

|r(x,e)^{p}-r(x,e+u)^{p}|\leq R(x)^{p}|u|^{p}.

This shows that
e\mapsto r(x,e)^{p}
, and then also
e\mapsto r(x,e)
,
is continuous, and thus
K(x)
, as the preimage of
[0,1]
, is closed.
This is the first half of (
ii
).

We finally consider the second claim in (
ii
).
If
e\in K(x)
and
t\in(0,1)
, then
r(x,te)=tr(x,e)\leq t<1
,
so each
e\in K(x)
can be arbitrarily well approximated by
elements
te\in\widetilde{K}(x):=\{e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}:\ r(x,e)<1\}
.
As a preimage of an open set, this is open, and obviously contained in
K(x)
and thus
\operatorname{Int}K(x)
. On the other hand, if
r(x,e)=1
, then
e
is arbitrarily close to
te\notin K(x)
for
t>1
, and hence
e\notin\operatorname{Int}K(x)
. Thus,
\operatorname{Int}K(x)=\widetilde{K}(x)
, and we already observed that this is dense in
K(x)
,
which completes the proof of (
ii
) and hence Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Before presenting the properties of
K^{\circ}(x)
,
we recall some basic concepts related to sets.
A set
E\subset\mathbb{F}^{m}

is said to be
symmetric
if
-E=E
,
and said to be
convex
if

tx+(1-t)y\in E
for every
x,y\in E
and
t\in(0,1)
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
p\in(0,1]
and
R(x)
be the same as in Lemma
3.3
(
iii
).
For
p
-seminorm
\vec{e}\in\mathbb{F}^{m}\mapsto r(x,\vec{e})
,
its polar set
K^{\circ}(x)
in (
3.2
) satisfies

(i)

K^{\circ}(x)
is closed and convex;

(ii)

\lambda K^{\circ}(x)=K^{\circ}(x)
for every
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}
with
|\lambda|=1
;

(iii)

\mathbf{0}\in K^{\circ}(x)
;

(iv)

K^{\circ}(x)\subset\overline{B(\mathbf{0},R(x))}
.

Proof.

First, from the definitions of closedness, convexity,
and
K^{\circ}(x)
, we immediately deduce (
i
) through (
iii
).
For (
iv
), let
u\in K^{\circ}(x)
.
Lemma
3.3
(
iii
) shows that,
for any
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
with
|e|\leq R(x)^{-1}
,
we have
e\in K(x)
, which further implies
|(u,e)|\leq 1
.
Taking the supremum over all such
e
proves that

|u|R(x)^{-1}\leq 1
and hence

u\in\overline{B(\mathbf{0},R(x))}
.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

The following useful duality statement allows us to reduce the considerations from the possibly non-convex and unbounded unit balls
K(x)
to the more favourable polar sets
K^{\circ}(x)
. This differentiates our approach from that of
[
54
, Appendix A.4]
, where the primary objects are the sets
K(x)
for the larger part of their argument.

Lemma 3.5
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
,
it follows that, for any
x\in\Omega
and
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

r(x,e)\sim\sup\{|(e,u)|:\ u\in K^{\circ}(x)\},

where the positive equivalence constants depend at most on
m
and
p
.

Proof.

Let
\operatorname{co}K(x)
be the convex hull of
K(x)
.
While not equal to
K(x)
in general,
this is still uniformly comparable to
K(x)
,
as observed in
[
65
, p. 1237]
.
We recall the short argument for the reader’s convenience.
By a classical theorem
[
21
]

(see also
[
132
, Theorem 17.1]
),
every
v\in\operatorname{co}K(x)
of
K(x)\subset\mathbb{F}^{m}
,
where
\mathbb{F}^{m}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
or
\mathbb{F}^{m}=\mathbb{C}^{m}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2m}
,
is a convex combination of at most
2m+1
vectors
v_{i}\in K(x)
.
Hence, for some convex coefficients
\alpha_{i}
,

\displaystyle r(x,v)^{p}

\displaystyle=r\Bigg(x,\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}\alpha_{i}v_{i}\Bigg)^{p}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}r(x,\alpha_{i}v_{i})^{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}\alpha_{i}^{p}r(x,v_{i})^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}\alpha_{i}^{p}\leq\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}\alpha_{i}\Bigg)^{p}\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{2m+1}1\Bigg)^{1-p}=1\times(2m+1)^{1-p}.

Thus,
r(x,v)\leq(2m+1)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}
for all
v\in\operatorname{co}K(x)
, and hence

K(x)\subset\operatorname{co}K(x)\subset(2m+1)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}K(x).

Since
K(x)
is closed by Lemma
3.3
, we can also take closures above to deduce that

K(x)\subset\overline{\operatorname{co}K(x)}\subset(2m+1)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}K(x).

It is clear from the definition that the polar sets of any sets
E\subset F
satisfy the reverse containment
E^{\circ}\supset F^{\circ}
, and thus

K^{\circ}(x)\supset\Big(\overline{\operatorname{co}K}\Big)^{\circ}(x),\ \ K^{\circ\circ}(x)\subset\Big(\overline{\operatorname{co}K}\Big)^{\circ\circ}(x).

It is also immediate from the definition that

E\subset E^{\circ\circ}
for any set,
whereas equality holds for closed, convex,
and symmetric sets by the classical bipolar theorem (see e.g.
[
132
, Theorem 14.5]
, where the definition of polar is slightly different, but is seen to coincide with the present one for symmetric sets).
The set
\overline{\operatorname{co}K}
is closed and convex by definition,
and it clearly inherits the symmetry of
K(x)
,
which is guaranteed by Lemma
3.3
. Thus,

K(x)\subset K^{\circ\circ}(x)\subset\Big(\overline{\operatorname{co}K}\Big)^{\circ\circ}(x)=\overline{\operatorname{co}K(x)}\subset(2m+1)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}K(x),

and in particular

K(x)\subset K^{\circ\circ}(x)\subset(2m+1)^{\frac{1}{p}-1}K(x).

Expressed in terms of the defining
p
-seminorm of
K(x)

and the defining property of
K^{\circ\circ}(x)=(K^{\circ})^{\circ}(x)
,
this says that, for any
e\in\mathbb{F}^{m}
,

(2m+1)^{-(\frac{1}{p}-1)}r(x,e)\leq\sup\{|(e,u)|:\ u\in K^{\circ}(x)\}\leq r(x,e).

This is a quantitative formulation of the claim that we wanted to prove, and finishes the proof of Lemma
3.5
.
∎

We then turn to the more delicate measurability issues.
To make direct use of the results from
[
9
]
,
we now concentrate on
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, keeping in mind that
this suffices for our goal of proving Theorem
3.1

in view of Lemma
3.2
. We will need
the following notion of measurability of set-valued mappings
(see, for instance,
[
2
, Definition 8.1.1]
)
and its characterization in the subsequent lemma.

Definition 3.6
.

A set-valued mapping

F:\ \Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\{\text{closed, nonempty subsets of }\mathbb{R}^{m}\}

(3.4)

on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
is said to be
measurable
if

F^{-1}(U):=\{x\in\Omega:\ U\cap F(x)\neq\varnothing\}\in\mathcal{A}

for every open subset
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Remark 3.7
.

If
F
is singleton-valued, i.e.,
F(x)=\{f(x)\}
for some
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
and every
x\in\Omega
, then
F^{-1}(U)=f^{-1}(U)
coincides with the usual definition of the preimage, and
F
is measurable in the sense of Definition
3.6
if and only if
f
is measurable in the usual sense.

In
[
74
, Definition 1.1]
, “measurable” in the sense of Definition
3.6
is called “weakly measurable”, which may be useful to note when comparing related results in different sources.

The following lemma is a part of
[
2
, Theorem 8.1.4]
.

Lemma 3.8
.

A set-valued mapping
F:\ \Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

on a measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A})
is measurable if and only if

\operatorname{Graph}(F):=\{(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ v\in F(x)\}\subset\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{m}

belongs to the product
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{A}\times\operatorname{Bor}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
,
where
\operatorname{Bor}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Now, we can show the measurability of
K^{\circ}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.1
with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, the mapping

K^{\circ}:\ \Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m}),

where
K^{\circ}
and
\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m})

are as in (
3.2
) and (
3.4
),
is measurable in the sense of Definition
3.6
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.4
, we find that
K^{\circ}

indeed takes values in
\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
.

We use the equivalent condition of Lemma
3.8

with
F
replaced by
K^{\circ}
to check measurability.
Recall that
v\in K^{\circ}(x)
if and only if,
for every
e\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

r(x,e)\leq 1\Rightarrow|(v,e)|\leq 1.

(3.5)

Since both sides of (
3.5
) are continuous in
e
,
it is equivalent to ask this condition for all
e\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}
.
Also note from elementary logic that “
A\Rightarrow B
”
if and only if “
B
or not
A
” holds.
Hence we can write

\displaystyle\{(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ v\in K^{\circ}(x)\}

\displaystyle\quad=\bigcap_{e\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}}\{(x,v)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ |(v,e)|\leq 1\text{ or }r(x,e)>1\}

\displaystyle\quad=\bigcap_{e\in\mathbb{Q}^{m}}\Big(\Big[\Omega\times\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{m}:\ |(v,e)|\leq 1\}\Big]\cup\Big[\{x\in\Omega:\ r(x,e)>1\}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\Big]\Big).

This is a very explicit product-measurable subset of
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
which completes the proof of Lemma
3.9
.
∎

The following lemma is precisely
[
9
, Theorem 3.7]
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose that
F:\ \Omega\to\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
is measurable
in the sense of Definition
3.6

and takes values in the smaller target

\mathcal{K}_{\rm{bcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\{E\in\mathcal{K}(\mathbb{R}^{m}):\ E\text{ is bounded, convex, and symmetric}\}.

Then there exists a measurable
A:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
such that,
for any
x\in\Omega
, the matrix
A(x)^{T}A(x)
is diagonal and

A(x)\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\subset F(x)\subset\sqrt{m}\,A(x)\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}},

where
\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\ |x|\leq 1\}
.

Remark 3.11
.

Measurability of
F:\Omega\to\mathcal{K}_{\rm{bcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
may be equivalently characterised by viewing the target as a separable metric space with the Hausdorff distance of sets, see
[
9
, Theorem 3.5]
, and this is the approach taken in
[
54
, Appendix A.4]
. However, they largely work with the still smaller space (in the notation of
[
9
]
)

\mathcal{K}_{\rm{abcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m}):=\{E\in\mathcal{K}_{\rm{bcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m}):\ \mathbf{0}\in\operatorname{Int}E\},

and
K^{\circ}(x)
may fail to be in
\mathcal{K}_{\rm{abcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
under the mere assumptions of our Theorem
3.1
.

We are now ready to show Theorem
3.1
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

By Lemma
3.2
,
it suffices to consider
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.

Applying Lemma
3.5
, we obtain,
for all
x\in\Omega
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

r(x,e)\sim\sup\{|(e,v)|:v\in K^{\circ}(x)\},

(3.6)

where
K^{\circ}(x)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rm{bcs}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
by Lemma
3.4
.
Thus, Lemma
3.10
guarantees the existence of a measurable

A:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
such that,
for all
x\in\Omega
,

A(x)\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\subset K^{\circ}(x)\subset\sqrt{m}\,A(x)\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}.

(3.7)

It easily follows from
the singular value decomposition that

A(x)\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}=[A(x)A(x)^{T}]^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}
,
so replacing
A(x)
by
[A(x)A(x)^{T}]^{\frac{1}{2}}
if necessary,
we may assume that
A(x)
is nonnegative definite.
Now (
3.7
) implies that,
for all
x\in\Omega
and
e\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\displaystyle\sup\{|(e,v)|:\ v\in K^{\circ}(x)\}

\displaystyle\sim\sup\Big\{|(e,A(x)u)|:\ u\in\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\Big\}

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{|(A(x)e,u)|:\ u\in\overline{B_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}}\Big\}=|A(x)e|.

This, together with (
3.6
),
finishes the proof of Theorem
3.1
.
∎

4.
Maximal and Carleson embedding-type inequalities

The Hardy–Littlewood maximal inequality and its vector-valued extension—the Fefferman–Stein maximal inequality
[
58
]
—have been standard tools in the theory of Triebel–Lizorkin spaces for a long time. In the matrix-weighted theory of these spaces, a more recent addition to this toolbox consists of a group of estimates presented by Frazier and Roudenko
[
67
, Theorem 3.7]
and attributed by them to Fedor Nazarov; these also played a key role in the subsequent studies of the more general matrix-weighted Triebel–Lizorkin-
type
spaces in
[
12
,
15
]
. These estimates may be vaguely describes as
Carleson embedding-type
, due their similarity with the classical Carleson embedding theorem
[
22
]
. (While the name Carleson is not explicitly mentioned in
[
67
]
, the suggestive notation
\|\ \|_{\mathcal{C}}
is used for a key quantity featuring in their estimates; see
[
67
, Lemma 3.6]
.)
The aim of this section is to develop appropriate multi-parameter extensions of these tools, which will serve a similar role as their one-parameter versions in our theory of the multi-parameter Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces in the subsequent sections.

The structure of this section is as follows.
In Subsection
4.1
, we study the (rescaled) strong maximal operator in the iterated mixed-norm
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
spaces, describing its boundedness in terms of the notion of
admissible permutations
of the component space
L^{p_{i}}
and
\ell^{q_{i}}
; this will place restrictions on the scales of the general Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin spaces that are covered by our theory. While the sufficiency part of the description is a straightforward application of known results and some book-keeping of indices, we also present in Subsection
4.2
a full set of counterexamples to show the necessity of all imposed restrictions.

In Subsection
4.3
, we obtain sufficient conditions for pointwise multiplier sequences
\{\gamma_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
to satisfy the multi-parameter Carleson embedding-type inequality

\big\|\{\gamma_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\lesssim\big\|\{f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}},

(4.1)

and in Subsection
4.4
we prove the sharpness of these conditions.

In Subsection
4.5
, we specialise the considerations to particular multipliers
\gamma_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
that arise from matrix-weighted considerations. For these special multipliers, our sufficient conditions for (
4.1
) take a form that is similar to but formally stronger than the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
condition, and we refer to this new condition as
\mathscr{A}_{p}^{\mathrm{Carl}}
. In the one-parameter case, the identity
\mathscr{A}_{p}^{\mathrm{Carl}}=\mathscr{A}_{p}
follows from the boundedness of the Christ–Goldberg maximal operator
[
29
,
73
]
. For the general multi-parameter case, this suggests a need of a multi-parameter extension of the Christ–Goldberg maximal theorem. Such a result is recently due to Vuorinen
[
151
]
, and could be directly used for our needs when
p\in(1,\infty)
. However, as usual, we cannot expect maximal function bounds for
p\in(0,1]
unless some rescaling is applied, and this required some care in the matrix-weighted setting.

In Subsection
4.6
, we define and study a new reduced-operator-valued maximal function, which circumvents the above-mentioned problems, and provides an approach to prove the required inequalities in the full range
p\in(0,\infty)
. Interestingly, this also gives an alternative proof the result of
[
151
]
for
p\in(1,\infty)
: while
[
151
]
makes use of the convex-set-valued maximal operator of
[
9
]
as a linearising device in the intermediate steps, our approach via the reduced-operator-valued maximal function avoids this set-valued analysis. While the preference of one over the other may be a matter of taste, an objective advantage of our new approach is covering estimates in the full range
p\in(0,\infty)
.

In the final Subsection
4.7
of this section, we combine the results of Subsections
4.3
through
4.6
to show that the Carleson embedding-type inequalities (
4.1
) indeed hold for the particular choices of
\gamma_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
arising from matrix weights, as needed in the subsequent applications to weighted Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin type spaces.

4.1.
Maximal inequalities

For a function of several variables, we denote by
\mathcal{M}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}_{z}
) the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator acting with respect to the
i
-th variable (resp. the variable
z
),
whichever notation seems more convenient in a particular context.
The following proposition is a generalization of
the Fefferman–Stein vector-valued maximal inequality.

For a vector of exponents
\vec{q}=(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
the iterated
L^{\vec{q}}
space is defined recursively as

\begin{split}L^{(q_{1})}&:=L^{q_{1}}\qquad\text{(the usual $L^{q_{1}}$ space of scalar-valued functions)},\\
L^{(q_{1},q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}&:=L^{q_{1}}(L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})})\quad\text{(the $L^{q_{1}}$ space of $L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}$-valued functions)},\quad k\geq 2.\end{split}

It will be convenient to have an interpretation of the previous recursive formula also for
k=1
. For this purpose, corresponding to the empty string of exponents, we define

L^{\varnothing}:=\mathbb{F},\qquad\text{hence}\qquad L^{(q_{1})}=L^{q_{1}}=L^{q_{1}}(\mathbb{F})=L^{q_{1}}(L^{\varnothing}),

which indeed extends the recursive formula to
k=1
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
w\in A_{p}

(the classical, scalar-valued Muckenhoupt class).
Let
\vec{q}\in(1,\infty)^{k}\times\{\infty\}^{l}
for some
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,
and
L^{\vec{q}}
be the iteration of
L^{q_{i}}(\Omega_{i})
spaces
over arbitrary
\sigma
-finite measure spaces
\Omega_{i}
.
Then the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{x}
is bounded on
L^{p}_{x}(w)L^{\vec{q}}_{y}
.

For our immediate needs, the point of stating this with abstract spaces
\Omega_{i}
is simply to cover both
L^{q_{i}}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})
and
\ell^{q_{i}}=L^{q_{i}}(\mathbb{Z}^{n_{i}})
at the same time. This result is very well known in the special case that
L^{\vec{q}}=\ell^{q}
is a usual (non-iterated) sequence space; this can be found e.g. in
[
36
, page 62]
or
[
85
, Theorem 8.2.6]
. The version as stated is certainly known to experts, and can be proved by iterating the same argument. Since we were not able to find this explicitly stated, we provide a proof for completeness.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Let first
l=0
, i.e., there is no
L^{\infty}
component in
\ell^{\vec{q}}
.

For
k=0
, i.e.,
L^{\vec{q}}=L^{\varnothing}=\mathbb{F}
according to our convention, this is the classical Muckenhoupt theorem about the boundedness of the maximal operator on
L^{p}(w)
for all
w\in A_{p}
(see, for instance,
[
85
, Theorem J.1.1]
). Assume for induction that we already know the said boundedness on
L^{r}(w;L^{\vec{q}})
, whenever
r\in(1,\infty)
,
w\in A_{r}
, and
L^{\vec{q}}
is an iterated
L^{q}
space with
\vec{q}=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
of some fixed length
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Given a function
g
of one more variable
z
, we apply the induction assumption to
(x,y)\mapsto g(x,z,y)
for each fixed
z
and take the
L^{r}
norms with respect to
z
. This shows that

\|\mathcal{M}_{x}g\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{r}_{x}(w)L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{r}_{x}(w)L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}

and hence, by Fubini’s theorem, we obtain

\Big\|x\mapsto\|\mathcal{M}_{x}g(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}\Big\|_{L^{r}(w)}\lesssim\Big\|x\mapsto\|g(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}\Big\|_{L^{r}_{x}(w)}.

(4.2)

This holds for all
w\in A_{r}
. Hence the pair of functions

(f(x),h(x)):=\Big(\|g(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{\vec{q}}_{y}},\|\mathcal{M}_{x}g(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{r}_{z}L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}\Big)

satisfy the assumptions of (the operator-free formulation of) Rubio de Francia’s extrapolation theorem (see, for instance,
[
85
, Theorem J.2.1]
), which shows that the same estimate (
4.2
) remains valid if we replace
L^{r}(w)
by
L^{p}(w)
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
and
w\in A_{p}
. But this is precisely the boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
on
L^{p}(w)L^{r}L^{\vec{q}}=L^{p}(w)L^{(r,\vec{q})}
, where we have increased the length of the vector
(r,\vec{q})
by one from the previous step.
By induction, we complete the proof of Proposition
4.1
for
l=0
.

Let then
l\geq 1
. We write
\vec{q}=(\vec{r},\infty)
, where
\vec{r}\in(1,\infty)^{k}
; hence
L^{\vec{q}}=L^{\vec{r}}L^{\infty}
. We write a function
f\in L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{q}}=L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{r}}L^{\infty}
as
f(x,y,z)
. Now

|f(x,y,z)|\leq\|f(x,y,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}}=:g(x,y),\qquad\mathcal{M}_{x}f(x,y,z)\leq\mathcal{M}_{x}g(x,y).

Hence
\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f(x,y,z)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq\mathcal{M}_{x}g(x,y)
and thus

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{q}}}&=\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{r}}L^{\infty}}\leq\|\mathcal{M}_{x}g\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{r}}}\\
&\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{r}}}\quad\text{by case $l=0$ that we already proved}\\
&=\|f\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{r}}L^{\infty}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(w)L^{\vec{q}}}.\end{split}

This completes the proof of Proposition
4.1
also for
l\geq 1
.
∎

In Proposition
4.1
, the ranges of
p
and
\vec{q}
are sharp in the following sense.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty]
and
\vec{q}:=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\in(0,\infty]^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
Let
L^{\vec{q}}
be
the iteration of
L^{q_{i}}(\Omega_{i})
spaces
over arbitrary
\sigma
-finite measure spaces
\Omega_{i}
.
If further we assume that,
for all
i
, there exists a countable partition of
\Omega_{i}

into sets of equal measure,
then the Hardy–Littlewood maximal operator
\mathcal{M}_{x}

is bounded on
L^{p}_{x}L^{\vec{q}}_{y}
if and only if

(p,\vec{q})\in(1,\infty)^{l}\times\{\infty\}^{k+1-l}\text{ for some }l\in\{0,\ldots,k+1\}.

(4.3)

Proof of the sufficiency part of Theorem
4.2
.

If
p\in(1,\infty)
and
\vec{q}\in(1,\infty]^{k}
,
then the boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
on
L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\vec{q}}_{y}

follows from Proposition
4.1
with
w\equiv 1
.
If
p=q_{1}=\cdots=q_{k}=\infty
, note that

\mathop{\operatorname{ess\,sup}}_{y\in\Omega_{1}\times\cdots\times\Omega_{k}}\mathcal{M}f(\cdot,y)\leq\mathcal{M}\Bigg(\mathop{\operatorname{ess\,sup}}_{y\in\Omega_{1}\times\cdots\times\Omega_{k}}|f(\cdot,y)|\Bigg),

and the boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
on
L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\vec{q}}_{y}
follows from the boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{x}
on
L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
This finishes the proof of the sufficiency part of Theorem
4.2
.
∎

Since the proof of the necessity part is lengthy,
we place it in the next subsection.

Remark 4.3
.

Some special cases of Theorem
4.2
have already been studied.

(i)

It is classical that
\mathcal{M}
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

if and only if
p\in(1,\infty]
.

(ii)

\mathcal{M}_{x}
is not bounded on
L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\ell^{2}
(see
[
69
, Remark 2.9]
).

(iii)

\mathcal{M}_{x}
is not bounded on
L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\ell^{1}
for any
p\in(1,\infty)

(see
[
69
, Proposition 2.4]
or
[
139
, p. 75, Section 5.1]
).

(iv)

\mathcal{M}_{x}
is not bounded on
L^{2}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\ell^{\infty}\ell^{2}

(see
[
148
, Proposition 8.1]
).
One of the authors (T.H.) would like to thank
Emiel Lorist for informing him about this result.

We will also need versions of Theorem
4.2
,
where some of the exponents may be outside the range
(1,\infty]
.
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
the set of all permutations of
[k]
is denoted by
S_{[k]}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
(p_{j})_{j=1}^{k+l}=(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty]^{k}\times(0,\infty]^{l}
,
and
\pi\in S_{[k+l]}
.
Fix some
i\in[k+l]
such that
\pi(i)\in[k]
,
and suppose that for some
h\in\{0,\ldots,k+l-i+1\}

(p_{\pi(j)})_{j=i}^{k+l}\in(1,\infty)^{(k+l-i+1)-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h},

(4.4)

i.e., all these values are in
(1,\infty]
, and all infinite values, if any, are at the end of the sequence.
Then there exists a positive constant
C
,
which depends only on
\vec{p}
,
\vec{q}
, and
\vec{n}
, such that,
for every
f\in(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
,

\|\mathcal{M}_{\pi(i)}f\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\leq C\|f\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}.

Proof.

The claimed inequality can be spelled out as

\|\mathcal{M}_{y}f(x,y,z)\|_{X(dx,L^{\pi(i)}(dy,Z(dz)))}\lesssim\|f(x,y,z)\|_{X(dx,L^{\pi(i)}(dy,Z(dz)))},

where
X
and
Z
are iterated
L^{p}\ell^{q}
spaces consisting of the first
i-1
and the last
k+l-i
factors of
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
, respectively, and
\mathcal{M}_{y}
is the maximal operator with respect to the variable
y
. [If
i=1
or
i=k+l
, we interpret
X(dx,L^{\pi(i)}(dy,Z(dz)))
as
L^{\pi(i)}(dy,Z(dz))
or
X(dx,L^{\pi(i)}(dy))
, respectively, and omit the respective variable
x
or
z
inside the quasi-norm.]

From this we observe that it suffices to prove the boundedness of
\mathcal{M}_{y}
on
L^{\pi(i)}(dy,Z(dz))
, as the claimed inequality then follows by applying this boundedness to
(y,z)\mapsto f(x,y,z)
for each
x
, and taking the quasi-norm in
X(dx)
of both sides.
Indeed, this boundedness is a special case of Theorem
4.2
,
which completes the proof of Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(p_{i})_{i=1}^{2k}=(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty]^{k}\times(0,\infty]^{k}
and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
.
Let

\mu(\pi):=\min\{i\in[2k]:\ \pi(i)\in[k]\},

(4.5)

and suppose that assumption (
4.4
) of Lemma
4.4
is satisfied with
l=k
and
i=\mu(\pi)
.
Then there exists a positive constant
C
,
depending only on
\vec{p}
,
\vec{q}
, and
\vec{n}
, such that,
for any
f\in(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
,

\|\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}f\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\leq C\|f\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}.

Proof.

It is well known and straightforward that

\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}f\leq\Bigg(\prod_{i\in[2k]:\,\pi(i)\in[k]}\mathcal{M}_{\pi(i)}\Bigg)f,

where the products are understood as compositions of operators. If assumption (
4.4
) of Lemma
4.4
is satisfied with
i=\mu(\pi)
, it follows that it is satisfied with every
i\in[2k]
such that
\pi(i)\in[k]
. Thus, by repeated applications of Lemma
4.4
, we can extract each of the maximal operators
\mathcal{M}_{\pi(i)}
from the norm, arriving at the claimed conclusion.
∎

Definition 4.6
.

We say that
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
is an
admissible permutation
for
(p_{i})_{i=1}^{2k}=(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty]^{k}\times(0,\infty]^{k}
, if the following condition holds:

(p_{\pi(j)})_{j=\mu(\pi)}^{2k}\in(0,\infty)^{(2k-\mu(\pi)+1)-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h}

(4.6)

for some
h\in\{0,1,\ldots,2k-\mu(\pi)+1\}
, where
\mu(\pi)
is defined in (
4.5
); i.e., all infinite values of the above sequence, if any, are at the end of the sequence.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be a permutation for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty]^{2k}
.
Then
\pi
is an admissible permutation if and only if

\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
(L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}})_{\pi}

for some
a\in(0,\infty)
.
Moreover, if
\pi
is an admissible permutation, then,
for every
a\in(0,r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q}))
,

\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
(L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}})_{\pi}
, where

r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q}):=\min\{p_{\pi(i)}:\ i\in\{\mu(\pi),\ldots,2k\}\}

(4.7)

with
\mu(\pi)
defined as in (
4.5
).

Proof.

We first prove “
\Longrightarrow
”.
Since admissibility depends on the position of infinities only,
\pi
is also admissible for
(\vec{p}/a,\vec{q}/a)
. From the defining property of admissibility, (
4.6
), and the definition of
r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})>a>0
, it follows that

(p_{\pi(j)}/a)_{j=\mu(\pi)}^{2k}\in(1,\infty)^{(2k-\mu(\pi)+1)-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h}

for some
h\in\{0,1,\ldots,2k-\mu(\pi)+1\}
. But this is precisely condition (
4.4
) of Lemma
4.4
for
l=k
,
i=\pi(\mu)
, and
(\vec{p}/a,\vec{q}/a)
in place of
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
. This, in turn, is the assumption of Lemma
4.5
with
(\vec{p}/a,\vec{q}/a)
in place of
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
, while the claim of the proposition is also the same as the claim of the lemma with this substitution. Hence “
\Longrightarrow
” follows from Lemma
4.5
.

Next, we show “
\Longleftarrow
”.
By Theorem
4.2
, we find that
if
\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
(L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}})_{\pi}

for some
a\in(0,\infty)
, then
(p_{\pi(j)}/a)_{j=\mu(\pi)}^{2k}\in(1,\infty)^{2k-\mu(\pi)+1-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h}

for some
h\in\{0,\ldots,2k-\mu(\pi)+1\}
.
This further implies that
\pi
is an admissible permutation,
which completes the proof of Theorem
4.7
.
∎

To better understand Theorem
4.7
, we present two simple corollaries and omit the details.
The first is the boundedness of the strong maximal operator on mixed
Lebesgue spaces.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
.
Then
\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}

for some
a\in(0,\infty)
if and only if

\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h}\quad\text{for some}\quad h\in\{0,\ldots,k\}.

(4.8)

Moreover, if (
4.8
) holds, then

\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}

for all
a\in(0,\min\vec{p})
.

Remark 4.9
.

In
[
130
, Theorem 1.4]
, Nogayama gave a version of Corollary
4.8
for Morrey spaces.
However, Ferenc Weisz has pointed out that the range of indices in
[
130
, Theorem 1.4]
is not correct (see
[
81
, Remark 3.3]
).

The second is the multi-parameter Fefferman–Stein vector-valued inequality.

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty]^{2k}
.
Then
\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}
is bounded on
L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}}

for some
a\in(0,\infty)
, that is,
there exists a positive constant
C
such that, for every sequence
\{f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\in L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}}
,

\big\|\{\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}}}\leq C\big\|\{f_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{L^{\frac{\vec{p}}{a}}\ell^{\frac{\vec{q}}{a}}},

(4.9)

if and only if

(\vec{p},\vec{q})\in(0,\infty)^{2k-h}\times\{\infty\}^{h}\quad\text{for some}\quad h\in\{0,\ldots,2k\}.

(4.10)

Moreover, if (
4.10
) holds, then
(
4.9
) holds for all
a\in(0,\min(\vec{p},\vec{q}))
.

Example 4.11
.

With
k=1
, consider
(p,q)\in(0,\infty]^{2}
. Now
S_{[2]}
consists of two permutations,
F:=\mathrm{id}
and
B:=(1\ 2)
, where the notation is suggestive of the fact that Triebel–Lizorkin spaces are defined in terms of an
(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{F}=L^{p}\ell^{q}
quasi-norm, and Besov spaces in terms of an
(L^{p}\ell^{q})_{B}=\ell^{q}L^{p}
quasi-norm.

For
\pi=F
, condition (
4.6
) says: if
p=\infty
, then
q=\infty
. For
\pi=B
, condition (
4.6
) is a tautology. In other words, the only cases that are
not
admissible are when
\pi=F
,
p=\infty
, and
q<\infty
. This reflects the fact that Triebel–Lizorkin spaces with
p=\infty
and
q<\infty
require a different definition from all other Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, which are “admissible” for a simpler definition.

Since the main focus of this article is on weighted spaces,
we will mainly deal with the case when
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
and
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
. In this case,
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
is admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
if and only if
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
looks like

\ell^{\vec{u}}(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{v}})_{\sigma}\ell^{\vec{\infty}},

where

(i)

(\vec{u},\vec{v},\vec{\infty})
is a permutation of the vector
\vec{q}
,

(ii)

\vec{u}\in(0,\infty]^{a}
may have an arbitrary mixture of finite and infinite values,

(iii)

\vec{v}\in(0,\infty)^{b}
has finite values only,
\sigma\in S_{k+b}
, and

(iv)

\vec{\infty}=\{\infty\}^{c}
has infinite values only.

Here
a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}
and
a+b+c=k
; if any of these values is zero, the corresponding component is understood to be missing from the space
\ell^{\vec{u}}(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{v}})_{\sigma}\ell^{\vec{\infty}}
.

Thus, infinite values can appear before the first
L^{p}
(where they may alternate with finite values), and as a pure sequence of infinities in the end, necessarily after the last
L^{p}
.

4.2.
The necessity of admissible permutations

In this subsection, building on ideas from several earlier works
(see
[
69
,
148
,
139
]
), we prove
the necessity part of Theorem
4.2
.

Proof of the necessity part of Theorem
4.2
.

Assume that
\mathcal{M}_{x}
is bounded on
L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\vec{q}}_{y}
.
We first prove that
(p,\vec{q})\in(1,\infty]^{k+1}
.
Let
g\in L^{\vec{q}}
satisfy
\|g\|_{L^{\vec{q}}}=1
.
For every
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\mathcal{M}_{x}(f(x)g(y))\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}\lesssim\|f(x)g(y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\vec{q}}_{y}}=\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

and hence
p\in(1,\infty]
.

Let
g\in L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}
satisfy
\|g\|_{L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}}=1
.
For every
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}(\Omega_{1})
,

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f(x,y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}&=\|\mathcal{M}_{x}(f(x,y)g(z))\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}_{z}}\\
&\lesssim\|f(x,y)g(z)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{(q_{2},\ldots,q_{k})}_{z}}=\|f(x,y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}.\end{split}

(4.11)

By the assumptions of the present theorem, we find that there exists a sequence
of pairwise disjoint subsets
\{F_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
\Omega_{1}
with equal positive measure.
For every
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
y\in\Omega_{1}
, let

f_{N}(x,y):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E_{j}}(x)\mathbf{1}_{F_{j}}(y),

where, for all
j\in[N]
,

E_{j}:=\Big(\tfrac{j-1}{N},\tfrac{j}{N}\Big]\times(0,1]^{n-1}.

Then

\|f_{N}(x,y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}=\mu_{1}(F_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\|\mathbf{1}_{(0,1]^{n}}\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\mu_{1}(F_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}.

By this and (
4.11
), we find that

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f_{N}(x,y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}<\infty.

(4.12)

Next, we prove that (
4.12
) fails
to hold if
q_{1}\in(0,1]
.
Note that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f_{N}(x,y)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\|\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{1}(F_{j})[\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{E_{j}})]^{q_{1}}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}(F_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\Bigg\|\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{N}[\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{E_{j}})]^{q_{1}}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Here, for any
l\in[N]
and

x\in(\frac{l-1}{N},\frac{l}{N}]\times(0,1]^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{E_{j}})(x)\gtrsim\frac{1}{|l-j|+1}

and hence, for any
x\in(0,1]^{n}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}[\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{E_{j}})(x)]^{q_{1}}\gtrsim\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\frac{1}{(i+1)^{q_{1}}}\sim\begin{cases}\log(N+1),&\text{if }q_{1}=1,\\
N^{1-q_{1}},&\text{if }q_{1}\in(0,1),\end{cases}

where the implicit positive constants depend only on
n
and
q
.
Therefore, (
4.12
) fails to hold if
q_{1}\in(0,1]
;
hence
q_{1}\in(1,\infty]
.
By repeating the proof of
q_{1}\in(1,\infty]
with
some slight modifications, we obtain
q_{l}\in(1,\infty]

for all
l\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
.
This finishes the proof of
(p,\vec{q})\in(1,\infty]^{k+1}
.

To finish the proof of (
4.3
), it remains to argue that any components of
(p,\vec{q})
equal to
\infty
must be the last entries of the vector.
To this end, we need to show the following two claims:

(i)

If
p=\infty
, then
q_{1}=\cdots=q_{k}=\infty
.

(ii)

For every
l\in[k-1]
,
if
p\in(1,\infty)
and
q_{l}=\infty
,
then
q_{l+1}=\infty
.

We first prove (
i
).
If
p=\infty
, we proceed by contradiction
to show
q_{1}=\cdots=q_{k}=\infty
.
Suppose, on the contrary, that
q_{1}\in(1,\infty)
.
For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
y\in\Omega_{1}
, let

\widetilde{f}_{N}(x,y):=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{E}_{j}}(x)\mathbf{1}_{F_{j}}(y),

where, for every
j\in[N]
,

\widetilde{E}_{j}:=\Big(2^{-j},2^{-j+1}\Big]\times(0,1]^{n-1}.

Then

\Big\|\widetilde{f}_{N}(x,y)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}=[\mu_{1}(F_{1})]^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\|\mathbf{1}_{(2^{-N},1]\times(0,1]^{n-1}}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\mu_{1}(F_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}.

By this and (
4.11
), we find that

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Big\|\mathcal{M}_{x}\widetilde{f}_{N}(x,y)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}<\infty.

(4.13)

Note that, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

j\in[N]
, and
x\in(0,2^{-N}]\times(0,1]^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{E}_{j}})(x)\geq\fint_{(0,2^{-j+1}]\times I_{n-1}}\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{E}_{j}}(z)\,dz=\frac{1}{2},

where cube
I_{n-1}\subset(0,1]^{n-1}

satisfies
\ell(I_{n-1})=2^{-j+1}

and
x\in(0,2^{-N}]\times I_{n-1}
.
Then, for any
x\in(0,2^{-N}]\times(0,1]^{n-1}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}[\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{E}_{j}})(x)]^{q_{1}}\geq N\frac{1}{2^{q_{1}}},

which further implies that

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathcal{M}_{x}\widetilde{f}_{N}(x,y)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\|\Bigg[\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mu_{1}(F_{j})[\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{E}_{j}})]^{q_{1}}\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{1}(F_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}N^{\frac{1}{q_{1}}}\frac{1}{2}.

This contradicts (
4.13
); hence
q_{1}=\infty
.
By repeating the proof of
q_{1}=\infty
with
some slight modifications, we obtain
q_{l}=\infty
for all
l\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
.
This finishes the proof of (
i
).

Next, we show (
ii
).
Without loss of generality, we may assume
l=1
.
In this case,
p\in(1,\infty)
and
q_{1}=\infty
,
we proceed by contradiction to show
q_{2}=\infty
.
Suppose, on the contrary, that
q_{2}\in(1,\infty)
.
Repeating the argument used in the proof of (
4.11
)
with some slight modifications, we obtain,
for every
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}(\Omega_{2})
,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{M}_{x}f(x,y,z)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}_{z}(\Omega_{2})}\lesssim\|f(x,y,z)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{q_{1}}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}_{z}(\Omega_{2})}.

(4.14)

By the assumptions of the present theorem,
we find that there exists a sequence
of pairwise disjoint subsets
\{G_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
\Omega_{2}

with equal positive measure.
For every
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

y\in\Omega_{1}
, and
z\in\Omega_{2}
, let

g_{N}(x,y,z):=\mathbf{1}_{[0,1)^{n}}(x)\sum_{j\in[2^{N}-1]}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E_{j,i}}(x)\mathbf{1}_{F_{j}}(y)\mathbf{1}_{G_{i}}(z),

where, for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
and

i\in[N]
, let

E_{j,i}:=\Big(2^{-i}+j\cdot 2^{-N},2^{-i+1}+j\cdot 2^{-N}\Big]\times(0,1]^{n-1}.

Then, for all
x\in(0,1]^{n}
,

\displaystyle\|g_{N}(x,y,z)\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}_{z}(\Omega_{2})}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}(G_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}\Bigg\|\Bigg[\sum_{j\in[2^{N}-1]}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E_{j,i}}(x)\mathbf{1}_{F_{j}}(y)\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}\Bigg\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}(G_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}\Bigg[\sup_{j\in[2^{N}-1]}\mathbf{1}_{(2^{-N},1]}(x_{1}-j\cdot 2^{-N})\Bigg]^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}=\mu_{2}(G_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}},

and hence (noting that
g_{N}
has
x
-support on
(0,1]^{n}
),

\|g_{N}(x,y,z)\|_{L^{p}_{x}(\mathbb{R}^{n})L^{\infty}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}_{z}(\Omega_{2})}=\mu_{2}(G_{1})^{\frac{1}{q_{2}}}.

With (
4.14
), this implies that

\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\|\mathcal{M}_{x}g_{N}(x,y,z)\|_{L^{p}_{x}L^{\infty}_{y}(\Omega_{1})L^{q_{2}}_{z}(\Omega_{2})}<\infty.

(4.15)

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
j\in[2^{N}-1]
be fixed.
For every
i\in[N]
and
x\in(j\cdot 2^{-N},(j+1)\cdot 2^{-N}]\times(0,1]^{n-1}
, let

Q_{x}:=(x_{1}-2^{-i+1},x_{1}+2^{-i+1}]\times I_{n-1},

where
I_{n-1}\subset(0,1]^{n-1}
is a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}

such that
Q_{x}\subset(0,1]^{n}
is a cube in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
x\in Q_{x}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\Big(2^{-i}+j\cdot 2^{-N},2^{-i+1}+j\cdot 2^{-N}\Big]

\displaystyle\subset((j+1)\cdot 2^{-N}-2^{-i+1},j\cdot 2^{-N}+2^{-i+1}]

\displaystyle\subset(x_{1}-2^{-i+1},x_{1}+2^{-i+1}].

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{1}_{E_{j,i}})(x)\geq\fint_{Q_{x}}\mathbf{1}_{E_{j,i}}(y)\,dy=\frac{2^{-i}|I_{n-1}|}{2^{-i+2}|I_{n-1}|}=\frac{1}{4}
