and Proposition
4.40
would give us in Lemma
7.7
.

From the two Lemmas
7.5
and
7.7
, we immediately obtain the boundedness of almost diagonal operators on the spaces
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
with
\tau=0
.

Theorem 7.10
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
, and
V
be a matrix weight such that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
).
Let
B
be
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal with

\vec{D}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r},\quad\vec{E}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{s},\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s},\qquad r:=r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\wedge 1

(7.6)

with
r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})
as in (
4.7
).
If
t\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, then
Bt
is well defined and

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
t
.

Proof.

By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to consider
\vec{s}=\vec{0}
. If
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
satisfies (
7.6
), then we can choose
a\in(0,r)
close enough to
r
so that
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
still satisfies

\vec{D}>\frac{\vec{n}}{a},\quad\vec{E}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2},\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{a}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}.

(7.7)

Let us denote by
\mathfrak{R}
the right-hand side of (
7.4
) with these parameters and
H_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\equiv V
. From the convergence of geometric series in (
7.4
) with parameters (
7.7
), it is immediate that

\mathfrak{R}\lesssim\sup_{\vec{h}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k},\vec{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\bigg\|\bigg\{\bigg[\fint_{C(\cdot 2^{\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}})}|V(\cdot)t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(y)|^{a}dy\bigg]^{\frac{1}{a}}\bigg\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\bigg\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}=:\mathfrak{S}.

Now Lemma
7.7
says that

\mathfrak{S}\lesssim\|Vt\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}L^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}=\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}.

Recall that Lemma
7.5
works under the condition that
\mathfrak{R}<\infty
. Since we have just verified this condition, Lemma
7.5
implies that
Bt
is well defined and satisfies

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}=\|V(Bt)\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\lesssim\mathfrak{R}.

A combination of the last three estimates shows that

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\lesssim\mathfrak{R}\lesssim\mathfrak{S}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)},

which concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 7.11
.

Theorem
7.10
reproduces
[
71
, Theorem 5.2]
in the unweighted case
V\equiv 1
.
Moreover, it extends
[
71
, Theorem 5.2]
to the matrix-
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-weighted situation
with exactly the same assumptions on the almost diagonal parameters as in the unweighted case.
Even in the one-parameter case, the fact that matrix-
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-weighed estimates
can be obtained with the same almost diagonal assumptions as in
the unweighted case was only recently observed in
[
13
]
,
while earlier results in the matrix-weighted Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
[
65
,
67
,
133
]

required almost diagonal assumptions with additional dependence on the properties of the weight.
In the multi-parameter context, the matrix-weighted estimates of Theorem
7.10
are completely new.

7.3.
Case
\tau\in(0,\infty)

The extension of Theorem
7.10
to
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
with
\tau\in(0,\infty)
requires some additional work. At the present, our understanding of this situation is not as complete as in the one-parameter theory
[
13
]
. We state and prove the following result, noting however that there should be a better version yet to be discovered; see Remark
7.13
below.

Theorem 7.12
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\tau\in[0,\infty)
,
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
, and
V
be a matrix weight such that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
).
Let
B
be
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal with

\vec{D}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{E}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{n}\tau+\vec{s},\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s},

(7.8)

where
r:=r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\wedge 1
.
If
t\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, then
Bt
is well defined, and

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)},

where the implicit positive constant is independent of
t
.

Proof.

By Remark
7.4
, it suffices to consider
\vec{s}=\vec{0}
.
As in the proof of Theorem
7.10
, the idea is to estimate

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}
by Lemma
7.5
, and then use
Lemma
7.7
to estimate each of the terms on the right-hand side
of (
7.4
) of Lemma
7.5
. The main difference
compared to Theorem
7.10
is that the quasi-norm of
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)

for
\tau>0
is no longer just the
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}
quasi-norm,
but involves an additional supremum over the choice of a truncating set
\Omega
.

To obtain the relevant quasi-norms of
Bt
on the left-hand side of (
7.4
), for a given
\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, we now choose
H_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}:=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}V
. For a fixed
\Omega
, these
H_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
are no longer a.e. invertible; however, for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
and every
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we can choose
\Omega
so that
H_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(x)
is invertible, and this is enough for the qualitative conclusion that
Bt
is well defined, as one can check by inspection of
[
13
, Remark 4.5]
. On the right-hand side of (
7.4
), we will then have expressions of the form

\bigg\|\bigg\{\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}}(\cdot)\bigg[\fint_{C(\cdot,2^{\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}})}|V(\cdot)t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(y)|^{a}dy\bigg]^{\frac{1}{a}}\bigg\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\bigg\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}.

(7.9)

We also wish to insert an indicator as a multiplier of
t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(y)
. If
x=(x_{\nu})_{\nu=1}^{k}\in\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}
, it means by definition that
x\in R\subset\Omega
for some
R=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}R_{\nu}\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Then suppose that

\displaystyle y\in C(x,2^{\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}})

\displaystyle=C(x,2^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})+(\vec{h}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}})})=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}B(x_{\nu},2^{l_{\nu}+(h_{\nu})_{-}}\ell(R_{\nu}))

\displaystyle\subset\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}3\cdot R_{\nu}^{(l_{\nu}+(h_{\nu})_{-})}=3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})},

where
R_{\nu}^{(l_{\nu}+(h_{\nu})_{-})}
is the
(l_{\nu}+(h_{\nu})_{-})
generations older dyadic ancestor of
R_{\nu}
.

Note that both
R\subset\Omega
and
R\subset 3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}
, and hence

\mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}(y)\gtrsim\fint_{3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}(z)dz=\frac{|(3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})})\cap\Omega|}{|3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}|}\geq\frac{|R|}{|3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}|}\sim 2^{-(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}.

Thus,

y\in 3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}:=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}:\ \mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}(y)>c2^{-(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}\}

for some dimensional constant
c>0
.

Recalling that
R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, it follows that
R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}\in\mathscr{D}_{(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h})-(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})=\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{l}-\vec{h}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, and the cube
3\cdot R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})}
is a union of
3^{n}
dyadic rectangles
S\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{l}-\vec{h}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, namely
S=R^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+})}
and all its neighbours. Since these
S\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{l}-\vec{h}_{+}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
satisfy
S\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
, they also satisfy
S\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{l}-\vec{h}_{+}}
; since
y
belongs to one of these
S
,
it follows that
y\in\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{l}-\vec{h}_{+}}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}
, since
\vec{l},\vec{h}_{+}\geq\vec{0}
.

Thus, we may insert the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}(y)
in the integral in (
7.9
) without changing its value. Once we have done this, we estimate up by dropping the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}}(\cdot)
outside the integral. It follows that

\displaystyle\bigg\|\bigg\{\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}-\vec{h}}}(\cdot)\bigg[\fint_{C(\cdot,2^{\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}})}|V(\cdot)t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(y)|^{a}dy\bigg]^{\frac{1}{a}}\bigg\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\bigg\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\bigg\|\bigg\{\bigg[\fint_{C(\cdot,2^{\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{+}-\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}})}|V(\cdot)\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}(y)t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(y)|^{a}dy\bigg]^{\frac{1}{a}}\bigg\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\bigg\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\Big\|\Big\{V\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\Big\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\Big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\leq|\widetilde{\Omega}|^{\tau}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)},

where the last two steps were applications of Lemma
7.7
with the sequence
\{\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\Omega}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
in place of
\{t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}
and the definition of the quasi-norm of
\dot{a}^{\vec{0},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
.

The strong maximal inequality shows that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\Omega}|

\displaystyle=|\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}:\ \mathcal{M}_{\vec{n}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega}(y)>c2^{-(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}\}|\lesssim 2^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}[1+\log^{k-1}2^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}]|\Omega|

\displaystyle\sim 2^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}}[1+(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}]^{k-1}|\Omega|.

Substituting the above estimates into (
7.4
) of Lemma
7.5
with
H_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}=\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}V
, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\{\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}V(Bt)_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}^{u}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sum_{\vec{h}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\sum_{\vec{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}}\Big[2^{-(\vec{E}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2})\cdot\vec{h}_{-}}2^{-(\vec{F}+\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\frac{\vec{n}}{a})\cdot\vec{h}_{+}}2^{-(\vec{D}-\frac{\vec{n}}{a})\cdot\vec{l}}2^{(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n}\tau}P(\vec{l},\vec{h})|\Omega|^{\tau}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}\Big]^{u},

where
P(\vec{l},\vec{h}):=(1+(\vec{l}+\vec{h}_{-})\cdot\vec{n})^{(k-1)\tau}
. It is immediate that the series converges if and only if

\vec{D}>\frac{\vec{n}}{a}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{E}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{a}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2};

(7.10)

the polynomial factor
P(\vec{l},\vec{h})
has no impact on this. Since Lemmas
7.5
and
7.7
allow us to choose any
a\in(0,r)
, where
r:=r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\wedge 1
, inequalities (
7.10
) will hold under the assumed (
7.8
) (with
\vec{s}=\vec{0}
), as soon as
a
is chosen close enough to
r
. Thus, we obtain

\|\{\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}V(Bt)_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}\lesssim|\Omega|^{\tau}\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{0},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}.

Dividing by
|\Omega|^{\tau}
and taking the supremum over
\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
prove the claimed result for
\vec{s}=\vec{0}
. This suffices by Remark
7.4
, as we already pointed out in the beginning of the proof.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

Theorem
7.12
clearly extends Theorem
7.10
, which is recovered as the special case
\tau=0
. Theorem
7.12
is the first result on the boundedness of almost diagonal operators on multi-parameter Besov or Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces with
\tau\in(0,\infty)
, even in the unweighted bi-parameter case, where the only result prior to this work,
[
71
, Theorem 5.2]
, dealt with
\tau=0
only.

However, Theorem
7.12
is not a complete extension of the one-parameter results of
[
159
]
(unweighted) and
[
13
]
(matrix-weighted), since the almost diagonal conditions imposed by those works are weaker than what one obtains by specialising (
7.8
) to the one-parameter case. More precisely,
[
13
, Theorem 5.1]
assumes a condition like (
7.8
), but with
\tau
replaced by a modified parameter

\widehat{\tau}:=\Big(\tau-\frac{1}{p}\Big[1-\frac{d}{n}\Big]\Big)_{+},

when
V
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension
d
. In particular, in the unweighed case
V\equiv 1
, the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension is
d=0
, and hence
\widehat{\tau}=0
for all
\tau\in[0,\frac{1}{p}]
; this agrees with the assumptions of
[
159
, Theorem 4.1]
in this parameter range. At the moment, it remains open how to construct a multi-parameter argument that reproduces this improvement in the one-parameter case (and hopefully provides a natural extension to several parameters, taking advantage of the available theory of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions in this setting as developed in Subsection
2.3
).

On the other hand, the said improvements of
[
13
, Theorem 5.1]
over the present Theorem
7.12
require the additional information about the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of the weight, while the assumptions of Theorem
7.12
are uniform over all
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. From this perspective, these assumptions are not so unreasonable, as we will show in Corollary
7.25
below.

Next, we want to study the composition of two almost diagonal operators.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F},\vec{D}^{*},\vec{E}^{*},\vec{F}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}

satisfy that, for every
i\in[k]
,

D_{i},D^{*}_{i}>n_{i},\ E_{i}+F^{*}_{i}>\min(D_{i},D^{*}_{i}),\ F_{i}+E^{*}_{i}>n_{i},\ E_{i}\neq E^{*}_{i},\ F_{i}\neq F^{*}_{i}.

If
A:=\{a_{Q,R}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal
and
B:=\{b_{R,P}\}_{R,P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
is

(\vec{D}^{*},\vec{E}^{*},\vec{F}^{*})
-almost diagonal, then

\displaystyle A\circ B:=\Bigg\{\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}a_{Q,R}b_{R,P}\Bigg\}_{Q,P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is
(\min(\vec{D},\vec{D}^{*}),\min(\vec{E},\vec{E}^{*}),\min(\vec{F},\vec{F}^{*}))
-almost diagonal.

Proof.

By the definition of
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal, we obtain,
for every
Q,P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\displaystyle|(A\circ B)_{Q,P}|\lesssim\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\prod_{i=1}^{k}b^{D_{i},E_{i},F_{i}}_{i;Q_{i},R_{i}}b^{D_{i}^{*},E_{i}^{*},F_{i}^{*}}_{i;R_{i},P_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Bigg(\sum_{R_{i}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{i}})}b^{D_{i},E_{i},F_{i}}_{i;Q_{i},R_{i}}b^{D_{i}^{*},E_{i}^{*},F_{i}^{*}}_{i;R_{i},P_{i}}\Bigg).

Therefore, without loss of generality, we may assume
k=1
.
For every
Q,P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}b^{D,E,F}_{Q,R}b^{D^{*},E^{*},F^{*}}_{R,P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\min(j_{Q},j_{P})}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\cdots+\sum_{j=\min(j_{Q},j_{P})+1}^{\max(j_{Q},j_{P})}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\cdots

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=\max(j_{Q},j_{P})+1}^{\infty}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\cdots

\displaystyle=:\mathrm{I}+\mathrm{II}+\mathrm{III}.

From
[
76
, Appendix B.1]

(see also
[
15
, Lemma 3.6]
), we infer that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\min(j_{Q},j_{P})}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{j}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{Q}-c_{R}|}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(R)}\bigg]^{-D}\bigg[\frac{\ell(Q)}{\ell(R)}\bigg]^{E}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{R}-c_{P}|}{\ell(R)\vee\ell(P)}\bigg]^{-D^{*}}\bigg[\frac{\ell(P)}{\ell(R)}\bigg]^{F^{*}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\min(j_{Q},j_{P})}\Big(1+2^{j}|c_{Q}-c_{P}|\Big)^{-\min(D,D^{*})}2^{(j-j_{Q})E}2^{(j-j_{P})F^{*}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\min(j_{Q},j_{P})}\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{Q}-c_{P}|}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(P)}\bigg]^{-\min(D,D^{*})}2^{-[j-\min(j_{Q},j_{P})]\min(D,D^{*})}2^{(j-j_{Q})E}2^{(j-j_{P})F^{*}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg[1+\frac{|c_{Q}-c_{P}|}{\ell(Q)\vee\ell(P)}\bigg]^{-\min(D,D^{*})}2^{\min(j_{Q},j_{P})(E+F^{*})-j_{Q}E-j_{P}F^{*}}=b^{\min(D,D^{*}),E,F^{*}}_{Q,P}.

Similarly, we obtain
\mathrm{II}\lesssim b^{\min(D,D^{*}),\min(E,E^{*}),\min(F,F^{*})}_{Q,P}

and
\mathrm{III}\lesssim b^{\min(D,D^{*}),E^{*},F}_{Q,P}
.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
7.14
.
∎

Definition 7.15
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\tau\in[0,\infty)
, and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
.
An infinite matrix
\{b_{Q,R}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is said to be
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal if it is
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal with

\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F}
satisfying (
7.8
).

Proposition 7.16
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\tau\in[0,\infty)
, and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
.
If
A^{(i)}:=\{a^{(i)}_{Q,R}\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal for both
i=1,2
, then

A^{(1)}\circ A^{(2)}
is also
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal.

Proof.

By the definition of the
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal,
it follows that
for both
i=1,2
, the matrix
A^{(i)}
is
(\vec{D}^{(i)},\vec{E}^{(i)},\vec{F}^{(i)})
-almost diagonal with some

\vec{D}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{E}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{n}\tau+\vec{s},\quad\vec{F}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}.

(7.11)

Note that

\Big(\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{n}\tau+\vec{s}\Big)+\Big(\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}\Big)=\vec{n}\tau+\frac{\vec{n}}{r}\geq\vec{n}>\vec{0}.

(7.12)

Making some components slightly smaller, if necessary, while still maintaining the lower bounds in (
7.11
), we can assume without loss of generality that

\vec{E}^{(1)}+\vec{F}^{(2)}>\min(\vec{D}^{(1)},\vec{D}^{(2)})

and, for every
\nu\in[k]
,

(E^{(1)})_{\nu}\neq(E^{(2)})_{\nu}
and
(F^{(1)})_{\nu}\neq(F^{(2)})_{\nu}
.
Inequality (
7.12
) also shows in particular that

\vec{D}^{(1)},\vec{D}^{(2)}>\vec{n},\quad\vec{F}^{(1)}+\vec{E}^{(2)}>\vec{n}.

These, together with Lemma
7.14
, further imply that

A^{(1)}\circ A^{(2)}
is
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal,
where

\begin{split}\vec{D}=\min_{i=1,2}\vec{D}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{E}=\min_{i=1,2}\vec{E}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{n}\tau+\vec{s},\quad\vec{F}=\min_{i=1,2}\vec{F}^{(i)}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}.\end{split}

Thus,
A^{(1)}\circ A^{(2)}
is also
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal.
This finishes the proof of Proposition
7.16
.
∎

Remark 7.17
.

In
[
63
, Theorem 9.1(i)]
, Frazier and Jawerth proved that
the composition of two
\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}
-almost diagonal operators
is still an
\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}
-almost diagonal operator.
In the case where
k=1
,
\tau=0
, and
\pi=F
, we have

\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}=\dot{f}^{s}_{p,q}
,
and thus Proposition
7.16
in this case coincides with
[
63
, Thoerem 9.1(i)]
.

We can also obtain the following variant of Theorem
7.12
. Note that the almost diagonality assumption (
7.13
) below is stronger than the corresponding assumption (
7.8
) of Theorem
7.12
(indeed, the lower bounds for each
\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F}
have been increased by a new term); the advantage of Corollary
7.18
is that we can trade the assumption that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
) against the condition (
6.4
); thus, besides pure Besov or Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, we can also allow matrix-weighted space with the
\vec{q}\leq\vec{p}
.

Corollary 7.18
.

Let
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,
\vec{p}=p\cdot\vec{1}
with
p\in(0,\infty)
,

\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
,
and
r:=r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\wedge 1
.
Suppose that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
has
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimensions
(\vec{d},\vec{e},\vec{\Delta})
,
and let
B
be
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal with

\vec{D}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}+\vec{n}\tau+\vec{\Delta},\quad\vec{E}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{s}+\vec{n}\tau+\frac{\vec{d}}{p},\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}+\frac{\vec{e}}{p}.

(7.13)

Then
Bt
is well defined for every
t\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})
, and

\|Bt\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})}

with the implicit positive constant independent of
t
. If, in addition, we have (
6.4
), then the same holds with
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
in place of
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})
.

Proof.

Recalling that the unweighted case is one of the main cases covered by Theorem
7.12
, the said theorem implies that all
(\vec{D}^{\prime},\vec{E}^{\prime},\vec{F}^{\prime})
-almost diagonal matrices with

\vec{D}^{\prime}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{E}^{\prime}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{s}+\vec{n}\tau,\quad\vec{F}^{\prime}>\frac{\vec{n}}{r}-\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}

(7.14)

define bounded operators on the unweighed
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

Feeding this information into Corollary
7.2
, it follows that all
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})=(\vec{D}^{\prime}+\vec{\Delta},\vec{E}^{\prime}+{\vec{d}}/{p},\vec{F}^{\prime}+{\vec{e}}/{p})
-almost diagonal matrices define bounded operators on
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})
. When
(\vec{D}^{\prime},\vec{E}^{\prime},\vec{F}^{\prime})
takes arbitrary values as in (
7.14
), it is clear that
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
can take arbitrary values as in (
7.13
). This proves the first claim concerning boundedness on
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})
.

Finally, adding assumption (
6.4
), it follows from Corollary
6.5
that
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}([V]_{p})=\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
with equivalent norms, and this completes the proof.
∎

7.4.
Sharpness

The sharpness of the one-parameter version of Theorem
7.10
was studied in detail in
[
13
]
. The results of these considerations can be transferred to the multi-parameter setting without the need to revisit the details of the original counterexamples, for which the reader may consult
[
13
]
. In order to lift one-parameter examples to several parameters, we will make use of the following:

Lemma 7.19
.

For sequences
t^{(\nu)}=\{t^{(\nu)}_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})}
with
\nu\in[k]
, define the tensor sequence

t^{\otimes}:=(t^{\otimes}_{R})_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},\quad t^{\otimes}_{R}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}t^{(\nu)}_{R_{\nu}}.

Given a permutation
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
, define
\pi_{\nu}\in S_{[2]}
for each
\nu\in[k]
, by

\pi_{\nu}:=\begin{cases}F:=\mathrm{id},&\text{if}\quad\pi^{-1}(\nu)<\pi^{-1}(\nu+k),\\
B:=(1\ 2),&\text{if}\quad\pi^{-1}(\nu)>\pi^{-1}(\nu+k).\end{cases}

Let
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
, and

V(x)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}V(x_{\nu})

be a scalar-valued product weight, and suppose moreover that one of the following additional conditions is satisfied:

(i)

\tau=0
, or

(ii)

\vec{p}=\vec{q}=p\cdot\vec{1}
for some
p\in(0,\infty)
, and
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
.

Then

\|t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},\tau}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}(V_{\nu})}.

Proof.

(
i
): Let first
\tau=0
. This simplifies the quasi-norm of
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}=\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
since the supremum over
\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
is avoided, and

\|t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}=\|\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}Vt_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}^{\otimes}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}=\Big\|\Big\{x\mapsto\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}2^{i_{\nu}s_{\nu}}V_{\nu}(x_{\nu})t^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}(x_{\nu})\Big\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\Big\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}},

(7.15)

where the
\nu
-th component of the product depends only on the variables
i_{\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x_{\nu}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}
. For any product of functions of different variables on
\sigma
-finite measure spaces, we have the Fubini identities

\displaystyle\|(y,z)\mapsto f(y)g(z)\|_{L^{u}(d\lambda(y);L^{v}(d\omega(z)))}

\displaystyle\quad=\|f\|_{L^{u}(\lambda)}\|g\|_{L^{v}(\omega)}=\|(y,z)\mapsto f(y)g(z)\|_{L^{v}(d\omega(z);L^{u}(d\lambda(y)))}.

Hence, in (
7.15
), we may permute the order of any consecutive
L^{p_{\nu}}
or
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
and
L^{p_{\mu}}
or
\ell^{q_{\mu}}
quasi-norms, except the combination of
L^{p_{\nu}}
and
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
with the same
\nu\in[k]
. By a repeated application of such permutations, we find that

\begin{split}\Bigg\|\Bigg\{x\mapsto\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}2^{i_{\nu}s_{\nu}}V_{\nu}(x_{\nu})t^{(\nu)}_{i_{\nu}}(x_{\nu})\Bigg\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\Bigg\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}&=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|\{2^{i_{\nu}s_{\nu}}V_{\nu}t_{i_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}\}_{i_{\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}}\|_{(L^{p_{\nu}}\ell^{q_{\nu}})_{\pi_{\nu}}}\\
&=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},0}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}(V_{\nu})},\end{split}

where
\pi_{\nu}\in S_{[2]}
simply encodes the fact that
L^{p_{\nu}}
and
\ell^{q_{\nu}}
retain their original order. Together with (
7.15
), this completes the proof of case (
i
).

(
ii
): We now have to deal with the supremum over
\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
in

\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}=\sup_{\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}|\Omega|^{-\tau}\|\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}Vt_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}},

but the assumption that
\vec{p}=\vec{q}=p\cdot\vec{1}
allows us to simplify
(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}=\ell^{p}L^{p}
. Hence

\displaystyle\|\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}Vt_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}^{p}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}|2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}(x)V(x)t_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(x)\}|^{p}dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}(\Omega)}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}p}\int_{R}V^{p}(x)dx\frac{|t_{R}|^{p}}{|R|^{\frac{p}{2}}}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}\vec{\ell}(R)^{-\vec{s}}V^{p}(R)\frac{|t_{R}|^{p}}{|R|^{\frac{p}{2}}},

where
V^{p}(R):=\int_{R}V^{p}(x)dx
,

\vec{\ell}(R):=(\ell(R_{1}),\ldots,\ell(R_{k})),\qquad\vec{\ell}(R)^{-\vec{s}}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\ell(R_{\nu})^{-s_{\nu}}.

In particular, for
t=t^{\otimes}
, we obtain

\|\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}}Vt^{\otimes}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\|_{(L^{\vec{p}}\ell^{\vec{q}})_{\pi}}^{p}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}u^{\otimes}_{R},

where

u^{\otimes}_{R}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}u_{R_{\nu}},\quad u_{R_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}:=\ell(R_{\nu})^{-s_{\nu}}V_{\nu}^{p}(R_{\nu})|R_{\nu}|^{-\frac{p}{2}}|t^{(\nu)}_{R_{\nu}}|^{p}.

Specialising to
\Omega=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\Omega_{\nu}
with
\Omega_{\nu}\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})
, we note that

\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{\tau p}}\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}u_{R_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\frac{1}{|\Omega_{\nu}|^{\tau p}}\sum_{R_{\nu}\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega_{\nu})}u_{R_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}.

Taking the supremum over all
\Omega
of such product form is equivalent to taking the supremum independently over each
\Omega_{\nu}\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})
, and we deduce that

\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},\tau}_{p,p}(V_{\nu})}\leq\|t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)};

(7.16)

this direction did not make use of the assumption that
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
.

We finally want to prove the inequality (
7.16
) in the reverse direction. This is a bit more delicate, given that (
7.16
) as stated was obtained by specialising the condition defining the quasi-norm on the right to rather particular sets of the product form. For the reverse direction, we need to obtain an estimate for general open sets from the knowledge about a much more specific special case.

Suppose without loss of generality that
\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(V_{\nu})}\leq 1
for each
\nu\in[k]
. Let us further assume, still without loss of generality, that the sequences
(t^{(\nu)}_{Q})_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})}
are finitely non-zero. Indeed, if we can prove the desired estimate of
t^{\otimes}
in this special case, the general case easily follows by monotone convergence.

Specialising the defining condition of
\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(V_{\nu})}\leq 1
from all open sets
\Omega_{\nu}\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})
to just
\Omega_{\nu}=P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})
, we have the estimates

\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(P)}u_{Q}^{(\nu)}\leq|P|.

(7.17)

For each
\nu\in[k]
, we claim that there exist disjoint measurable subsets
E_{Q}^{(\nu)}\subset Q
such that
u_{Q}^{(\nu)}=|E_{Q}^{(\nu)}|
. To accomplish this, we follow an algorithm from the proof of
[
108
, Lemma 6.3]
. Under the assumption that the sequence
t^{(\nu)}_{Q}
, and hence
u^{(\nu)}_{Q}
, is finitely non-zero, for the smallest cubes
Q
with
u_{Q}^{(\nu)}>0
, we simply choose any measurable subset
E^{(\nu)}_{Q}\subset Q
with
|E^{(\nu)}_{Q}|=u_{Q}^{(\nu)}\leq|Q|
by (
7.17
). Proceeding recursively, suppose that for some
Q
with
u_{Q}^{(\nu)}>0
, we have already defined
E^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}
with required properties for all
Q^{\prime}\subsetneq Q
. Then

u_{Q}^{(\nu)}=\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subset Q}u^{(\nu)}_{Q}-\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subsetneq Q}u^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}\leq|Q|-\sum_{Q^{\prime}\subsetneq Q}|E^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}|=\Big|Q\setminus\bigcup_{Q^{\prime}\subsetneq Q}E^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}\Big|,

and hence we can find a measurable subset

E^{(\nu)}_{Q}\subset Q\setminus\bigcup_{Q^{\prime}\subsetneq Q}E^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}

(7.18)

of size

|E^{(\nu)}_{Q}|=u_{Q}^{(\nu)}.

By (
7.18
),
E^{(\nu)}_{Q}
is disjoint from all the previously constructed
E^{(\nu)}_{Q^{\prime}}
. Since there are only finitely many
Q
with
u_{Q}^{(\nu)}>0
, this recursion stops after finitely many steps, and gives the required sets.

Using this representation of each
u_{Q}^{(\nu)}
, let us also define

E_{R}^{\otimes}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}E_{R_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}\subset R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}).

Then these
E_{R}^{\otimes}
are pairwise disjoint, and
u^{\otimes}_{R}=|E^{\otimes}_{R}|
. Then it follows at once, for every
\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, that

\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}u^{\otimes}_{R}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}|E^{\otimes}_{R}|=\Big|\bigcup_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\Omega)}E^{\otimes}_{R}\Big|\leq|\Omega|.

This completes the proof that
\|t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{s,\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(V)}\leq 1
when
\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p}(V_{\nu})}\leq 1
for every
\nu\in[k]
, and hence the proof of Lemma
7.19
.
∎

With the help of Lemma
7.19
(
i
), we obtain the following necessary conditions for the boundedness of almost diagonal operators.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
.
Suppose that, for some
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})\in\mathbb{R}^{3k}
, every
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal operator is well defined and bounded on
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
.
Then, for every
\nu\in[k]
,

D_{\nu}>\frac{n_{\nu}}{\min(1,p_{\nu})},\quad E_{\nu}>\frac{n_{\nu}}{2},\quad F_{\nu}>\frac{n_{\nu}}{\min(1,p_{\nu})}-\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}-s_{\nu}.

(7.19)

Proof.

The proof consists of a reduction to the one-parameter case via Lemma
7.19
.

For
t^{(\nu)}\in\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},0}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}
, let
t^{\otimes}
be defined as in Lemma
7.19
. In a similar way, for
(D_{\nu},E_{\nu},F_{\nu})
-almost diagonal matrices
B^{(\nu)}=\{b^{(\nu)}_{I,J}\}_{I,J\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})}
, we define

B^{\otimes}:=\{b^{\otimes}_{P,R}\}_{P,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},\quad b^{\otimes}_{P,R}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}b^{(\nu)}_{P_{\nu},R_{\nu}}.

It is then evident that
B^{\otimes}
is
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal. Moreover, if
u^{(\nu)}:=B^{(\nu)}t^{(\nu)}
and
u^{\otimes}
is constructed from these
u^{(\nu)}
in the same way as
t^{\otimes}
from the
t^{(\nu)}
, then
B^{\otimes}t^{\otimes}=u^{\otimes}
.

The assumption that every
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal matrix is bounded on
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
, together with Lemma
7.19
, then implies that

\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|B^{(\nu)}t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},0}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}}=\|B^{\otimes}t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}\lesssim\|t^{\otimes}\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},0}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\|t^{(\nu)}\|_{\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},0}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}}.

With all but one
B^{(\nu)}
equal to
\{\delta_{I,J}\}_{I,J\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})}
, it follows that
B^{(\nu)}
must be bounded on the respective
\dot{a}^{s_{\nu},0}_{p_{\nu},q_{\nu},\pi_{\nu}}
. By
[
13
, Lemma 7.1 or 9.1]
(depending on whether
\pi_{i}\in\{B,F\}
, but the conclusion is the same), a necessary condition for this boundedness is precisely (
7.19
). Since this applies to every
\nu\in[k]
, the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

Proposition
7.20
shows in particular that the assumptions of Theorem
7.10
are sharp whenever
p_{i}\equiv p\in(0,\infty)
and
r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\geq\min(1,p)
for all
i\in[k]
. Assuming the first condition
\vec{p}=p\cdot\vec{1}
, the second condition is automatic in the Besov case [when
r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})=p
] and requires that
q_{i}\geq\min(1,p)
in the Triebel–Lizorkin case (when
r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})=\min\{p,q_{i}:i\in[k]\}
).

Thus, for non-mixed exponents
\vec{p}=p\cdot\vec{1}
and
\vec{q}=q\cdot\vec{1}
, Theorem
7.10
is sharp in the Besov case for all exponents, and in the Triebel–Lizorkin case if
q\geq\min(1,p)
. In particular, the assumptions of
[
71
, Theorem 5.2]
, the special case of Theorem
7.10
for unweighted bi-parameter spaces with non-mixed exponents
\vec{p}=(p,p)
and
\vec{q}=(q,q)
, are sharp under the said restriction
q\geq\min(1,p)
in the Triebel–Lizorkin case. This sharpness is new even in this special case.

The question about sharp almost diagonal conditions for Triebel–Lizorkin spaces in the remaining range
q<\min(1,p)
is open even in the one-parameter case
[
13
]
, where the same restriction appears.

We will then discuss the sharpness of Theorem
7.12
for the special parameter
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
. By Remark
7.13
, we already know that sharper conditions than those of Theorem
7.12
can be found in the one-parameter case by taking into account the
\mathscr{A}_{p}
-dimension of the weights
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Thus, a meaningful way to discuss possible sharpness is to inquire about the minimal almost diagonal conditions that guarantee the boundedness for all
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. To this end, the following necessary condition from the one-parameter case is a slight reformulation of
[
13
, Lemma 11.1]
:

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(1,\infty]
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\pi\in S_{[2]}
. Let
(D,E,F)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
be such that every
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operator is bounded on
\dot{a}^{s,\frac{1}{p}}_{p,q,\pi}(V)
for every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, or even just every power weight
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then

D\geq n+\frac{n}{p},\quad E\geq\frac{n}{2}+s+\frac{n}{p},\quad F>\frac{n}{2}-s.

Proof.

[
13
, Lemma 11.1]
contains the following result, formulated in terms of
W\in A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
instead of
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Recall that these are in one-to-one correspondence via
V(x)=W(x)^{\frac{1}{p}}
. If the assumptions of the lemma hold just for the single weight
W(x)=|x|^{-d}
(thus
V(x)=|x|^{-\frac{d}{p}}
), then

D>n+\frac{d}{p},\quad E>\frac{n}{2}+s+\frac{d}{p},\quad F>\frac{n}{2}-s.

Since
W\in A_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
d\in[0,n)
, if the assumptions of the lemma holds as stated, the previous display must hold for all
d\in[0,n)
, and the only way that this can be is that
(D,E,F)
must be as stated in the lemma.
∎

Corollary 7.23
.

Except possibly for the end-points for
D
and
E
, the assumptions (
7.8
) of Theorem
7.12
are sharp, in the sense of being the minimal assumptions to work for all weights
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
simultaneously, in the one-parameter case with
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
,
p\in[1,\infty)
, and
q\in(1,\infty]
.

Proof.

The assumption that
p\geq 1
and
q>1
implies that, irrespective of
\pi
, there holds
r(\pi,p,q)\geq 1
, and hence
r=r(\pi,p,q)\wedge 1=1
.
In this situation, the assumptions (
7.8
) of Theorem
7.12
read as

D>n+\frac{n}{p},\quad E>\frac{n}{2}+\frac{n}{p}+s,\quad F>n-\frac{n}{2}-s=\frac{n}{2}-s.

(7.20)

Note that the assumptions of Corollary
7.23
on
p,q,\tau
, and
V
are special cases of the assumptions of Lemma
7.22
. Hence, if all
(D,E,F)
-almost diagonal operators are bounded on
\dot{a}^{0,\frac{1}{p}}_{p,q,\pi}(V)
for all
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the conclusions of Lemma
7.22
on
D,E,F
are in force. Except for the end points of the conditions for
D
and
E
, these are seen to coincide with (
7.20
), proving the claimed sharpness.
∎

We also obtain necessary conditions at least for a limited case of genuinely multi-parameter situations with
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
. The parameters here are restricted by the fact that they need to be compatible with both the one-parameter result of Lemma
7.22
and the lifting result of Lemma
7.19
(
ii
).

Proposition 7.24
.

Let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
. Let
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})\in\mathbb{R}^{3k}
be such that every
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal operator is bounded on
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p,\pi}(V)
for every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, or even just every product power weight
V(x)=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}|x_{\nu}|^{\alpha_{\nu}}
in this class.
Then

\vec{D}\geq\vec{n}+\frac{\vec{n}}{p},\quad\vec{E}\geq\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{s}+\frac{\vec{n}}{p},\quad\vec{F}>\frac{\vec{n}}{2}-\vec{s}.

Note that the permutation
\pi
is redundant in this case, since it is immediate from Fubini’s theorem that
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p,\pi}(V)=\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\frac{1}{p}}_{p,p,\pi^{\prime}}(V)
for any two
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in S_{[2k]}
, but we have kept it to avoid introducing ad hoc notation just for this one place in the paper.

Proof of Proposition
7.24
.

This follows from a combination of Lemmas
7.19
(
ii
) (with weights
V_{\nu}(x)=|x_{\nu}|^{\alpha_{\nu}}
) and
7.22
in the same way that Proposition
7.20
was proved using Lemma
7.19
(
i
) and the necessary conditions for in the one-parameter case from
[
13
]
.
∎

Corollary 7.25
.

Except possibly for the end-points for
\vec{D}
and
\vec{E}
, the assumptions (
7.8
) of Theorem
7.12
are sharp, in the sense of being the minimal assumptions to work for all weights
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
simultaneously, in the case that
p\in(1,\infty)
,
\vec{p}=\vec{q}=p\cdot\vec{1}
, and
\tau=\frac{1}{p}
.

Proof.

This follows from a comparison of the sufficient conditions of Theorem
7.12
with the necessary conditions of Proposition
7.24
in the same way as Corollary
7.23
was deduced from the comparison of the one-parameter case of Theorem
7.12
with Lemma
7.22
.
∎

8.
Molecules and their applications

8.1.
Molecular characterisation

In this section, we establish the molecular characterization of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, i.e., we describe basic building blocks (the “molecules”) such that, in a sense to be made precise in Theorem
8.9
,

(1)

every distribution in
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
can be represented as a convergent series of these molecules, with suitable estimates for the coefficients;

(2)

every distribution that admits such an expansion must belong to
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, and its norm in this space can be estimated by the size of the coefficients.

This can be seen as generalisation of the
\varphi
-transform characterisation (Theorem
6.16
), but allowing more flexibility in the choice of the molecules. This is particularly useful in verifying the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
-membership with the help of a molecular expansion, which will be key to the
T(1)
theorem (a boundedness criterion for singular integral operators) on the matrix-weighted multi-parameter spaces that we consider (Theorem
10.30
).

In the unweighted one-parameter case, such molecular characterisations go back to Frazier and Jawerth
[
63
]
; the matrix-weighted one-parameter case is due to Bu et al.
[
14
]
. The goal of this section is a multi-parameter extension of these results. While following the same broad outline, there are additional technical challenges. Notably, in the one-parameter setting, one can typically estimate the pairing of two functions by using the smoothness of one of them and cancellation properties of the other one, depending on the relative scales of the two functions. In the multi-parameter contexts, each function will have independent scaling properties in different component directions, and we will need to couple the smoothness of
f
with cancellation of
g
in some directions, and cancellations of
f
with the smoothness of
g
in the remaining ones; see e.g. (
8.11
).

Turning to the details, we first give some notation. For every
r\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\begin{cases}\lfloor r\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k\leq r\},\\
\lfloor\!\lfloor r\rfloor\!\rfloor:=\max\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k<r\},\end{cases}\begin{cases}\lceil r\rceil:=\min\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k\geq r\},\\
\lceil\!\lceil r\rceil\!\rceil:=\min\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ k>r\},\end{cases}

and
r^{**}:=r-\lfloor\!\lfloor r\rfloor\!\rfloor\in(0,1]
.
For every
\gamma:=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{\vec{n}}
,

|\gamma|_{\rm{vec}}:=(|\gamma_{1}|,\ldots,|\gamma_{k}|)
.
For every
\vec{a},\vec{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, let

\vec{a}\wedge\vec{b}:=(a_{1}\wedge b_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\wedge b_{k}),

\vec{a}\vee\vec{b}:=(a_{1}\vee b_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\vee b_{k}).

The following definition is a multi-parameter extension of
[
14
, Definition 3.4]
, which in turn is inspired by several related definitions in the earlier literature. There is some tradition in the literature of fixing, from the beginning, a choice of the molecular parameters that is convenient for the specific situation at hand. Instead, we follow the style of
[
14
]
of first defining molecules with generic parameters, and only a little later “discovering” a choice of these parameters that is convenient in the context of the spaces
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
.
A version of product-space molecules has also been previously introduced in
[
112
, Definition 14]
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\vec{K},\vec{M}\in[0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{L}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}^{k}:=(\mathbb{Z}_{+}\cup\{-1,0\})^{k}
, and
\vec{N}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
.
A function
m_{Q}
is called a

(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
-molecule on
Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

if it satisfies

(i)

the size and smoothness condition: for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}^{\vec{n}}
with
|\gamma|_{\rm{vec}}<\vec{N}
, all
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, and all
x,h\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
:

|\Delta_{h}^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}m_{Q}(x)|\leq|Q|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\begin{cases}\displaystyle\Big[1+\frac{|x_{\nu}-c_{Q_{\nu}}|}{\ell(Q_{\nu})}\Big]^{-(K_{\nu}\vee M_{\nu})},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}=0=\gamma_{\nu},\\
\displaystyle\Big[1+\frac{|x_{\nu}-c_{Q_{\nu}}|}{\ell(Q_{\nu})}\Big]^{-M_{\nu}}\ell(Q_{\nu})^{-|\gamma_{\nu}|},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}=0\neq\gamma_{\nu},\\
\displaystyle\sup_{|z_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|\leq|h_{\nu}|}\Big[1+\frac{|z_{\nu}-c_{Q_{\nu}}|}{\ell(Q_{\nu})}\Big]^{-M_{\nu}}\frac{|h_{\nu}|^{N_{\nu}^{**}}}{\ell(Q_{\nu})^{|\gamma_{\nu}|+N_{\nu}^{**}}},\quad\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}\neq 0,\end{cases}

(8.1)

where

\Delta_{h}^{\alpha}:=\prod_{\nu\in[k]:\alpha_{\nu}=1}\Delta_{h_{\nu}}^{(\nu)},\qquad\Delta_{h_{\nu}}^{(\nu)}f(x):=f(x)-f(x_{\nu}-h_{\nu},x^{\prime});

(8.2)

(ii)

the cancellation condition: for every
\nu\in[k]
and

\gamma_{\nu}\in\mathbb{N}^{n_{\nu}}
with
|\gamma_{\nu}|\leq L_{\nu}
,
and a.e.
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}x_{\nu}^{\gamma_{\nu}}m_{Q}(x_{\nu},x^{\prime})\,dx_{\nu}=0.

(8.3)

Remark 8.2
.

The parameters
\vec{L},\vec{N}
count the number of vanishing moments and derivatives of
m_{Q}
, respectively. Allowing the value
L_{\nu}=-1
is relevant to account for the possibility of not imposing any vanishing moments (
8.3
) in the
x_{\nu}
variable. Molecules with no vanishing moments are useful in trace theorems; see
[
14
, Remark 5.7]
.

To keep the technical aspects at a reasonable level,
we restrict the range of
\vec{N}
into
(0,\infty)^{k}

in Definition
8.1
.
More precisely, when

k=1
and
N\in(0,\infty)
,
condition (
8.1
) agrees with

[
144
, (1.1.6), (1.1.8), and (1.1.9)]
; however,
in our setting, we only consider positive values of
N
.

The following lemma is a multi-parameter extension of
[
63
, Lemma B.1]
. It uses partially the same ideas, but requires some additional care with iterated Taylor expansions with respect to different variables.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\vec{L}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}^{k}
,
\vec{N}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
, and
\vec{K},\vec{M}\in[0,\infty)^{k}
.
Let
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and suppose that its distributional derivatives up to order
\vec{E}
are also given by
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
-functions.
Suppose that, for some
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, these functions satisfy
for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{\vec{n}}

with
|\gamma|_{\rm{vec}}<\vec{N}
, every
\alpha\in\{0,1\}^{k}
,
and every
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

|\Delta_{y}^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}g(x)|\leq 2^{{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\frac{\vec{n}}{2}}}\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\begin{cases}\displaystyle(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-(K_{\nu}\vee M_{\nu})},&\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}=0=\gamma_{\nu},\\
\displaystyle(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}}2^{j_{\nu}|\gamma_{\nu}|},&\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}=0\neq\gamma_{\nu},\\
\displaystyle\sup_{|z_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|\leq|y_{\nu}|}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|z_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}}2^{j_{\nu}(|\gamma_{\nu}|+{N_{\nu}^{**}})}|y_{\nu}|^{N_{\nu}^{**}},&\text{if }\alpha_{\nu}\neq 0,\end{cases}

(8.4)

where
\Delta_{y}^{\alpha}
is defined in (
8.2
); and
for every
\nu\in[k]
and every
\gamma_{\nu}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n_{\nu}}

with
|\gamma_{\nu}|\leq L_{\nu}
,
and a.e.
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\ominus\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}x_{\nu}^{\gamma_{\nu}}g(x_{\nu},x^{\prime})\,dx_{\nu}=0,

(8.5)

where this last condition is void for any
\nu\in[k]
with
L_{\nu}=-1
.

Further assume that
h\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
satisfies the same assumptions as above with some
\vec{K}^{\prime}
,
\vec{L}^{\prime}
,
\vec{M}^{\prime}
,
\vec{N}^{\prime}
in place of
\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N}
, and some
\vec{l}
in place of
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}
. We further assume that

\vec{K}^{\prime}>\vec{N}+\vec{n},\qquad\vec{K}>\vec{N}^{\prime}+\vec{n},

(8.6)

and

\vec{L}^{\prime}=\lfloor\!\lfloor\vec{N}\rfloor\!\rfloor,\qquad\vec{L}=\lfloor\!\lfloor\vec{N}^{\prime}\rfloor\!\rfloor.

(8.7)

Then there exists a positive constant
C
,
independent of
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}
,
\vec{l}
, and
x
, such that, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

|g*h(x)|\leq C2^{-(\vec{l}-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}})_{+}\cdot(\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N})-(\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}-\vec{l})_{+}\cdot(\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N}^{\prime})}\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\Big(1+2^{j_{\nu}\wedge l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|\Big)^{-(M_{\nu}\wedge M_{\nu}^{\prime})}.

(8.8)

Proof.

For any
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
and
I\subseteq[k]
,
we write
y_{I}=(y_{\nu})_{\nu\in I}
. Let

G:=\{\nu\in[k]:j_{\nu}\leq l_{\nu}\},\qquad H:=[k]\setminus G.

Then

g*h(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{H}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{G}}}g(y_{G},x_{H}-y_{H})h(x_{G}-y_{G},y_{H})dy_{G}\,dy_{H},

where
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{H}}:=\bigotimes_{\nu\in H}\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}

and
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{G}}:=\bigotimes_{\nu\in G}\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}
.
We recall the multivariate Taylor polynomial

\operatorname{Tayl}_{x,d}f(y):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq d}\frac{(y-x)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial^{\alpha}f(x)

and the Taylor formula for the error term

f(y)-\operatorname{Tayl}_{x,d-1}f(y)=\sum_{|\beta|=d}\frac{(y-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}\int_{0}^{1}\partial^{\beta}f(x+t(y-x))d(1-t)^{d-1}dt,

(8.9)

from which we also deduce

f(y)-\operatorname{Tayl}_{x,d}f(y)=\sum_{|\beta|=d}\frac{(y-x)^{\beta}}{\beta!}\int_{0}^{1}\big[\partial^{\beta}f(x+t(y-x))-\partial^{\beta}f(x)\big]d(1-t)^{d-1}dt.

(8.10)

Originally derived for functions with continuous partial derivatives, this formula remains valid for a.e.
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
assuming only that the distributional derivatives are given by integrable functions: this can be proved by pairing both sides with a test function, moving all derivatives and translations to the test function side, and using (
8.9
) on the test function; details can be found in
[
1
]
.

By the cancellation assumption (
8.5
) for both
g
and
h
, and the relations (
8.7
), for each
\nu\in[k]
, we can subtract a Taylor polynomial from one of the functions
g
or
h
, since the integral of this polynomial times the other function, over the variable
x_{\nu}
, will vanish for a.e. value of the other variables. Hence

\begin{split}g*h(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{H}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}_{G}}}&\Big(\prod_{\nu\in G}(I-\operatorname{Tayl}_{x_{\nu},\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor}^{(\nu)})\Big)g(y_{G},x_{H}-y_{H})\\
&\times\Big(\prod_{\eta\in H}(I-\operatorname{Tayl}_{x_{\eta},\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\eta}^{\prime}\rfloor\!\rfloor}^{(\eta)})\Big)h(x_{G}-y_{G},y_{H})dy_{G}\,dy_{H},\end{split}

(8.11)

where
\operatorname{Tayl}^{(\nu)}
indicates Taylor expansion with respect to the
\nu
-th coordinate.

Clearly, the factors related to both
g
and
h
in (
8.11
) will be some combinations of the values of these functions and their derivatives. These both satisfy upper bounds (
8.4
) that split into a product of factors, each of which only depends on one coordinate
y_{\nu}
at the time. Hence estimating
|g*h(x)|
by bringing the absolute values inside the integral and applying (
8.4
), we can estimate the integral over each
y_{\nu}
at a time.

Consider some
\nu\in G
. (The case of
\nu\in H
will be entirely symmetric by swapping the roles of
g
and
h
.) In this case, there is no Taylor expansion in the
y_{\nu}
direction acting on
h
. Hence, the bound for the
y_{\nu}
factor of
h
will be simply that from (the
h
analogue of) (
8.4
) with
\alpha_{\nu}=0=\gamma_{\nu}
, that is,

h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})=2^{l_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}(1+2^{l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{-(K_{\nu}^{\prime}\vee M_{\nu}^{\prime})}.

For the
g
factor, we have two alternative bounds, either via the Taylor formula (
8.10
), or estimating the
I
and

\operatorname{Tayl}_{x_{\nu},\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor}^{(\nu)}
terms separately. Using (
8.4
) with
t(x-y)
in place of
y
, the first option leads to the bound

g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})\leq\sum_{|\beta|=\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|^{|\beta|}2^{j_{\nu}(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+|\beta|+N_{\gamma}^{**})}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|^{N_{\gamma}^{**}}\sup_{|z_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|\leq|y_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|z_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}}

(8.12)

and the second option to the bound

g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})\leq 2^{j_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}|)^{-(K_{\nu}\vee M_{\nu})}+\sum_{|\alpha|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|^{|\alpha|}2^{j_{\nu}(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+|\alpha|)}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}},

(8.13)

respectively.

If
|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|<3\cdot 2^{-j_{\nu}}
, we note that the variable
z_{\nu}
appearing in (
8.12
) satisfies

2^{j_{\nu}}|z_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|\leq 2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}-x_{\nu}|<3,

and hence
(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|z_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}}\sim(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}}
.
Also, noting that
|\beta|=\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor
is constant in the summation in (
8.12
),
and hence
|\beta|+N_{\nu}^{**}=N_{\nu}
, this estimate simplifies to

g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})\lesssim 2^{j_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{N_{\nu}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}},\quad|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|<3\cdot 2^{-j_{\nu}}.

(8.14)

On the other hand, if
|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\geq 3\cdot 2^{-j_{\nu}}
, then
(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{|\alpha|}<(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{N_{\nu}}

for all
|\alpha|\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor N_{\nu}\rfloor\!\rfloor<N_{\nu}
in the summation in (
8.13
), and hence this bound also simplifies to

g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})\lesssim 2^{j_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\bigg[(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}|)^{-(K_{\nu}\vee M_{\nu})}+\frac{(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{N_{\nu}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}}}\bigg],\quad|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\geq 3\cdot 2^{-j_{\nu}}.

(8.15)

Note that the second term on the right-hand side of (
8.15
) is the same as the right-hand side of (
8.14
). Moreover, if
|y_{\nu}|>\frac{1}{2}|x_{\nu}|
, then the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.15
) is dominated by the second one. Thus, the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.15
) is only needed in the remaining case when both
|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\geq 3\cdot 2^{-j_{\nu}}
and
|y_{\nu}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{\nu}|
.
Combining (
8.14
) and (
8.15
) with the observation just made, we arrive at

\begin{split}g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})&\lesssim 2^{j_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\Bigg[\frac{(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{N_{\nu}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}}}+\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\geq 3,\ |y_{\nu}|\leq\frac{1}{2}|x_{\nu}|\}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}|)^{K_{\nu}\vee M_{\nu}}}\Bigg]\\
&=:g_{\nu}^{0}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})+g_{\nu}^{1}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu}).\end{split}

(8.16)

(Note that, without the indicator,
g^{1}_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})
would be just the trivial upper bound that we could have made without any Taylor expansions; what we have gained in (
8.16
) is that this bound is only needed for restricted values of the variables, while otherwise we can use the new upper bound
g_{\nu}^{0}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})
instead.)

To estimate
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}g_{\nu}(y_{\nu})h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})dy_{\nu}
, we first consider

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}&g_{\nu}^{0}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})dy_{\nu}\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}+l_{\nu})\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}\frac{(2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{N_{\nu}}}{(1+2^{l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|)^{K_{\nu}^{\prime}}}dy_{\nu}\\
&\lesssim\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}+l_{\nu})\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{(2^{j_{\nu}-l_{\nu}}r)^{N_{\nu}}}{(1+r)^{K_{\nu}^{\prime}}}2^{-l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}r^{n_{\nu}-1}dr\sim\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+N_{\nu})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}}},\end{split}

(8.17)

where condition (
8.6
) guarantees the convergence of the integral in the last step.

It remains to estimate
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}g_{\nu}^{1}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})dy_{\nu}
.
Under the conditions in the indicator appearing in
g_{\nu}^{1}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})
in (
8.16
), we have
|x_{\nu}|\sim|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\gtrsim 2^{-j_{\nu}}
. Hence

1+2^{l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|\gtrsim 2^{l_{\nu}}(2^{-j_{\nu}}+|x_{\nu}|)=2^{l_{\nu}-j_{\nu}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)\geq 2^{l_{\nu}-j_{\nu}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}|)

and thus

h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})\lesssim 2^{l_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\Big[2^{l_{\nu}-j_{\nu}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)\Big]^{-(K_{\nu}^{\prime}\vee M_{\nu}^{\prime})}\leq 2^{l_{\nu}\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})K_{\nu}^{\prime}}(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{-M_{\nu}^{\prime}}.

We can then compute

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}g_{\nu}^{1}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})dy_{\nu}\\
&\quad\lesssim 2^{(j_{\nu}+l_{\nu})\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})K_{\nu}^{\prime}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}\frac{dy_{\nu}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|y_{\nu}|)^{K_{\nu}}}\\
&\quad\sim 2^{(j_{\nu}+l_{\nu})\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})K_{\nu}^{\prime}}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}2^{-j_{\nu}n_{\nu}}\quad\text{since $K_{\nu}>N_{\nu}^{\prime}+n_{\nu}>n_{\nu}$ by \eqref{eq:K>N+n}}\\
&\quad=2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}}\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})(K_{\nu}^{\prime}-n_{\nu})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}\leq\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+N_{\nu})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}\quad\text{since $K_{\nu}^{\prime}>N_{\nu}+n_{\nu}$ by \eqref{eq:K>N+n}};\end{split}

note that we used both bounds in (
8.6
) in different steps of this estimate.

Combining the previous computation with (
8.17
), we arrive at

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}g_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})h_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})dy_{\nu}\lesssim\frac{2^{(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+N_{\nu})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}\wedge M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}=\frac{2^{-(l_{\nu}-j_{\nu})_{+}(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+N_{\nu})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}\wedge l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}\wedge M_{\nu}^{\prime}}},

recalling that this bound was obtained for indices
\nu\in G
, i.e., the ones with
j_{\nu}\leq l_{\nu}
.

For
\nu\in H=[k]\setminus G
, it follows by symmetry that we obtain the analogous bound

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}}}g_{\nu}(x_{\nu}-y_{\nu})h_{\nu}(y_{\nu};x_{\nu})dy_{\nu}\lesssim\frac{2^{-(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})_{+}(\frac{n_{\nu}}{2}+N_{\nu}^{\prime})}}{(1+2^{j_{\nu}\wedge l_{\nu}}|x_{\nu}|)^{M_{\nu}\wedge M_{\nu}^{\prime}}}.

Taking the product of these bounds over all
\nu\in[k]
, we arrive at the claimed upper bound (
8.8
). This finishes the proof of Lemma
8.3
.
∎

We can now prove that the pairing of molecules gives rise to an almost diagonal operator. The following lemma is a multi-parameter version of
[
14
, Lemma 3.7]
, which in turn is an extension of
[
63
, Corollary B.3]
from specific molecular parameters to general ones.

Lemma 8.4
.

Let

\vec{K},\vec{K}^{\prime}>\vec{n},\quad\vec{M},\vec{M}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)^{k},\quad\vec{L},\vec{L}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}^{k},\quad\vec{N},\vec{N}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)^{k}.

Let
m_{Q}
and
b_{P}
be
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
-molecules on
P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, respectively.

Then, for every
\vec{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
, there exists a positive constant
C
, independent of
P
and
Q
, such that

|\langle m_{P},b_{Q}\rangle|\leq Cb_{\vec{n};P,Q}^{\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F}},

where

\vec{D}=\vec{M}\wedge\vec{M}^{\prime},\quad\begin{cases}\vec{E}&=\frac{1}{2}\vec{n}+\vec{N}^{\prime}\wedge(\vec{L}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}+\vec{1})\wedge(\vec{K}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}-\vec{n}-\vec{\alpha})\\
\vec{F}&=\frac{1}{2}\vec{n}+\vec{N}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}\wedge(\vec{L}^{\prime}+\vec{1})\wedge(\vec{K}^{\prime}-\vec{n}-\vec{\alpha}).\end{cases}

(8.18)

Proof.

Let
\vec{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
. Note that each molecule with certain parameters is also a molecule with any smaller parameters. We first wish to replace
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
by some

(\vec{K},\vec{L}^{\star},\vec{M},\vec{N}^{\star})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\dagger},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\dagger})
,
respectively, such that the new parameters satisfy conditions (
8.6
) and (
8.7
) of Lemma
8.3
.

We are led to choose

\begin{cases}\vec{N}^{\star}&:=\vec{N}\wedge(\vec{L}^{\prime}+\vec{1})\wedge(\vec{K}^{\prime}-\vec{n}-\vec{\alpha}),\\
\vec{N}^{\dagger}&:=\vec{N}^{\prime}\wedge(\vec{L}+\vec{1})\wedge(\vec{K}-\vec{n}-\vec{\alpha}),\end{cases}\qquad\begin{cases}\vec{L}^{\star}&:=\lfloor\!\lfloor N^{\dagger}\rfloor\!\rfloor,\\
\vec{L}^{\dagger}&:=\lfloor\!\lfloor\vec{N}^{\star}\rfloor\!\rfloor.\end{cases}

(8.19)

It is immediate from these definitions that
\vec{N}^{\star}\leq\vec{N}
and
\vec{N}^{\dagger}\leq\vec{N}^{\prime}
. Moreover,

\vec{L}^{\star}:=\lfloor\!\lfloor\vec{N}^{\dagger}\rfloor\!\rfloor\leq\lfloor\!\lfloor\vec{L}+\vec{1}\rfloor\!\rfloor=\vec{L}

and symmetrically
\vec{L}^{\dagger}\leq\vec{L}^{\prime}
. Hence, by monotonicity of the molecular conditions, the molecules
m_{Q}
and
b_{P}
are also molecules with parameters
(\vec{K},\vec{L}^{\star},\vec{M},\vec{N}^{\star})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\dagger},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\dagger})
, respectively. The new parameters satisfy (
8.7
) by definition, and (
8.6
) follows from

\vec{K}^{\prime}>\vec{K}^{\prime}-\vec{n}-\vec{\alpha}+\vec{n}\geq\vec{N}^{\star}+\vec{n}

and a symmetric estimate for
\vec{K}
and
\vec{N}^{\dagger}
.

Suppose next that
\vec{\ell}(P)=2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}
and
\vec{\ell}(Q)=2^{-\vec{l}}
and let

g(\cdot):=m_{Q}(c_{Q}-\cdot)\text{ and }h(\cdot):=b_{P}(c_{P}+\cdot)

which are molecules on rectangles of the same shape as
Q
and
P
,
but both centered at the origin. Moreover, by a change of variables, we have

\begin{split}\langle m_{Q},b_{P}\rangle&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}g(c_{Q}-y)h(y-c_{P})\,dy\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}g(x)h(c_{Q}-c_{P}-x)\,dx=(g*h)(c_{Q}-c_{P}).\end{split}

(8.20)

Let us immediately observe both
g
and
h
satisfy
the assumptions of Lemma
8.3
with parameters
(\vec{K},\vec{L}^{\star},\vec{M},\vec{N}^{\star})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\dagger},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\dagger})
in place of
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
.
Thus, from (
8.20
) and Lemma
8.3
, we conclude that

\displaystyle|\langle m_{P},b_{Q}\rangle|\lesssim 2^{-(\vec{l}-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}})_{+}\cdot(\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N}^{\star})-(\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}-\vec{l})_{+}\cdot(\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N}^{\dagger})}\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\Big(1+2^{j_{\nu}\wedge l_{\nu}}|(c_{Q}-c_{P})_{\nu}|\Big)^{-(M_{\nu}\wedge M_{\nu}^{\prime})}.

(8.21)

Let us note that

2^{-(l_{\nu}-j_{\nu})}=\frac{2^{-l_{\nu}}}{2^{-j_{\nu}}}=\frac{\ell(Q_{\nu})}{\ell(P_{\nu})},\qquad 2^{-(l_{\nu}-j_{\nu})_{+}}=\begin{cases}\ell(Q_{\nu})/\ell(P_{\nu}),&\ell(Q_{\nu})\leq\ell(P_{\nu}),\\
1,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and analogously with
2^{-(j_{\nu}-l_{\nu})_{+}}
. Using these identities and comparing with (
7.1
), we recognise the right-hand side of (
8.21
) as

\mathrm{RHS}\eqref{from B1}=b_{\vec{n};P,Q}^{\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F}},\quad\vec{D}=\vec{M}\wedge\vec{M}^{\prime},\quad\vec{E}=\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N}^{\dagger},\quad\vec{F}=\frac{\vec{n}}{2}+\vec{N}^{\star}.

Substituting the values of
\vec{N}^{\star}
and
\vec{N}^{\dagger}
from (
8.19
), this finishes the proof of Lemma
8.4
.
∎

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,
r:=r(\pi,\vec{p},\vec{q})\wedge 1
,

\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
,
and
V
be a matrix weight such that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs
to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
).
For both
i=1,2
, let
\{m^{(i)}_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
and
\{b^{(i)}_{P}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
be families of
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
and
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
-molecules, respectively, where the parameters

\vec{K},\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{M},\vec{M}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)^{k},\quad\vec{N},\vec{N}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)^{k},\quad\vec{L},\vec{L}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}^{k}

satisfy

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vec{K}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}>\vec{n}+(\tau\vec{n}+\vec{s})_{+},\\
\vec{K}^{\prime}>\vec{n}+[(\tfrac{1}{r}-1)\vec{n}-\vec{s}]_{+},\end{cases}\qquad\vec{M},\vec{M}^{\prime}>(\tfrac{1}{r}+\tau)\vec{n},

(8.22)

and

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\vec{L}+\vec{1}\phantom{{}^{\prime}},\ \vec{N}^{\prime}>\tau\vec{n}+\vec{s},\\
\vec{L}^{\prime}+\vec{1},\ \vec{N}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}>(\frac{1}{r}-1)\vec{n}-\vec{s}.\end{cases}

(8.23)

Then

(i)

if
\varphi
a Littlewood–Paley function on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
, then each of the matrices

\Big\{\Big\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\}_{P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},\quad\Big\{\Big\langle m_{P}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}\Big\rangle\Big\}_{P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},\quad\Big\{\Big\langle\varphi_{P},b_{Q}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\}_{P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal (Definition
7.15
);

(ii)

if
t:=\{t_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, then

s_{P}:=\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle m_{Q}^{(2)},b_{R}^{(2)}\Big\rangle t_{R}

converges unconditionally for each
P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
and
s:=\{s_{P}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
satisfies

\|s\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\leq C\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)},

where the positive constant
C
is independent of
t
,

\{m_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
, and
\{b_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
.

Proof.

(
i
): Since the Littlewood–Paley functions
\varphi_{Q},\varphi_{P}
are molecules with any parameters that we like, it is enough to consider the first matrix. Our plan is to apply Lemma
8.4
. Note that the condition
\vec{K},\vec{K}^{\prime}>\vec{n}
of that lemma is immediate from assumption (
8.22
). Hence, Lemma
8.4
implies that

\{\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\rangle\}_{P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
is
(\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F})
-almost diagonal with parameters
\vec{D},\vec{E},\vec{F}
as in (
8.18
).

In order to be
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal, by Definition
7.15
, these parameters need to satisfy (
7.8
). Checking these conditions from the
assumptions (
8.22
) and (
8.23
) is straightforward.
This concludes the proof of part (
i
).

(
ii
):
By (
i
), we find that both

\{|\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\rangle|\}_{P,Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

and
\{|\langle m_{Q}^{(2)},b_{R}^{(2)}\rangle|\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

are
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal.
Then Lemma
7.14
guarantees that the composition

b_{P,R}:=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big|\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\rangle\Big|\Big|\langle m_{Q}^{(2)},b_{R}^{(2)}\rangle\Big|

also defines an

\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal

B:=\{b_{P,R}\}_{P,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
.
Now Theorem
7.12
implies the absolute convergence

\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big|\Big\langle m_{P}^{(1)},b_{Q}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big|\Big|\Big\langle m_{Q}^{(2)},b_{R}^{(2)}\Big\rangle\Big||t_{R}|=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}b_{P,R}|t_{R}|<\infty

and the claimed quasi-norm estimate for
s
.
This finishes the proof of (
ii
) and hence Theorem
8.5
.
∎

The following definition, motivated by Theorem
8.5
, is analogous to
[
14
, Definition 3.10]
, which in turn is inspired by the classical definition
[
63
, pp. 56–57]
.

Definition 8.6
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\tau\in[0,\infty)
, and
\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
.
Assume that
\vec{K},\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{M},\vec{M}^{\prime}\in[0,\infty)^{k},

\vec{L},\vec{L}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}^{k},
and
\vec{N},\vec{N}^{\prime}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
.
Whenever the corresponding parameters satisfy (
8.22
) and (
8.23
), then

(i)

a
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
-molecule is called an
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecule
;

(ii)

a
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
-molecule is called an
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecule
;

(iii)

a family of
(\vec{K},\vec{L},\vec{M},\vec{N})
-molecules is called a
family of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecules
;

(iv)

a family of
(\vec{K}^{\prime},\vec{L}^{\prime},\vec{M}^{\prime},\vec{N}^{\prime})
-molecules is called a
family of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecules
.

Remark 8.7
.

Theorem
8.5
can be restated in the language of Definition
8.6
by saying that conclusions (
i
) and (
ii
) hold whenever
\{m_{P}^{(i)}\}_{P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
and
\{b^{(i)}_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
are families of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecules and
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecules, respectively.

If
f\in\dot{A}^{s,\tau}_{p,q}(V)
and
\Phi
is an analysis molecule,
then
f\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{C}^{m})
.
However,
\Phi
need not belong to
\mathscr{S}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
and hence the pairing
\langle f,\Phi\rangle
requires justification;
see
[
63
, p. 155]
or
[
10
, Lemma 5.7]
.

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,

\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
,
and
V
be a matrix weight such that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs
to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
).
Let
f\in\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
and
\Phi
be an

\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecule on a cube
P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Then, for every Littlewood–Paley pair
(\varphi,\psi)
on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

\langle f,\Phi\rangle:=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},\Phi\rangle

(8.24)

is well defined: the series above converges absolutely and its value is independent of the choices of
\varphi
and
\psi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(\varphi^{(i)},\psi^{(i)})
, for
i=1,2
,
are two Littlewood–Paley pairs on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
.
Then

\{t_{R}\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}:=\Big\{\Big\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\}_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)

by Corollary
6.20
.
By Theorem
8.5
(
ii
) (or its reformulation in Remark
8.7
) applied to
this sequence, the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecules
m_{P}^{(1)}:=\Phi
and
m^{(2)}_{Q}:=\varphi^{(2)}_{Q}
, and the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecules
b_{S}^{(i)}:=\psi_{S}^{(3-i)}
,
S\in\{Q,R\}
, we find that

\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\Big\rangle

(8.25)

converges absolutely. By Lemma
5.3
,
the claim (
8.24
) holds if either
\Phi
or
f

is replaced by a test function
\Psi\in\mathscr{S}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Thus, if we sum up the double series (
8.25
) in one or the other order,
as justified by the absolute convergence that we just showed,
we then conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\Phi\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big(\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\Big\rangle\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big(\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle f,\varphi_{R}^{(1)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{R}^{(1)},\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\Big\rangle\Big)\Big\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\Big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\langle f,\varphi_{Q}^{(2)}\Big\rangle\Big\langle\psi_{Q}^{(2)},\Phi\Big\rangle.

This proves both the absolute convergence of the right-hand side of (
8.24
)
and its independence of
\varphi
and
\psi
,
which completes the proof of Lemma
8.8
.
∎

Applying Theorem
8.5
, Lemma
8.8
, and a method pioneered by
Frazier and Jawerth in the proofs of
[
63
, Theorems 3.5 and 3.7]
,
we obtain the following conclusion.

Theorem 8.9
.

Let
\vec{p}\in(0,\infty)^{k}
,
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
,
\vec{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,

\pi\in S_{[2k]}
be admissible for
(\vec{p},\vec{q})
,
and
V
be a matrix weight such that
(\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi,V)
belongs
to one of the main cases (Definition
7.3
).

(i)

If
\{m_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is a family of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-analysis molecules, each on the cube indicated by its subscript,
then there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for every
f\in\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
,

\Big\|\Big\{\Big\langle f,m_{Q}\Big\rangle\Big\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\leq C\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}.

(ii)

If
\{b_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is a family of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecules,
each on the cube indicated by its subscript,
then, for every
t:=\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\in\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, there exist
f\in\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
such that

f=\sum_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}t_{Q}b_{Q}

in
\mathscr{S}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{C}^{m})
and a positive constant
C
,
independent of
\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

and
\{b_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
, such that

\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\leq C\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}.

Proof.

We first show (
i
).
Let
(\varphi,\psi)
be a Littlewood–Paley pair on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
.
By Lemma
8.8
, we find that, for every
Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\langle f,\varphi_{R}\rangle\langle\psi_{R},m_{Q}\rangle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\langle\psi_{R},m_{Q}\rangle\Big(S_{\varphi}f\Big)_{R}.

(8.26)

Using Theorem
8.5
(or better, its reformulation in Remark
8.7
), we conclude that

B:=\{\langle\psi_{R},m_{Q}\rangle\}_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}

is an
\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-almost diagonal operator.
From this, we infer that

\begin{split}\Big\|\{\langle f,m_{Q}\rangle\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}&=\Big\|B\Big(S_{\varphi}f\Big)\Big\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}\qquad\text{by \eqref{91}}\\
&\lesssim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}\qquad\text{by Theorem \ref{AD st}}\\
&\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}}\qquad\text{by Corollary \ref{phiV}}.\end{split}

This finishes the proof of (
i
).

Now, we prove (
ii
). By Theorem
8.5
(
ii
) (or its reformulation in Remark
8.7
), with

\phi\in\mathscr{S}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
in place of the
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}
-synthesis molecule
m_{P}
,
we find the absolute convergence of

\sum_{Q,R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}t_{R}\langle b_{R},\varphi_{Q}\rangle\langle\psi_{Q},\phi\rangle=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}t_{R}\langle b_{R},\phi\rangle=:\langle f,\phi\rangle,

where the first identity follows from summing first over
Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

(as we can, by absolute convergence) and applying Lemma
5.3
.
In particular, the sum of the series is a well defined element

f\in\mathscr{S}_{0}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}};\mathbb{C}^{m})
. Taking
\phi=\varphi_{P}
,
we have
\langle f,\varphi_{P}\rangle=(S_{\varphi}f)_{P}
, and we deduce

\|f\|_{\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\sim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}\lesssim\|t\|_{\dot{a}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)}

from Corollary
6.20
in the first step
and the quasi-norm estimate of Theorem
8.5
in the second step.
This finishes the proof of Theorem
8.9
.
∎

Remark 8.10
.

Let
k=1
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
,
p\in(0,\infty)
,
q\in(0,\infty]
,

\pi\in S_{[2]}
, and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. If

\begin{split}\begin{cases}\tau=0&\text{or}\quad\tau=\frac{1}{p},\ q\in[p,\infty]\quad\text{or}\\
\tau=\frac{1}{p},\ q\in(0,p),\ \pi=F&\text{or}\quad\tau\in(\frac{1}{p},\infty),\end{cases}\end{split}

(8.27)

then, using Theorem
9.12
below, we conclude that
\dot{A}^{s,\tau}_{p,q,\pi}(V)=\dot{A}^{s,\tau,\mathrm{cube}}_{p,q,\pi}(V)
.
In this case, by comparing Theorem
8.9
in the present setting with

[
14
, Theorem 3.17]
, we observe that the two results have
the same formal structure.
However, the admissible ranges of the relevant indices are not identical even in one-parameter case.

The narrower ranges arise from the boundedness theory of almost
diagonal operators used in the proof of Theorem
8.9
,
which requires stronger index
conditions than those in
[
14
]
(see Remark
7.13
).
In contrast, by restricting the smoothness parameter
N

to the positive range
N>0
, several estimates can be carried out
more precisely, which leads to a relaxation of some index conditions.
For this reason, all results in the present article that depend
on almost diagonal operator bounds and molecular characterizations
involve slightly different index ranges from their one-parameter
counterparts.

On the other hand, for parameters outside the cases (
8.27
),
our present results deal with a scale of spaces that is different from the ones previously considered in the one-parameter case, as will be elaborated in Theorem
9.12
below.
In the multi-parameter context, Theorem
8.9
is completely new.

As an application of Theorem
8.9
,
we obtain the following conclusion (see
[
14
, Proposition 3.18]
