\big\|\big\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{n}(\frac{1}{r_{1}}-\frac{1}{r_{0}})}h_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}(x)\big\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{\ell^{1}}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\mathbf{1}_{R}(x)|R|^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}h_{R}\leq\sum_{R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\mathbf{1}_{R}(x)|R|^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}\langle H\rangle_{R}=:I_{\mathscr{D}}^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}H(x).

Hence, (
11.5
) follows from the inequality

\|I_{\mathscr{D}}^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}H\|_{L^{r_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim\|H\|_{L^{r_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},\qquad 1<r_{0}<r_{1}<\infty,

(11.6)

where
I_{\mathscr{D}}^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}
is a dyadic fractional integral-type operator.
While (
11.6
) is essentially known, we indicate an argument as follows:

For
s:=\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}\in(0,1)
,
the kernel of
I_{\mathscr{D}}^{s}
at
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}\times\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
is

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\\
x,y\in R\end{subarray}}|R|^{s-1}=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R_{\nu}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n_{\nu}})\\
x_{\nu},y_{\nu}\in R_{\nu}\end{subarray}}|R_{\nu}|^{s-1}\lesssim\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|^{n_{\nu}(s-1)},

noting that each sum over
R_{\nu}
is a decaying geometric series, where the leading term involves the minimal dyadic cube
R_{\nu}
that contains both
x_{\nu},y_{\nu}
and hence satisfies
\operatorname{diam}(R_{\nu})\geq|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|
. The right-hand side of the previous display is the kernel of the product fractional integral
I_{\vec{\alpha}}
with
\vec{\alpha}=s\vec{n}
, using the notation of
[
142
, p. 2]
. By the pointwise bound between the kernels and
[
142
, Corollary 1.2]
, it follows that

\|I^{\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}}}_{\mathscr{D}}H\|_{L^{r_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim\|I_{(\frac{1}{r_{0}}-\frac{1}{r_{1}})\vec{n}}H\|_{L^{r_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\lesssim\|H\|_{L^{r_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})};

in fact,
[
142
, Corollary 1.2]
characterises a weighted version of the last inequality, and the characterising condition in
[
142
, Corollary 1.2(b)]
is obvious for
w\equiv 1
.

This finishes the proof of (
11.6
). Thanks to the reductions leading to (
11.6
), we have also proved (
11.3
),
and hence Proposition
11.1
.
∎

Theorem 11.2
.

Let
\vec{s}_{0},\vec{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\tau\in[0,\infty)
, and
\vec{q}\in(0,\infty]^{k}
.
Assume that
V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
V_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
If
0<p_{0}<p_{1}<\infty
, then the following assertions are equivalent.

(i)

\dot{B}_{p_{0},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{B}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1});

(ii)

\dot{F}_{p_{0},\infty}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{F}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1});

(iii)

\dot{b}_{p_{0},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{b}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1});

(iv)

\dot{f}_{p_{0},\infty}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{f}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1});

(v)

there exists a positive constant
C

such that, for every
P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,

\displaystyle 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{1}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{1}})}\|V_{1}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{1}}(P)}\leq C2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{0}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{0}})}\|V_{0}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{0}}(P)}.

(11.7)

Proof.

By similarity, we only prove (
ii
)
\Longleftrightarrow
(
iv
)
\Longleftrightarrow
(
v
).
Now, we show (
v
)
\Longrightarrow
(
iv
).
Inequality (
11.7
) implies that

2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{1}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{1}})}|[V_{1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p_{1}}(P)}z|\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{0}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{0}})}|[V_{0}]_{\textit{\L}^{p_{0}}(P)}z|

and hence, for all sequences
\{t_{Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
,

2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot(\vec{s}_{1}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{1}})}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}|[V_{1}]_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}},p_{1}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|\lesssim 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot(\vec{s}_{0}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p_{0}})}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}|[V_{0}]_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}},p_{0}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|.

This, together with Proposition
11.1
,
further implies that

\displaystyle\|t\|_{\dot{f}_{p_{1},q}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}([V_{1}]_{p_{1}})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}|\Omega|^{-\tau}\big\|\big\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{1}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}|[V_{1}]_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}},p_{1}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|\big\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{L^{p_{1}}\ell^{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{\Omega\in\mathrm{Open}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}|\Omega|^{-\tau}\big\|\big\{2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{0}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}}|[V_{0}]_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}},p_{0}}t_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|\big\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}\big\|_{L^{p_{0}}\ell^{\infty}}=\|t\|_{\dot{f}_{p_{0},\infty}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}([V_{0}]_{p_{0}})},

which, together with Theorem
6.4
, further implies (
iv
).

Next, we prove (
iv
)
\Longrightarrow
(
ii
).
By the
\varphi
transform characterisation (Theorem
6.16
),

\|f\|_{\dot{F}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1})}\sim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{f}_{p_{1},\vec{q}}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}(V_{1})}\lesssim\|S_{\varphi}f\|_{\dot{f}_{p_{0},\infty}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})_{\mathrm{w}}}\sim\|f\|_{\dot{F}_{p_{0},\infty}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}(V_{0})}

and hence (
ii
) holds.

Finally, we show (
ii
)
\Longrightarrow
(
v
). Let
\{\theta^{(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}})}\}_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}\in[2^{\vec{n}}-\vec{1}]}
be Meyer wavelets on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
.
For every
P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,
let
f:=z\theta_{P}^{(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}})}

for some
\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}_{0}\in[2^{\vec{n}}-\vec{1}]
.
By orthonormality, it follows that the wavelet coefficients of
f
are given by
\langle f,\theta_{Q}^{(\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}})}\rangle=z\delta_{\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}_{0},\vec{\textit{\char 16\relax}}}\delta_{P,Q}

From Meyer wavelet characterisation (Theorem
8.20
) of
\dot{A}^{\vec{s},\tau}_{\vec{p},\vec{q},\pi}(V)
, it follows that

\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{s}_{i},\tau}_{p_{i},\vec{q}_{i}}(V_{i})}\sim\|\{z\delta_{P,Q}\}_{Q\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|_{\dot{f}^{\vec{s}_{i},\tau}_{p_{i},\vec{q}_{i}}(V_{i})}\sim|P|^{-\tau}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot\vec{s}_{i}}\|z\widetilde{\mathbf{1}}_{P}\|_{L^{p_{i}}(V_{i})}=|P|^{-\tau-\frac{1}{2}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{i}-\vec{n}/p_{i})}\|V_{i}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{i}}(P)}

Using this with both
i=0,1
and
\vec{q}_{0}=\infty
,
\vec{q}_{1}=\vec{q}
, the assumption (
ii
) implies

|P|^{-\tau-\frac{1}{2}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{1}-\vec{n}/p_{1})}\|V_{1}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{1}}(P)}\sim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{s}_{1},\tau}_{p_{1},\vec{q}_{1}}(V_{1})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{s}_{0},\tau}_{p_{0},\vec{q}_{0}}(V_{0})}\sim|P|^{-\tau-\frac{1}{2}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{s}_{0}-\vec{n}/p_{0})}\|V_{0}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p_{0}}(P)}.

Cancelling out the common factors
|P|^{-\tau-\frac{1}{2}}
, we get (
v
)
and finish the proof of Theorem
11.2
.
∎

Remark 11.3
.

(i)

Theorem
11.2
is new in the multi-parameter case. In the one-parameter case, it essentially agrees with
[
18
, Theorem 6.11]
for the usual Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin space with
\tau=0
, and slightly more generally (see Theorem
9.12
), while in general it deals with a different scale of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces. We note that
[
18
, Theorem 6.11]
also addresses more general
\mathscr{A}_{p,\infty}
(instead of
\mathscr{A}_{p}
) matrix weights, which have not been studied in the multi-parameter setting.

(ii)

In the one-parameter case, another approach to Theorem
11.2
is via
an interpolation inequality. However, this inequality is no longer
valid in the multi-parameter case (see Proposition
11.5
below).
That is why we first use the boundedness of the
dyadic fractional integral-type operator to prove Proposition
11.1
,
and then apply Proposition
11.1
to prove Theorem
11.2
.

Lemma 11.4
.

For
a,b\in(0,\infty)
and
A,B\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\min(2^{ja}A,2^{-jb}B)\sim A^{\frac{b}{a+b}}B^{\frac{a}{a+b}},

where the positive equivalence constants may depend on
a,b
, but independent of
A,B
.

Proof.

Note that
2^{ja}A\leq 2^{-jb}B
if and only if
2^{j}\leq(B/A)^{\frac{1}{a+b}}
. Hence

\operatorname{LHS}=\sum_{2^{j}\leq(B/A)^{\frac{1}{a+b}}}2^{ja}A+\sum_{2^{j}>(B/A)^{\frac{1}{a+b}}}2^{-jb}B\sim(B/A)^{\frac{a}{a+b}}A+(B/A)^{-\frac{b}{a+b}}B\sim A^{\frac{b}{a+b}}B^{\frac{a}{a+b}}.

This finishes the proof of Lemma
11.4
.
∎

Proposition 11.5
.

Let
\vec{s}_{0},\vec{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Assume that
\theta\in(0,1)
,

\vec{s}:=\theta\vec{s}_{0}+(1-\theta)\vec{s}_{1}
, and
q\in(0,\infty]
.

(i)

If
q=\infty
or if
k=1
,
q\in(0,\infty)
,
and
\vec{s}_{0}\neq\vec{s}_{1}
, then
there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for every sequence
\{c_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
in
\mathbb{C}
,

\Big[\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}2^{(\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s})q}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|^{q}\Big]^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\Big[\sup_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{0}}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|\Big]^{\theta}\Big[\sup_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{1}}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|\Big]^{1-\theta}.

(11.8)

(ii)

If
k\in\mathbb{N}\cap[2,\infty)
and
q\in(0,\infty)
,
then (
11.8
) is not valid for any
\vec{s}_{0},\vec{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Proof.

(
i
):
Let first
q=\infty
. Then

2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|=(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{0}}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|)^{\theta}(2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{1}}|a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}|)^{1-\theta},

and (
11.8
) with
q=\infty
follows by taking the supremum on both sides of the above equality over
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

Let then
k=1
and
q\in(0,\infty)
. We may assume by symmetry that
s_{0}<s_{1}
, and hence
s_{0}<s<s_{1}
. Moreover, we have
s_{1}-s=\theta(s_{1}-s_{0})
and
s-s_{0}=(1-\theta)(s_{1}-s_{0})
.

Let
A:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{js_{0}}|a_{j}|
and
B:=\sup_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{js_{1}}|a_{j}|
. Thus,
|a_{j}|\leq\min\{2^{-js_{0}}A,2^{-ja_{1}}B\}
,
and then

\begin{split}\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{false eq}^{q}&\leq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\min\{2^{j(s-s_{0})q}A^{q},2^{-j(s_{1}-s)q}B^{q}\}\\
&\sim(A^{q})^{\frac{(s_{1}-s)q}{(s_{1}-s_{0})q}}(B^{q})^{\frac{(s-s_{0})q}{(s_{1}-s_{0})q}}\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref{sum-min}}\\
&=A^{\frac{(s_{1}-s)q}{s_{1}-s_{0}}}B^{\frac{(s-s_{0})q}{s_{1}-s_{0}}}=A^{\theta q}B^{(1-\theta)q}=\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{false eq}^{q}.\end{split}

This finishes the proof of (i).

(
ii
):
To avoid complicated indexing, let
\vec{r}:=\vec{s}_{0}
and
\vec{t}:=\vec{s}_{1}
. We write all vectors
\vec{u}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
as
\vec{u}=(u_{1},\vec{u}\,^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}
.
We may assume by symmetry that
r_{1}<t_{1}
and hence
r_{1}<s_{1}<t_{1}
.

Given
\vec{s}_{0},\vec{s}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, we define the sequence
a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}:=\min\{2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{0}},2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{s}_{1}}\}=\min\{2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{r}},2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\cdot\vec{t}}\}
. For both
h=0,1
, then clearly
2^{-\vec{h}\cdot\vec{s}_{h}}a_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}}\leq 1
for all
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
with equality when
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}=\vec{0}
, hence
\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{false eq}=1
.

On the other hand, writing
\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}=(\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{1},\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}
, we have

\begin{split}\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{false eq}^{q}&=\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}\sum_{j_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\min\Big\{2^{j_{1}(s_{1}-r_{1})q}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\cdot(\vec{s}\,^{\prime}-\vec{r}\,^{\prime})q},2^{-j_{1}(t_{1}-s_{1})q}2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\cdot(\vec{t}\,^{\prime}-\vec{s}\,^{\prime})q}\Big\}\\
&\sim\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\cdot(\vec{s}\,^{\prime}-\vec{r}\,^{\prime})q\frac{t_{1}-s_{1}}{t_{1}-r_{1}}}2^{-\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\cdot(\vec{t}\,^{\prime}-\vec{s}\,^{\prime})q\frac{s_{1}-r_{1}}{t_{1}-r_{1}}}\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref{sum-min}}\\
&=\sum_{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k-1}}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}\,^{\prime}\cdot\vec{u}^{\prime}}=\prod_{i=2}^{k}\Bigg(\sum_{j_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j_{i}u_{i}}\Bigg)\end{split}

for some
\vec{u}^{\prime}=(u_{2},\ldots,u_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
. (One can see an exact formula for
\vec{u}^{\prime}
above, but this is irrelevant.)

It is clear that the doubly infinite geometric series
\sum_{j_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{j_{i}u_{i}}
diverges for all values of
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence
\operatorname{LHS}\eqref{false eq}=\infty
, while we already observed that
\operatorname{RHS}\eqref{false eq}=1
.
This finishes the proof of (ii) and hence Proposition
11.5
.
∎

11.2.
Applications to matrix-weighted
L^{p}
and Sobolev spaces

Based on the relationships with other known function spaces
established in Section
9
, even when the results obtained in the previous
sections are applied to some specific function spaces, we can also
obtain some further interesting consequences, for example,

(i)

using the boundedness of lifting operators (Theorem
6.21
),
we can obtain the boundedness of fractional integral operators on
Lebesgue spaces with
\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
weights, and

(ii)

applying the Sobolev-type embedding of Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin-type spaces in Theorem
11.2
, we can obtain the Sobolev embedding theorem.

For every
\vec{\sigma}\in(\vec{0},\vec{n}):=(0,n_{1})\times\cdots\times(0,n_{k})
,
the
multi-parameter fractional integral operator

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}

is defined by setting, for all suitable functions
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(f)(x):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}\frac{f(y)}{|x-y|^{\vec{n}-\vec{\sigma}}}\,dy,

where
|x-y|^{\vec{n}-\vec{\sigma}}:=\prod_{\nu=1}^{k}|x_{\nu}-y_{\nu}|^{n_{\nu}-\sigma_{\nu}}
.
Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
. A matrix weight
V
is called
an
\mathscr{A}_{p,q}
-matrix weight
if
V
satisfies that

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}:=\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}<\infty.

Theorem 11.6
.

Let
\vec{\sigma}\in(\vec{0},\vec{n})
and
p,q\in(1,\infty)
satisfy

\big(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\big)\vec{n}=\vec{\sigma}.

(11.9)

If
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, then there exists
a positive constant
C
such that, for every
f\in L^{p}(V)
,

\|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{L^{q}(V)}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}.

(11.10)

Remark 11.7
.

In the case where
k=2
and
m=1
,
Theorem
11.6
coincides with a part of
[
137
, Theorem 5]
,
in which Sawyer and Wang established several equivalent characterizations of the boundedness of multi-parameter fractional integral operators.
In particular, they showed that if

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}:\ L^{p}(V)\to L^{q}(V)
is bounded,
then condition (
11.9
) holds.

To prove Theorem
11.6
, we need some lemmas.

Lemma 11.8
.

If
1<p<q<\infty
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
then
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\cap\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

Proof.

By Hölder’s inequality, we find that

\displaystyle[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}=\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}\leq\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}=[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})},

and hence
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Similarly, we obtain
V\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
11.8
.
∎

The following lemma provides an equivalent characterization of matrix
\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
weights.

Lemma 11.9
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
and
V
be a matrix weight.
Then

[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\sim\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)},

where the positive equivalence constants are independent of
V
.

Proof.

Note that
Y=\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)
is a Banach lattice with a dual pair
Y^{\prime}=\textit{\L}^{p}(P)
, with respect to the normalised integration
\langle\ \rangle_{P}:=\fint_{P}
over
P
.
Hence it follows from Lemma
2.5
(ii) and a change of variables that

\displaystyle\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}\sim\|f\mapsto V\langle V^{-1}f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}=\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)}.

Taking the supremum over
P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
on both sides of
the above equation yields the desired estimate.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
11.9
.
∎

Applying Lemma
11.9
, we obtain
a useful relation between scalar weights and matrix weights.

Lemma 11.10
.

Let
p,q\in(1,\infty)
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
Then there exists a positive constant
C
such that,
for every
M\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{m\times m}\setminus\{O_{m}\}
, we have

[|VM|]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\leq C[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
11.9
, we have

\begin{split}[|VM|]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}&\sim\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,|VM|)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,|VM|)},\\
[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}&\sim\sup_{P\in\mathrm{Rect}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle F\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)}.\end{split}

(11.11)

Given
M\in\mathbb{C}_{+}^{m\times m}\setminus\{O_{m}\}
and
f\in\textit{\L}^{p}(P,|VM|)
,
we consider the matrix-valued function
F:=f(\cdot)M
. Then

\begin{split}\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P,|VM|)}&=\big\|\mathbf{1}_{P}|V\langle f(\cdot)M\rangle_{P}|\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}=\|\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f(\cdot)M\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)}\\
&\leq\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle F\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)}\|f(\cdot)M\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)},\end{split}

where

\|f(\cdot)M\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)}=\big\|f(\cdot)|V(\cdot)M|\big\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}=\|f\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P,|VM|)}.

This shows that

\|f\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle f\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,|VM|)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,|VM|)}\leq\|F\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{P}\langle F\rangle_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P,V)\to\textit{\L}^{q}(P,V)},

which, together with (
11.11
), then completes the proof of Lemma
11.10
.
∎

The following lemma shows that the fractional integral operator
\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}

and the lifting operator
\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}
, defined in (
6.20
),
are equivalent in the sense of distributions.

Lemma 11.11
.

Let
\vec{\sigma}\in(\vec{0},\vec{n})
and
p,q\in(1,\infty)
satisfy (
11.9
).
Then there exists a positive constant
C
,
depending only on
\vec{\sigma}
and
\vec{n}
, such that,
for every
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
f\in L^{p}(V)
,

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f
is well defined and

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f=C\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f

in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f
is well defined.
By Tonelli’s theorem and Holder’s inequality, we conclude that, for every
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|f|)(x)|\varphi(x)|dx&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}|f(x)|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|\varphi|)(x)dx\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}|V^{-1}(x)|\,|V(x)f(x)|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|\varphi|)(x)dx\\
&\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}\|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|\varphi|)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(w)},\end{split}

(11.12)

where
w:=|V^{-1}|
.
From the definition of
\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})

and Lemmas
2.5
(i) and
11.10
, it follows that

\displaystyle V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\Longleftrightarrow V^{-1}\in\mathscr{A}_{q^{\prime},p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\Longrightarrow w\in\mathscr{A}_{q^{\prime},p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}).

Note that

\Bigg(\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}\Bigg)\vec{n}=\Bigg(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}\Bigg)\vec{n}=\vec{\sigma}.

Using the boundedness of
\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}
(see
[
137
, Theorem 5]
), we find that

\displaystyle\|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|\varphi|)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(w)}\lesssim\|\varphi\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}(w)}<\infty,

which, togeter with (
11.12
), further implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(|f|)(x)|\varphi(x)|dx<\infty.

(11.13)

Therefore,
\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f
is well defined.

Moreover, using a straightforward multi-parameter
extension of
[
56
, p. 88]
,
we find that there exists a positive constant
C
,
depending only on
\vec{\sigma}
and
\vec{n}
, such that,
for every
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and almost every

x\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}\varphi(x)=C\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}\varphi(x)
.
From this, (
11.13
), and Fubini’s theorem,
we deduce that,
for every
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,

\displaystyle\langle\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(f)(x)\varphi(x)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}}f(x)\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}(\varphi)(x)dx

\displaystyle=\langle f,\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}\varphi\rangle=\langle f,C\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}\varphi\rangle=C\langle\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f,\varphi\rangle,

and hence
\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f=C\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f

in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. This finishes the proof of Lemma
11.11
.
∎

Now, we can prove Theorem
11.6
.

Proof of Theorem
11.6
.

Let
f\in L^{p}(V)
.
Note that
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})\cap\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
(see Lemma
11.8
).
By Lemma
11.11
, Theorem
9.1
, and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, we find that

\|\mathscr{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{L^{q}(V)}\sim\|\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{\dot{F}^{0,0}_{q,2}(V)}.

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}}
,

P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,

|V(x)z|\leq\fint_{P}|V(x)V(y)^{-1}|\,|V(y)z|\,dy\leq\|V(x)V(\cdot)^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}\|V(\cdot)z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)},

which, together with (
11.9
), further implies that

\displaystyle 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{0}-\frac{\vec{n}}{q})}\|z\mathbf{1}_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(V)}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{\sigma}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p})}\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}\|z\mathbf{1}_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(V)}

\displaystyle\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})}2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{\sigma}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p})}\|z\mathbf{1}_{P}\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(V)}.

From this and Theorem
11.2
, we deduce that

\|\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{\dot{F}^{0,0}_{q,2}(V)}\lesssim\|\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,\infty}(V)}\leq\|\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,2}(V)},

where the last inequality is obvious.
Applying Theorems
6.21
and
9.1
and
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
, we obtain

\|\dot{I}_{\vec{\sigma}}f\|_{\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,2}(V)}\sim\|f\|_{\dot{F}^{0,0}_{p,2}(V)}\sim\|f\|_{L^{p}(V)}.

Combining the above results yields (
11.10
).
This finishes the proof of Theorem
11.6
.
∎

Unlike Theorem
11.6
, the Sobolev embedding below
follows directly from Theorems
11.2
and
9.4
.

Theorem 11.12
.

Let
\vec{\sigma},\vec{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
,
1<p<q<\infty
, and

V_{0}\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
,
V_{1}\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
.
If, for every
P\in\mathscr{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
,

\displaystyle 2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{l}-\frac{\vec{n}}{q})}\|V_{1}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}\leq C2^{\vec{\textit{\char 17\relax}}_{P}\cdot(\vec{\sigma}-\frac{\vec{n}}{p})}\|V_{0}z\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)},

(11.14)

then
\dot{W}^{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{W}^{\vec{l}}_{q}(V_{1})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
9.4
, we conclude that

\dot{W}^{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}(V_{0})=\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,2}(V_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\dot{W}^{\vec{l}}_{q}(V_{1})=\dot{F}^{\vec{l},0}_{q,2}(V_{1}).

Condition (
11.14
) ensures that condition (
v
) of Theorem
11.2
holds with

\vec{s}_{0}=\vec{\sigma},\quad\vec{s}_{1}=\vec{l},\quad\tau=0,\quad p_{0}=p,\quad p_{1}=q.

Since the conditions (
v
) and (
ii
) of Theorem
11.2
are equivalent, we obtain

\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,\infty}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{F}^{\vec{l},0}_{q,2}(V_{1})
.
Together the simple embedding

\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,2}(V_{0})\hookrightarrow\dot{F}^{\vec{\sigma},0}_{p,\infty}(V_{0})
, this
finishes the proof of Theorem
11.12
.
∎

Corollary 11.13
.

Let
\vec{\sigma},\vec{l}\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
and
0<p<q<\infty
satisfy

(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q})\vec{n}=\vec{\sigma}-\vec{l}.

(11.15)

Let
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. Then
\dot{W}^{\vec{\sigma}}_{p}(V)\hookrightarrow\dot{W}^{\vec{l}}_{q}(V)
.

Proof.

The plan is to verify the assumptions of Theorem
11.12
with
V_{0}=V_{1}=V
. By Lemma
11.8
, the assumption
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
implies both
V_{0}:=V\in\mathscr{A}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
and
V_{1}:=V\in\mathscr{A}_{q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
. It remains to verify (
11.14
). Under the assumption (
11.15
), the powers of
2
on both sides of (
11.14
) are equal, so we are reduced to showing that

\|Vz\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}\lesssim\|Vz\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}.

(11.16)

To prove (
11.16
), we note that the assumption
V\in\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{\vec{n}})
also implies that

\begin{split}|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}|&=|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}|\\
&\lesssim|[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}|\quad\text{by Lemma \ref{inverses} with $u=p^{\prime}$ and }M=[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}\\
&=|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}[V^{-1}]_{\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}|\sim\|V,V^{-1}\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P),\textit{\L}^{p^{\prime}}(P)}\leq[V]_{\mathscr{A}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.\end{split}

Hence

\begin{split}\|Vz\|_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}\sim|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}z|\leq|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{q}(P)}[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}^{-1}|\cdot|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}z|\lesssim|[V]_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}z|\sim\|Vz\|_{\textit{\L}^{p}(P)}.\end{split}

This proves (
11.16
). Hence all the assumptions of Theorem
11.12
are satisfied with
V_{0}=V_{1}=V
, and hence also its conclusion, which agrees with the claim of the corollary that we are proving. This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Carleson’s counterexample revisited

Carleson’s counterexample
[
23
]
is a classical construction showing a delicate difference between versions of multi-parameter conditions defined by rectangles and by general open sets. To demonstrate the sharpness of some of our results in this work, and the necessity of using general open sets instead of rectangles in some formulations, we make use of both the original Carleson result and certain elaborations that we present in this appendix. For the setting of Carleson’s example, we follow the presentation of Tao
[
143
]
. The core of the construction is two-dimensional; a version in higher dimensions will be obtained as a simple corollary in the end.

A.1.
The core construction in two dimensions

Let
\mathscr{D}
be the collection of dyadic subrectangles of
[0,1)^{2}
. For all
P\in\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{R}\subset\mathscr{D}
, we denote

\mathscr{R}(P):=\{R\in\mathscr{R}:R\subseteq P\},\quad\chi_{\mathscr{R}}:=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{R}}\mathbf{1}_{R},\quad\sigma(\mathscr{R}):=|\bigcup_{R\in\mathscr{R}}R|.

Following
[
143
]
, we define:

Definition A.1
.

A
quilt
is a finite collection
\mathscr{R}\subset\mathscr{D}
such that

\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}(P)}\|_{1}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{R}(P)}|R|\leq|P|

(A.1)

for every
P\in\mathscr{D}
, with equality when
P=[0,1)^{2}
.

With this definition, the essence of Carleson’s classical counterexample is as follows:

Lemma A.2

(
[
143
]
, Lemma 7.2)
.

The exist quilts
\mathscr{R}
with arbitrarily small
\sigma(\mathscr{R})
.

To explain the elaboration of Lemma
A.2
that we need, let us first make a reformulation. Since every quilt has
\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}}\|_{1}=1
, Lemma
A.2
is equivalent to the claim that there exist quilts with arbitrarily large
\sigma(\mathscr{R})^{-1}\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}}\|_{1}
. Note that this quantity is the average of
\chi_{\mathscr{R}}
on its support. We will prove the following generalisation:

Proposition A.3
.

For every
p\in(0,\infty]
, the exist quilts
\mathscr{R}
with arbitrarily large
\sigma(\mathscr{R})^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}}\|_{p}
.

Note that
\sigma(\mathscr{R})^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}}\|_{p}
is increasing in
p
by Hölder’s inequality, and hence the range
p\in[1,\infty]
of Proposition
A.3
is immediate from Lemma
A.2
. The main point of Proposition
A.3
is the range
p\in(0,1)
. To prove Proposition
A.3
, we will revisit and elaborate the proof of Lemma
A.2
. Indeed, we will see that the same construction used to prove Lemma
A.2
already has the stronger properties of Proposition
A.3
, but verifying this requires additional effort.

The proof of Lemma
A.2
is based on iterating the following:

Lemma A.4

(
[
143
]
, proof of Lemma 7.2)
.

For every quilt
\mathscr{R}
, there is another quilt
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
such that

\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma(1-\tfrac{1}{4}\sigma),\qquad\sigma:=\sigma(\mathscr{R}),\quad\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma(\mathscr{R}^{\prime}).

Moreover, such
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
is obtained by the following Algorithm
A.5
.

We recall the full details of the algorithm in order to verify its additional properties.

Algorithm A.5

(
[
143
]
, proof of Lemma 7.2)
.

Input:
a finite collection
\mathscr{R}\subset\mathscr{D}
.

Output:
another finite collection
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\subset\mathscr{D}
constructed as follows:

Choose
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough such that the smallest side-length of every
R\in\mathscr{R}
is at least
2^{-N}
; this is possible, since
\mathscr{R}
is finite. For every
j=1,\ldots 2^{N+1}
, we consider the affine transformations

a_{j}:[0,1)\to[j-1,j)\cdot 2^{-N-1},\quad x\mapsto(j-1+x)\cdot 2^{-N-1}

and, in two dimensions,

A_{j}^{1}(x,y):=(a_{j}(x),y),\qquad A_{j}^{2}(x,y):=(x,a_{j}(y)).

Let

A_{j}^{i}(\mathscr{R}):=\{A_{j}^{i}(R):R\in\mathscr{R}\}

and

\mathscr{R}^{i}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{2^{N+1}}A_{j}^{i}(\mathscr{R}),\qquad\mathscr{S}^{i}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{2^{N+1}}A_{j}^{i}(\mathscr{R}),\qquad\mathscr{R}^{\prime}:=\mathscr{S}^{1}\cup\mathscr{S}^{2}.

This concludes the construction, and the algorithm outputs the collection
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
.

Lemma A.6
.

If
\mathscr{R}
is a quilt, then
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
constructed by Algorithm
A.5
is also a quilt.

Proof.

We first consider the inequality (
A.1
).

Let
P\in\mathscr{D}
have dimensions
2^{-k_{1}}\times 2^{-k_{2}}
. If
k_{1}\geq N+1
, then
P\subseteq A_{j}^{1}([0,1)^{2})
for a unique
j
. Then only rectangles from this
A_{j}^{1}(\mathscr{R})
and no rectangles from any
A_{j^{\prime}}^{2}(\mathscr{R})
may be contained in
P
; hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in A_{j}^{1}(\mathscr{R})\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{R}\\
A_{j}^{1}(R)\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|A_{j}^{1}(R)|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{R}\\
R\subseteq(A_{j}^{1})^{-1}(P)\end{subarray}}2^{-N-1}|R|.

Since
P\subseteq A_{j}^{1}([0,1)^{2})
, we find that
(A_{j}^{1})^{-1}(P)\subseteq[0,1)^{2}
is a dyadic rectangle, and property (
A.1
) of
\mathscr{R}
shows that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R\in\mathscr{R}\\
R\subseteq(A_{j}^{1})^{-1}(P)\end{subarray}}|R|\leq|(A_{j}^{1})^{-1}(P)|=2^{N+1}|P|.

A substitution back gives the desired inequality (
A.1
) for
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
and such
P
. The case of
k_{2}\geq N+1
follows by symmetry.

Suppose then that both
k_{1},k_{2}\leq N
. Then
P
is a disjoint union of dyadic rectangles
P^{1}_{i}
of dimensions
2^{-N-1}\times 2^{-k_{2}}
. If
S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}
is contained in
P
, then it is contained in one of these
P^{1}_{i}
. By the first part of the proof applied to
P^{1}_{i}
in place of
P
, it follows that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}\\
S\subseteq P^{1}_{i}\end{subarray}}|S|\leq|P^{1}_{i}|.

On the other hand, half of these rectangles
P^{1}_{i}
contain no
S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}
. Thus

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|=\sum_{i}^{\prime}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}\\
S\subseteq P_{i}^{1}\end{subarray}}|S|\leq\sum_{i}^{\prime}|P^{1}_{i}|=\frac{1}{2}|P|,

where
\sum_{i}^{\prime}
designates the sum over those rectangles
P_{i}^{1}
that may contain some
S\in\mathscr{S}^{1}
. By symmetry, it follows that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{S}^{2}\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|\leq\frac{1}{2}|P|,

and hence

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{R}^{\prime}\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathscr{S}^{i}\\
S\subseteq P\end{subarray}}|S|\leq|P|.

This shows that
\mathscr{R}
satisfies (
A.1
) for all
P\in\mathscr{D}
.

Let us finally consider the equality in (
A.1
) when
P=[0,1)^{2}
. In this case, we sum over all rectangles in
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
. Note that
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
is a disjoint union
\mathscr{S}^{1}\cup\mathscr{S}^{2}
, where each
\mathscr{S}^{i}
is a disjoint union of
2^{N}
collections
A^{i}_{j}(\mathscr{R})
, and each
A^{i}_{j}(\mathscr{R})
consists of rectangles that are scaled-down versions of all
R\in\mathscr{R}
with factor
2^{-N-1}
in one or the other coordinate direction. Thus, in total,
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
consists of
2\cdot 2^{N}=2^{N+1}
scaled-down copies of itself, with the common scaling factor
2^{-N-1}
. It is clear that the areas of these scaled down copies add up to the same total as the areas of all
R\in\mathscr{R}
, which is
1
, since
\mathscr{R}
is a quilt. Hence
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
is also a quilt, as claimed.
∎

For a further analysis of the transformation
\mathscr{R}\mapsto\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
through Algorithm
A.5
, it is convenient to adopt a probabilistic interpretation.
We note that such an interpretation is mentioned in passing already in
[
23
, page 8]
, but we will make more systematic use of it further below.
Let

\delta:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{2^{N+1}}\mathbf{1}_{[j-1,j)\cdot 2^{-N-1}},\quad\delta_{1}(x,y):=\delta(x),\qquad\delta_{2}(x,y):=\delta(y)

(A.2)

and

f:=\chi_{\mathscr{R}}=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{R}}\mathbf{1}_{R},\quad f_{i}:=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{R}^{i}}\mathbf{1}_{S},\quad g_{i}:=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{S}^{i}}\mathbf{1}_{S}=\delta_{i}f_{i},\quad g:=g_{1}+g_{2}=\chi_{\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}.

(A.3)

Lemma A.7
.

Viewing
f,f_{i},\delta_{i}
in (
A.2
)–(
A.3
) as random variables on the probability space
[0,1)^{2}
with the Lebesgue measure (denoted by
\mathbb{P}
simply to emphasise the probabilistic interpretation),

(1)

\mathbb{P}(\delta_{i}=0)=\mathbb{P}(\delta_{i}=1)=\frac{1}{2}
;

(2)

f_{1}
and
f_{2}
have the same distribution as
f
;

(3)

f_{1},f_{2},\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
are independent random variables.

Proof.

Statement (
1
) is immediate, and so
is (
2
) from the fact that each
f_{i}
is disjoint sum of rescaled copies of
f
. To see the independence (
3
) we observe how each of the functions depends on the digits of the binary expansions of
(x,y)\in[0,1)^{2}
:

We note that

f_{1}(x,y)=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{N+1}}\sum_{I_{1}\times I_{2}\in\mathscr{R}}\mathbf{1}_{a^{1}_{j}(I_{1})\times I_{2}}(x,y)=\sum_{I_{1}\times I_{2}\in\mathscr{R}}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{2^{N+1}}\mathbf{1}_{a^{1}_{j}(I_{1})}(x)\Big)\mathbf{1}_{I_{2}}(y).

Here, the summation over
j
is
2^{-N-1}
-periodic. Hence, in the binary expansion

x=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k}x_{k},\qquad x_{k}\in\{0,1\},

it is independent of
x_{k}
for
k\leq N+1
, i.e., it only depends on
(x_{k})_{k>N+1}
. On the other hand, for
I_{1}\times I_{2}\in\mathscr{R}
, the component
I_{2}
is a dyadic interval of length at least
2^{-N}
. Hence the value of
\mathbf{1}_{I_{2}}(y)
only depends on
(y_{\ell})_{\ell\leq N}
. Thus, we have

f_{1}=f_{1}((x_{k})_{k>N+1},(y_{\ell})_{\ell\leq N}),\qquad f_{2}=f_{2}((x_{k})_{k\leq N},(y_{\ell})_{\ell>N+1}),

where the second identity follows by symmetry.

On the other hand, it is immediate that the value of
\delta(z)
is determined by the single binary digit
z_{N+1}
, and hence

\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(x_{N+1}),\qquad\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(y_{N+1}).

By inspection of the previous two displays, we find that all the four functions
f_{1},f_{2},\delta_{1}
, and
\delta_{2}
depend on disjoint subsets of the independent variables
(x_{k},y_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
. This proves the claimed independence, and completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

With the probabilistic language of Lemma
A.7
, we give:

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.

Given a quilt
\mathscr{R}
, we produce
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
by Algorithm
A.5
. By Lemma
A.6
,
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
is another quilt. It remains to show the claimed relation between
\sigma:=\sigma(\mathscr{R})
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma(\mathscr{R}^{\prime})
.

With notation as in (
A.2
)–(
A.3
), and using the probabilistic notation
\mathbb{P}
(probability) for the Lebesgue measure on
[0,1)^{2}
and
\mathbb{E}
(expectation) for the Lebesgue integral over
[0,1)^{2}
, we have

\mathbb{E}f=\sum_{R\in\mathscr{R}}|R|,\qquad\mathbb{E}g=\sum_{S\in\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}|S|,\

and

\mathbb{P}(f>0)=\Big|\bigcup_{R\in\mathscr{R}}R\Big|=\sigma,\qquad\mathbb{P}(g>0)=\Big|\bigcup_{S\in\mathscr{R}^{\prime}}S\Big|=\sigma^{\prime}.

From this probabilistic interpretation and the properties established in Lemma
A.7
, we immediately deduce that

\mathbb{E}g=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}g_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}(\delta_{i}f_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\mathbb{E}(\delta_{i})\mathbb{E}(f_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}f=\mathbb{E}f

and

\mathbb{P}(g>0)=\mathbb{P}(g_{1}>0)+\mathbb{P}(g_{2}>0)-\mathbb{P}(g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0),

where

\mathbb{P}(g_{i}>0)=\mathbb{P}(\delta_{i}>0,f_{i}>0)=\mathbb{P}(\delta_{i}>0)\mathbb{P}(f_{i}>0)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)

and

\mathbb{P}(g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0)=\mathbb{P}(g_{1}>0)\mathbb{P}(g_{2}>0)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0))^{2}.

Substituting back, we obtain

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(g>0)&=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)+\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)-(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0))^{2}\\
&=\mathbb{P}(f>0)-\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)^{2}=\mathbb{P}(f>0)(1-\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)),\end{split}

(A.4)

which translates to the claimed relation
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma(1-\frac{1}{4}\sigma)
and completes the proof of Lemma
A.4
.
∎

The deduction of Lemma
A.2
from Lemma
A.4
would now be straightforward. However, we continue with a more delicate argument in order to obtain the sharper result of Proposition
A.3
, of which Lemma
A.2
is a special case. The following lemma contains the main novelty of the argument compared to
[
23
,
143
]
:

Lemma A.8
.

Let
\mathscr{R}
be a quilt and
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
be the new quilt produced by Algorithm
A.5
.
For all
p\in(0,1]
, using the notation (
A.2
)–(
A.3
), we have

\mathbb{E}(g^{p})\geq\Big(1-\frac{2-2^{p}}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)\Big)\mathbb{E}(f^{p})

and

\frac{\mathbb{E}(g^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(g>0)}\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}(f^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(f>0)}\Big(1+\frac{2^{p}-1}{3}\mathbb{P}(f>0)\Big).

Proof.

Consider the decomposition

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}g^{p}=\mathbb{E}(g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}&=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}=0\}}g_{2}^{p}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0\}}g_{1}^{p}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0\}}((g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}-g_{1}^{p})]\\
&=:I+II+III\end{split}

Here, using the independence and equi-distribution properties guaranteed by Lemma
A.7
,

\begin{split}I&=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}=0\}})\mathbb{E}(g_{2}^{p})=\mathbb{P}(g_{1}=0)\mathbb{E}(g_{2}^{p})=(1-\mathbb{P}(\delta_{1}f_{1}>0))\mathbb{E}(\delta_{2}f_{2})^{p}\\
&=(1-\mathbb{P}(\delta_{1}>0)\mathbb{P}(f_{1}>0))\mathbb{E}(\delta_{2})\mathbb{E}(f_{2}^{p})=(1-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0))\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}(f^{p})\end{split}

and

II=\mathbb{E}(g_{1}^{p})=\mathbb{E}(\delta_{1}f_{1})^{p}=\mathbb{E}(\delta_{1})\mathbb{E}(f_{1}^{p})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}(f^{p}).

Moreover,

III=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0\}}((g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}-g_{1}^{p})];

we can introduce the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{2}>0\}}
for free, since the last factor vanishes when
g_{2}=0
.
Hence

E(g^{p})=(1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0))\mathbb{E}(f^{p})+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0\}}((g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}-g_{1}^{p})].

By symmetry, we can obtain a similar identity with the roles of
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
reversed, which only affect the very last term, as everything else is symmetric already. Taking the average of both these expressions, we obtain the new identity

E(g^{p})=(1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0))\mathbb{E}(f^{p})+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0\}}((g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}-\frac{g_{1}^{p}+g_{2}^{p}}{2})].

Next, by concavity of the function
t\mapsto t^{p}
(recalling that
p\in(0,1]
), we observe that

(g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}=2^{p}\Big(\frac{g_{1}+g_{2}}{2}\Big)^{p}\geq 2^{p}\frac{g_{1}^{p}+g_{2}^{p}}{2}.

Hence, denoting
c_{p}:=\frac{1}{2}(2^{p}-1)
, we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[&\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0\}}((g_{1}+g_{2})^{p}-\frac{g_{1}^{p}+g_{2}^{p}}{2})]\\
&\geq\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0,g_{2}>0\}}c_{p}(g_{1}^{p}+g_{2}^{p})]=c_{p}(\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{2}>0\}}g_{1}^{p}]+\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\{g_{1}>0\}}g_{2}^{p}])\\
&=2c_{p}\mathbb{P}(g_{2}>0)\mathbb{E}(g_{1}^{p})=2c_{p}\mathbb{P}(\delta_{2}>0)\mathbb{P}(f_{2}>0)\mathbb{E}(\delta_{1})\mathbb{E}(f_{1}^{p})\\
&=\frac{c_{p}}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)\mathbb{E}(f^{p}).\end{split}

Substituting back, we obtain the first claim, noting that

-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{c_{p}}{2}=-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{2^{p}-1}{4}=-\frac{2-2^{p}}{4}.

Combining the first claim for
\mathbb{E}(g^{p})
, which we just proved, with the identity for
\mathbb{P}(g>0)
from (
A.4
), it follows that

\begin{split}\frac{\mathbb{E}(g^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(g>0)}&\geq\frac{(1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)+\frac{c_{p}}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0))\mathbb{E}(f^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(f>0)(1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0))}\\
&=\frac{\mathbb{E}(f^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(f>0)}\Big(1+\frac{\frac{c_{p}}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)}{1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)}\Big),\end{split}

where

\frac{\frac{c_{p}}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)}{1-\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{P}(f>0)}\geq\frac{\frac{c_{p}}{2}\mathbb{P}(f>0)}{1-\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{2c_{p}}{3}\mathbb{P}(f>0)=\frac{2^{p}-1}{3}\mathbb{P}(f>0).

Substituting back, this gives the second claim of the lemma and completes the proof.
∎

Lemma
A.8
already shows that the number
\sigma(\mathscr{R})^{-\frac{1}{p}}\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}}\|_{p}
increases strictly when replacing a quilt
\mathscr{R}
by the new quilt
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}
produced by Algorithm
A.5
. To ensure we can make this number as large as we like, we need to have some quantitative control of this increase. The following lemma addresses the behaviour of the factor
\sigma(\mathscr{R})
.

Lemma A.9
.

Consider the sequence

\sigma_{0}:=1,\qquad\sigma_{n}:=\sigma_{n-1}(1-\tfrac{1}{4}\sigma_{n-1}),\quad n\geq 1.

Then
\sigma_{n}\sim\frac{1}{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

If
\sigma_{n-1}\in(0,1]
, the recursion shows that
\sigma_{n}\in(0,1]
as well; hence this holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.Then
\sigma_{n}<\sigma_{n-1}
, so the sequence has a limit, say
L\in[0,1]
, which satisfies
L=L(1-\frac{1}{4}L)
, and hence
L=0
. We also note that

\sigma_{n}\geq\sigma_{n-1}(1-\tfrac{1}{4})=\tfrac{3}{4}\sigma_{n-1}.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
n_{k}
be the first (i.e., smallest) index such that
\sigma_{n_{k}}<e^{-k}
, thus

\sigma_{n_{k}}\geq\frac{3}{4}\sigma_{n_{k}-1}\geq\frac{3}{4}e^{-k}>e^{-k-1},

so
n_{k+1}>n_{k}
. Since
e^{-0}=1=\sigma_{0}>\sigma_{1}
, we also note that
n_{0}=1
.

For
x\in[0,1]
, we note that
1-\frac{1}{4}x\geq e^{-x}
. Indeed, this holds at the endpoints by direct inspection, and hence in between by the convexity of
e^{-x}
. Thus

\sigma_{n_{k}+j}=\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(1-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{n_{k}+i})\sigma_{n_{k}}\geq(1-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{n_{k}})^{j}\sigma_{n_{k}}\geq e^{-\sigma_{n_{k}}j}\sigma_{n_{k}}\geq e^{-e^{-k}j}\frac{3}{4}e^{-k}.

Now, if
e^{-e^{-k}j}\frac{3}{4}\geq e^{-1}
, i.e.,
e^{-k}j+\log\frac{4}{3}\leq 1
, i.e.,
j\leq\log\frac{3e}{4}\cdot e^{k}
, then
\sigma_{n_{k}+j}\geq e^{-1-k}
, and thus
n_{k+1}>n_{k}+j
for each such
j
.

Choosing
j=\lfloor\log\frac{3e}{4}\cdot e^{k}\rfloor
, we find that

n_{k+1}\geq n_{k}+\lfloor\log\frac{3e}{4}\cdot e^{k}\rfloor+1>n_{k}+\log\frac{3e}{4}\cdot e^{k}\geq\frac{1}{e}\log\frac{3e}{4}\cdot e^{k+1},

and hence

n_{k}\geq c\cdot e^{k}.

Next, we claim that if
j\geq 4e^{k+1}
, then
n_{k+1}\leq n_{k}+j
. We may assume that
n_{k+1}\geq n_{k}+(j-1)
, for otherwise the claim is clear. Under this assumption, it follows that
\sigma_{n_{k}+i}\geq\sigma_{n_{k}+(j-1)}\geq e^{-k-1}
for all
i\leq j-1
. Using also
1-x\leq e^{-x}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we obtain

\sigma_{n_{k}+j}=\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}(1-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{n_{k}+i})\sigma_{n_{k}}\leq(1-\frac{1}{4}e^{-k-1})^{j}\sigma_{n_{k}}<e^{-\frac{1}{4}e^{-k-1}j}e^{-k}.

If
j\geq 4e^{k+1}
, it follows that
\sigma_{n_{k}+j}<e^{-1-k}
, and hence
n_{k+1}\leq n_{k}+j
, as we wanted. Taking
j=\lceil 4e^{k+1}\rceil
, we obtain

n_{k+1}\leq n_{k}+\lceil 4e^{k+1}\rceil\leq n_{k}+4e^{k+1}+1.

By iteration, it follows that

n_{k}\leq n_{0}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(4e\cdot e^{j}+1)=1+4e\frac{e^{k}-1}{e-1}+k\leq C\cdot e^{k}.

We have hence proved that

ce^{k}\leq n_{k}\leq Ce^{k}.

Recalling that
n_{0}=1
and
n_{k}<n_{k+1}
, it follows that every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
satisfies
n_{k}\leq n<n_{k+1}
for a unique
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then

\sigma_{n}\leq\sigma_{n_{k}}<e^{-k}=e\cdot e^{-k-1}\leq eC/n_{k+1}<eC/n

and

\sigma_{n}\geq\sigma_{n_{k+1}-1}\geq e^{-1-k}\geq e^{-1}c/n_{k}\geq e^{-1}c/n.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.10
.

Consider the sequence of quilts
(\mathscr{R}_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
, where
\mathscr{R}_{0}:=\{[0,1)^{2}\}
and, given
\mathscr{R}_{n}
, the next quilt
\mathscr{R}_{n+1}=(\mathscr{R}_{n})^{\prime}
is constructed by Algorithm
A.5
with
\mathscr{R}_{n}
as input. Let
h_{n}:=\chi_{\mathscr{R}_{n}}
. Then for all
p\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\frac{\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(h_{n}>0)}\geq n^{\gamma}

for some
\gamma=\gamma_{p}>0
.

Proof.

Since
h_{n}
is positive integer-valued, we have
h_{n}^{q}\geq h_{n}^{p}
whenever
q\geq p>0
. Hence it is enough to consider
p\in(0,1]
, and indeed this is the main difficulty.

By Lemma
A.4
, the values
\mathbb{P}(h_{n}>0)
coincide with the sequence
\sigma_{n}
of Lemma
A.9
. Thus, by Lemma
A.8
, we have

\frac{\mathbb{E}(h_{n+1}^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(h_{n+1}>0)}\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(h_{n}>0)}(1+a_{p}\sigma_{n}),\qquad a_{p}:=\frac{2^{p}-1}{3}\in(0,\tfrac{1}{3}].

Hence, by iteration,

\frac{\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(h_{n}>0)}\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}(h_{0}^{p})}{\mathbb{P}(h_{0}>0)}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1+a_{p}\sigma_{j})\geq 1\times\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}e^{b_{p}\sigma_{j}}=\exp\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}b_{p}\sigma_{j}\Big),

for
b_{p}=a_{p}\log 2>0
, noting that
1+x\geq e^{x\log 2}
for all
x\in[0,1]
by equality at the endpoints and convexity. By Lemma
A.9
, we find that

\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\sigma_{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\sigma_{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\frac{c}{j}\geq c\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{j}^{j+1}\frac{dt}{t}=c\log n.

Hence

\exp\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}b_{p}\sigma_{j}\Big)\geq\exp(b_{p}c\log n)=n^{b_{p}c}=:n^{\gamma_{p}}.

Substituting back, this completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

For the sequence of quilts
\mathscr{R}_{n}
as in Lemma
A.10
, we find that

\sigma(\mathscr{R}_{n})^{-1}\|\chi_{\mathscr{R}_{n}}\|_{p}^{p}=\mathbb{P}(h_{n}>0)^{-1}\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{p})\geq n^{\gamma_{p}}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\infty.

Thus, the quantity on the left can be made as large as desired by choosing a large enough
n
.
∎

A.2.
A version in higher dimensions

The following is a simple extension of the classical Carleson counterexample
[
23
]
that we gave in Lemma
A.2
following the formulation of
[
143
, Lemma 7.2]
. We state it separately, since it is already enough for some of our purposes (e.g., the proof of Theorem
9.18
) and does not require the more complicated Proposition
A.3
.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
k\geq 2
. For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
there exists a finite collection of
pairwise distinct dyadic rectangles
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscr{I}}

in
[0,1)^{\vec{n}}
such that

(i)

\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I}}|R_{i}|=1
,

(ii)

|\bigcup_{i\in\mathscr{I}}R_{i}|<\varepsilon
, and

(iii)

\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I},\,R_{i}\subset P}|R_{i}|\leq|P|

for every dyadic rectangle
P\subset[0,1)^{\vec{n}}
.

Note that the condition that the
R_{i}
are pairwise distinct is redundant, since it is automatic from (
iii
): If
R_{i}=R_{j}=R
for some
i\neq j
, then (
iii
) is immediately contradicted by choosing
P=R
.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.2
,
we find that, for every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
there exists a finite collection of
pairwise distinct dyadic rectangles
\{I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\}_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}

in
[0,1)^{2}
such that

\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\big|I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\big|=1,\ \Big|\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\Big|<\varepsilon,

and
for every dyadic rectangle
J^{(1)}\times J^{(2)}\subset[0,1)^{2}
,

\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K},\,I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\subset J^{(1)}\times J^{(2)}}\big|I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\big|\leq\big|J^{(1)}\times J^{(2)}\big|.

For every
\kappa\in\mathscr{K}
, let
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscr{I}_{\kappa}}
be a rearrangement of

\displaystyle\Big\{\Big(\bigotimes_{\nu=1,2}I_{\kappa}^{(\nu)}\times Q^{(\nu)}\Big)\times[0,1)^{n_{3}+\cdots+n_{k}}:\Big.

\displaystyle\qquad\Big.Q^{(\nu)}\subset[0,1)^{n_{\nu}-1}\text{ is a cube with edge-length }|I_{\kappa}^{(\nu)}|,\ \nu=1,2\Big\}.

Of course, we can make the index sets
\mathscr{I}_{\kappa}
completely pairwise distinct.
Let
\mathscr{I}:=\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\mathscr{I}_{\kappa}
.
Then it is easy to verify
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscr{I}}
is the desired sequence.
This finishes the proof of Lemma
A.11
.
∎

Here is a more general version based on Proposition
A.3
:

Lemma A.12
.

Let
k\geq 2
and
p\in(0,\infty]
. For every
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
there exists a finite collection of
pairwise distinct dyadic rectangles
\{R_{i}\}_{i\in\mathscr{I}}

in
[0,1)^{\vec{n}}
with the properties listed in Lemma
A.11
and, in addition,

\Big|\bigcup_{i\in\mathscr{I}}R_{i}\Big|^{-\frac{1}{p}}\Big\|\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I}}\mathbf{1}_{R_{i}}\Big\|_{p}>\frac{1}{\varepsilon}.

(A.5)

Proof.

We use the same construction of the rectangles
R_{i}
, starting with a collection dyadic rectangles
\mathscr{R}=:\{I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\}_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}
of
[0,1)^{2}
given by Proposition
A.3
. Then

\begin{split}\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I}}\mathbf{1}_{R_{i}}(x)&=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q^{(\nu)}\subset[0,1)^{n_{\nu}-1}\\
\ell(Q^{(\nu)})=\ell(I^{(\nu)}_{\kappa})\\
\nu=1,2\end{subarray}}\mathbf{1}_{(I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times Q^{(1)})\times(I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\times Q^{(2)})\times[0,1)^{n_{3}+\ldots+n_{k}}}(x)\\
&=\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\mathbf{1}_{(I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times[0,1)^{n_{1}-1})\times(I_{\kappa}^{(2)}\times[0,1)^{n_{2}-1})\times[0,1)^{n_{3}+\ldots+n_{k}}}(x)\\
&=\mathbf{1}_{[0,1)^{\vec{n}}}(x)\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\mathbf{1}_{I_{\kappa}^{(1)}\times I_{\kappa}^{(2)}}(x_{1,1},x_{2,1}).\end{split}

From this is is immediate that

\Big\|\sum_{i\in\mathscr{I}}\mathbf{1}_{R_{i}}\Big\|_{L^{p}([0,1)^{\vec{n}})}=\Big\|\sum_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}\mathbf{1}_{I^{(1)}_{\kappa}\times I^{(2)}_{\kappa}}\Big\|_{L^{p}([0,1)^{2})}

and that

\Big|\bigcup_{i\in\mathscr{I}}R_{i}\Big|_{\vec{n}}=\Big|\bigcup_{\kappa\in\mathscr{K}}I^{(1)}_{\kappa}\times I^{(2)}_{\kappa}\Big|_{2},

where we exceptionally indicate the dimension of the Lebesgue measure on each side for clarity. Since analogue of the right-hand side of (
A.5
) can be made arbitrarily large for the original collection
\mathscr{R}
on
[0,1)^{2}
by Proposition
A.3
, the last two identities show that it will become arbitrarily large also for the new collection
\{R_{i}:i\in\mathscr{I}\}
on
[0,1)^{\vec{n}}
.
∎
